Next, Bhumadevi looked at Vasishtha Muni and said, "O Sage, is this befitting of you? Will your sorrow vanish if you roll down from a mountain onto the ground? Perform penance towards Parameshwara. Then your sorrow will not only disappear, but you will also attain liberation," said Bhumadevi. At that time, Vasishtha Muni reached this Kayaranya Kshetram, also known as Thirukalathi, which is superior to all holy places. He controlled his mind, subdued his five senses, abstained from food, and meditating on Shiva, who wears the Kondrai garland, in the lotus of his heart, he performed severe penance for a long time, disregarding the miseries caused by fire, sun, rain, cold, snow, wind, and wild animals, and without hunger, thirst, or sleep. To bestow grace upon Vasishtha Muni, who was performing such penance, Paramashiva appeared before him in the form of a five-headed serpent, as a Parasiva Lingam, facing west. At that time, Vasishtha worshipped the Lord with a melting heart. Pleased by this, the Lord asked, "What boon do you desire?" Vasishtha requested, "May you always reside here in this form, in all ages and yugas, and bestow your grace." Not only that, but he also requested that the Lord impart divine knowledge to him. Therefore, the Lord resided here and, in the form of Dakshinamurthy under a Kallala tree, imparted divine knowledge to Vasishtha Muni. Later, in this very place, he remained as the Nagalingam that appeared to Vasishtha. At that time, Uma Devi, unable to bear the separation from Parameshwara, came from Mount Kailash to this Thirukalathi Sthalam, which is Kayaranya Kshetram, and resided there with the sacred name Gnanappoongodhai. Seeing this, Lord Ganesha, Lord Muruga, Lord Virabhadra, Lord Bhairava, and the Rudras, Shivaganas, and all others came to this Kayaranya Kshetram, where Shiva and Uma Devi resided, and lived there, having received the grace of Parameshwara. Mount Kailash, the silver mountain, seeing itself separated from Shiva's family, like children separated from their parents, became distressed and, thinking of reaching Kayaranya Kshetram with Mount Meru, both Kailash and Mount Meru came here. Therefore, this Kshetram became known as Dakshina Kailasam. Now, the history of Agastya's worship. Once, when the divine marriage of Lord Shiva and Parvati Devi took place, the northern side tilted down and the southern side rose up. To balance the southern side, the Lord sent Agastya. Agastya, along with his wife Lopamudra Devi, set off. He crossed the Kedara mountain, had darshan of Kasi surrounded by the Ganga river, and visited Virupaksham, Daksharamapuram, Nandigiri, and all other places. As he came southwards, he saw this Thirukalathi, the Southern Kailash. He worshipped it. Then, wishing for a sacred river here, he performed severe penance. Lord Shiva, pleased by his penance, commanded the Ganga to flow directly here as the Swarnamukhi, or Ponmugali river. At that time, Agastya Peruman explained the greatness of this Ponmugali river, the Swarnamukhi, to other sages. This river is the abundant grace of Lord Shiva. Even sinners who have committed cow slaughter, feticide, infanticide, and other acts of killing, if they bathe in this river, will become devas. Birds like swans and Garudas, who bathe in this river, will attain high positions as vehicles for the devas. The fish residing in it will shine as stars in the sky. Thus, this Ponmugari, or Swarnamukhi river, which shines in Sri Kalahasti, the Kayaranya Kshetram, is superior to all rivers. Even Agastya Muni, who is equal to Shiva, the transcendent one, performed penance for a long time and obtained this Ponmugari river as the fruit of his penance. This Puranam shows us that it possesses immeasurable greatness. In this Sthalam, Brahmadeva and Saraswati Devi performed penance and, by the grace of Lord Shiva, had a son named Ribhu. The name of this Sthalam is Sri Kalahasti, isn't it? Here, "Sri" refers to the spider, "Kala" refers to the snake named Kalan, and "Hasti" refers to the elephant. Because the spider (Sri), the snake named Kalan, and the elephant (Hasti) worshipped here and attained blessings, it came to be known as Sri Kalahasti. Now, let's contemplate this historical narrative. Brahmadeva was engaged in the work of creation. Madhava Uran, who appeared from the eye of Vishwakarma, harbored enmity towards Brahmadeva and also began to create worlds. He was the son of Vishwakarma, the divine architect, wasn't he? Therefore, he too began to create, competing with Brahmadeva. Knowing this, Brahma became angry with him and cursed him, saying, "Since you created without respecting me, you shall be born as a spider that creates small webs." Hearing this, Madhava Uran was saddened and bowed down to Brahmadeva, praising him. Seeing this, Brahmadeva, with a softened heart, said, "Do not grieve. Go to that Bilva forest, the Kayaranya Kshetram, where Lord Shiva is enshrined. Reside there as a spider and worship Lord Shiva with devotion." So, he, as a spider, worshipped the Nagalingam on the banks of the Ponmugari river in this Kshetram, the Southern Kailash. How does a spider build its web? Similarly, this spider, born here, was the son of Vishwakarma, wasn't he? Therefore, this spider built a temple with its own threads from its mouth. It beautifully constructed the temple components like the outer walls, gopurams, mandapams, palaces, and the sanctum sanctorum with its threads. Without resting even for a moment, it performed this sacred service for a long time and worshipped the Lord. At that time, Lord Shiva performed a divine play. Lord Shiva made the lamp burning in his sanctum burn brightly. Due to this, the temple built by the spider with its threads was destroyed by fire in an instant. Seeing this, the spider was greatly saddened. Thinking, "This temple, which I built with great love for the Lord, has been burnt by fire," it tried to jump into the fire itself to end its life. At that moment, Lord Shiva, the embodiment of grace, appeared there. The spider asked for a boon. The spider requested to always remain in the shadow of the Lord's feet. Lord Shiva granted that very blessing. This is the history of Sri, the spider, worshipping the Lord here. Now, let's look at the history of Kalan, the snake, and Hasti, the elephant, worshipping the Lord. Lord Shiva resides on Mount Kailash. He wears Nagas (serpents) like Adiseshan as ornaments all over his body. Among the Naga ornaments worn by the Lord, there was a snake named Kalan. One day, Lord Shiva noticed that Kalan, the snake, was not on his body. Kalan, the Naga, had gone to his beloved due to excessive lust. Therefore, Lord Shiva summoned Kalan, the snake, and said, "You were disturbed by the disease of lust. Therefore, go to Kayaranya Kshetram and worship me in the form of a snake. At that time, an elephant will also come there to worship. You will try to kill it, and both of you will die and attain liberation." Lord Shiva thus gave both a curse and a blessing. Accordingly, Kalan, the snake, reached the Kayaranya Kshetram, which is now Thirukalahasti, and worshipped the Lord. Now, let's look at the history of the elephant's previous birth. One of Lord Shiva's Shivaganas on Mount Kailash was named Athi. One day, Athi, this Shivagana, came to have darshan of the Lord and the Goddess. The gatekeepers of Kailash said that the Lord and Goddess were alone, and therefore, he could not have darshan at that moment. Athi, the Shivagana, disregarded the words of the gatekeepers and quickly went inside. As he quickly went inside, Parvati Devi looked at that Gana and cursed him, saying, "Athi, you have come in, disregarding the orders of the gatekeepers. Therefore, you shall be born as an elephant." At that time, Athi, the Gana, bowed down and praised the Lord. The Lord, with a softened heart, said, "Go to Kayaranya Kshetram in the form of an elephant and worship the Shiva Lingam. At that time, a snake will also come to worship the Shiva Lingam. Then enmity will arise between you and the snake, and both of you will die and attain liberation," said Lord Shiva. Accordingly, Athi, the Shivagana, was born as an elephant named Athi in Kayaranya Kshetram, the Southern Kailash. Thus, Kalan, the snake, and Athi, the elephant, born in this way, worshipped the Lord in Kayaranya Kshetram, which is now Thirukalathi. Kalan, the snake, worshipped the Shiva Lingam with its Naga gems. Athi, the elephant, after the snake had worshipped, would come and push away all the Naga gems on the Lord's head and worship with water, leaves, and flowers. Then, the next day, the snake would come and remove all the Bilva leaves, flowers, etc., that the elephant had used for worship, and worship with its Naga gems. Thus, the elephant and the snake worshipped alternately. The snake did not like the worship with leaves. The elephant did not like the fact that it had already been worshipped with gems. Therefore, enmity arose between the two. The snake decided, "I will see who removes all my gems and worships with these leaves." Thus, it vowed, "I will bite with my poisonous fangs the one who removes the gems I worshipped with." As usual, on that day, after worshipping with Naga gems, the snake hid a little distance away and watched. At that time, the elephant came and, as usual, pushed down the Naga gems on Lord Shiva's head, bathed Lord Shiva with water, and worshipped with Bilva leaves, flowers, and other things. The snake saw this. The next day, the snake decided to take revenge on this elephant and kill it. It hid among the leaves and flowers that the elephant had used for worship. The Naga hid itself on the Lord's body. The next day, as usual, when the elephant brought water from the Ponmugali river and performed abhishekam on the Shiva Lingam, the snake, which was hidden among the leaves and flowers, hissed in anger and entered the elephant's trunk. The snake, having entered, burrowed into the elephant's forehead with its poisonous fangs. The mad elephant trumpeted like the roaring clouds of dissolution and ran, unable to bear the pain, crashing everywhere. It struck its trunk on the ground, and with pain, it hit the mountains, uprooted and broke trees. As the torment of the poisonous snake did not subside, it thought, "The solution is to kill our enemy and then die ourselves." Filled with anger, it quickly rushed back, curled its trunk, and charged with such speed that the mountain and earth trembled. Due to this, the elephant's head split open and it fell down. The snake also was crushed inside. At that time, Lord Shiva, with Uma, appeared on Rishabharudha (on the bull) and, bestowing his Sarupya (similarity of form) and Sarupya Mukti (liberation of similar form), took the snake and the elephant with him. Thus, because Sri, the spider, Kalan, the snake, and Athi, the elephant, worshipped in this Southern Kailash, it came to be known by the sacred name Sri Kalahasti. Not only that, but Nakkirar Deva Nayanar worshipped Sri Kalahasti here and sang about it. Kannappa Nayanar attained liberation here. These histories are also extensively described in the Thirukalathi Puranam. Thus, Thirukalathi, or Sri Kalahasti, is a holy place of immeasurable greatness. The history of Kannappa Nayanar's previous birth is also mentioned in this Sri Kalahasti Puranam. We will see that in the appropriate place in the Kannappa Nayanar Puranam in Periya Puranam. As a benefit of reading this Puranam, it is stated that those who joyfully read or listen to this Sri Kalahasti Puranam, the Southern Kailash, and those who explain its meaning, will, while living on this earth, receive wealth such as land and houses, and all other riches, live excellently on this earth, and then join Kailash, where the Lord resides. With this, we conclude the Thirukalathi Puranam and will now continue with the Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. May the whole world prosper, may it rain three times a month, may the king rule justly, may those who do good deeds flourish, may the devotees of the Lord, who is half-female, prosper greatly on earth, and may the great ascetics who follow the Agamic scriptures of the Lord live long. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 128, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman, who narrates the history of each Nayanar, describes their name, village, lineage, clan, country, and profession. Similarly, Sekkizhar Swamigal, beginning the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar here, states that Kannappa Nayanar's country is Potthappi Nadu. When describing the sacred land where Kannappa Nayanar was born, he refers to Potthappi Nadu as Thirukalathi Kannappar Thirunadu. Thirukalathi and Kannappa Nayanar are inseparable. Therefore, he refers to him as Thirukalathi Kannappar. Thus, Kannappa Nayanar incarnated in a country called Potthappi Nadu. This is located about 40 to 50 nazhigai (a measure of distance) north of the Kalathi mountain range. This Potthappi Nadu belongs to the Kurinji region, which consists of mountains and mountainous areas. This Potthappi Nadu is the sacred land of Kannappa Nayanar. The name given to Kannappa Nayanar by his parents was Thinnan. However, Kannappa is the name given by God. Therefore, all the Acharyas praised the Nayanar by this name. This Potthappi Nadu is a country with good resources praised by scholars. There are two types of scholars: those blessed by Saraswati, the goddess of arts, and those who have the knowledge of Gnana embedded in their tongues. Thus, this Potthappi Nadu is a country praised and worshipped by these two great types of scholars, the scholars of arts and the scholars of wisdom. This is because it is the sacred land where Kannappa Nayanar, who exemplified supreme devotion, incarnated. Therefore, it is a country praised by both scholars of arts and scholars of wisdom. It is a mountainous country. Therefore, it is a country rich in resources. It is a country full of good resources because it possesses Kalathi, a place of liberation, and the glory of having Kannappa Nayanar incarnate there. Sekkizhar Peruman extensively describes the wealth of this country, the wealth of the city of Uduppur located here, and the wealth and characteristics of its people. The most ancient town in this Potthappi Nadu is Uduppur. It is our tradition to call the country where great people are born as Thirunadu and the village where great people incarnate as Thirupathi. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal mentions here that the Thirunadu where Kannappa Nayanar incarnated is Potthappi Nadu, and the Thirupathi where he incarnated is Uduppur Thirupathi. This Potthappi Nadu is a mountainous region. Therefore, it is surrounded by mountains, and these mountains serve as a natural protection, a natural rampart, for this country, Potthappi Nadu. It is a country surrounded by mountains. Therefore, it is a prosperous country due to the waterfalls originating from the mountains and the rivers formed by them. It is a dense forest area, a country where hunters live. The Thirupathi in this country is Uduppur Thirupathi. This town of Uduppur is not found now. However, there is a town called Udukkur in the same region in Andhra Pradesh now. It is near Rajampet. Therefore, there is a high probability that the name Uduppur has now transformed into Udukkur. Since the mountains and forests are dense above this Udukkur, Sekkizhar Swamigal's poem,

"Since there is an abundance of mountain resources, there is sufficient evidence to conclude that Udukkur is indeed the town of Uduppur." Furthermore, Kannappa Nayanar, born in this town, travels a long distance while hunting and reaches Kalathi, where the Ponmugali river is located. Even now, there is a distance of 40 miles between Uduppur and Sri Kalathi. Sekkizhar Swamigal states in this Thirupathi of Uduppur that there are fences around Uduppur. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that these fences are made by arranging elephant tusks in a row. It is situated in the mountain range. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that fences made of elephant tusks are built around Uduppur like a rampart to prevent fierce animals like tigers from entering the village and harming the domestic animals like sheep, cattle, and deer raised by the people. Naturally, mountains surround this country as a fortress. In addition, there are fences made of elephant tusks. The naturally formed mountains serve as fortifications protecting this country from external enemies. Generally, if a country has mountains as its natural fortifications, it is considered a country excellent in protection. Here too, mountains naturally surround and protect this country from external enemies. Similarly, people protect the city of Uduppur by artificially building fences with elephant tusks. This fence is used to protect these people from the animals within the country. Internal enemy means the enemy coming from wild animals. Thus, this country and city have mountains as natural fences to protect against external enemies and artificial fences made of elephant tusks to protect against internal enemies, the wild animals. Why does Sekkizhar Swamigal describe all this? It is because Kannappa Nayanar, the hero of this Puranam, who incarnated in this place, performed a lot of penance in his previous births to prevent both external and internal enemies from approaching him, and now he is incarnating here. Therefore, as a symbol and example of how external and internal enemies do not approach Kannappa Nayanar due to the penance he performed in his previous births, Sekkizhar Swamigal subtly implies that this country and city have built fences to protect against external and internal enemies. The inhabitants of this Uduppur Thirupathi are the Kuravas, the mountain dwellers. Sekkizhar Swamigal continues to describe their way of life and the wealth of this country. The Kurava people living in this region raise hunting dogs in their homes. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that the ears of these hunting dogs are bent. Bent and drooping ears are one of the characteristics of excellent breed hunting dogs. We can see how these hunting dogs are used by hunters in the following verses. Thus, the people raise hunting dogs. The place where they live is surrounded by wood apple trees. Naturally, wood apple trees are abundant there. In addition, people also grow wood apple trees near their homes. Therefore, the village appears as a residential area surrounded by wood apple trees. The wood apple tree is also called Kaviththa Maram, and the wood apple fruit is called Kaviththam Pazham.

The properties and benefits of the wood apple tree and the wood apple fruit are described in a book called Padartha Guna Chintamani. Padartha Guna Chintamani is a Tamil Siddha medical text. In this, the properties and benefits of each substance are described in poetic form. There is a verse about the wood apple tree: "Kasa swasam kathiththa varusi mudhal pesariya thaagam ivai perum kaan veesu thabiththalai sei piththam tharikkumo poovil kapiththa maram irukkum kaal." This is the verse. In this, it is stated that cough, asthma, anorexia, severe thirst, pitta, and kapha diseases will be cured by the benefits of the wood apple tree. Similarly, it is stated that respiratory problems, cough, kapha dominance, and pitta thirst will be relieved by the properties of the wood apple fruit. It is also stated that consuming wood apple fruit will bring comfort to the body and good appetite. Thus, those who wish to learn about the properties of each tree, the properties of fruits, their benefits, types of plants, creepers, and the properties and benefits of all substances can read the book Padartha Guna Chintamani. The residential areas of the hunters there are dense with such special wood apple trees. People raise "parvai mirugangal" (decoy animals) in their homes. Let's see what "parvai mirugangal" are. The profession of the Kuravas, the mountain dwellers, is hunting. Hunters who go hunting will kill animals. However, they also bring some animals, when they are young, from the forests and raise them in their homes. For example, they will bring a young deer from the forest and raise it at home. These hunters will tame and raise this deer. Then, when they go hunting, they will take this deer, which they raised at home, with them. This deer, raised at home, is under their control and obeys their commands, isn't it? So, when they hunt by taking this deer, they will release it in the forest and hunt other deer that come to see this deer. To assist in hunting in this manner, they will raise various types of animals as "parvai mirugangal" in their homes. Thus, they catch animals from the forests, raise them at home, and then, with their help, hunt other animals of the same species. The proverb "Maan kaatti maan pidippathu pola" (like catching a deer by showing another deer) originated from this. Even God bestows grace in the form appropriate to each being. This is a concept from Gnana Sastras (scriptures of wisdom). For example, to redeem us, who live in human bodies, God will guide us from within a Guru who has also taken a human body, just like us. How is this? Just as hunters tame deer and use them to attract other wild deer, this act of God's redemption is compared and explained in Gnana Sastra texts. "Parvai ena maakkalai mun patti pidiththarkaam porvaiyena kaanaar puvi" is from Thiruarutpayan. Just as wild animals are caught using trained animals, God's grace, through a Gnanaachariyan (spiritual teacher), redeems mature souls. When God's grace is hidden within the teacher, to the immature, that Gnanaachariyan appears like an ordinary human. To mature souls, he appears as a Gnanaachariyan, and the grace within him redeems them, i.e., the mature souls. These methods by which grace, using the Gnanaachariyan as its medium, redeems mature souls are called Dikshas (initiations). Dikshas are of seven types: Nayana Diksha, Sparsa Diksha, Manasa Diksha, Vasaka Diksha, Sastra Diksha, Yoga Diksha, and Avuththiri Diksha. Nayana Diksha is when the Acharya, standing as Shiva, looks at the disciple from head to toe with a gaze of grace and destroys the disciple's Anava Mala (ego). Sparsa Diksha is when the Acharya, considering his hand as the sacred hand of Lord Shiva, places it on the disciple's head and destroys his Maya Mala (illusion). Now let's see what Manasa Diksha is. Manasa Diksha is when the Acharya, through the path of Yoga, enters the disciple's mind, removes the disciple's self-awareness, and creates Shiva-awareness. Next, Sastra Diksha is the concise explanation of the truths found in the scriptures revealed by God, such as the Vedas and Agamas, to the disciple. Next, Vasaka Diksha is the initiation of the Panchakshara (the five-letter mantra of Shiva) and 11 other mantras with their respective matras (vowel durations). This is called Vasaka Diksha. Next, Yoga Diksha is when the Acharya enters the disciple's heart and unites the soul with Shiva. Next, Avuththiri Diksha is an initiation performed with Homa (fire ritual). Avuththiri means related to Homa. Generally, Diksha is divided into "Dee" and "Ksha," where "Dee" means giving knowledge, and "Ksha" means destroying bondage. "Dee yathe thath shivathvam hi kshiyathe paapa pancharam kshayathana visheshathvaath diksha seth abhidheeyathe" is a Sloka from Suprabodha Agama. This describes the aforementioned definition of Diksha. Thus, God, from within the Gnanaachariyan, who also has a human body, takes control of the mature souls within human bodies. This act is compared in Gnana Sastras to hunters using decoy animals to tame wild animals. Thus, every detail described by Sekkizhar Peruman in Periya Puranam, especially in the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar, contains profound spiritual meanings. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 129, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman describes the lifestyle of the Kuravas, the mountain-dwelling hunters. The children in the hunter families play with tiger cubs and elephant calves. The girls play with female deer. The hair of the hunter boys is short, curly, and matted, without oil or combing, and split in places. Hunter boys possess the bravery characteristic of men, don't they? Therefore, they play with elephant calves and tiger cubs. Hunter girls, on the other hand, play with deer, which possess the natural qualities of love and affection inherent in women. Hunter girls feed grass to the deer and praise them. They interact with them affectionately. The deer also interact and play with the girls very affectionately. Animals with cloven hooves, like deer and cattle, are naturally herd animals. They are animals that are raised at home by people and quickly become familiar and affectionate with them. Therefore, modern people call them domestic animals. Thus, hunter boys and hunter girls grow up interacting with different animals according to their respective aptitudes from a young age. Hunter boys grow up with qualities like bravery, masculinity, pride, and strength. Therefore, they play with tiger cubs and elephant calves. Hunter girls, on the other hand, grow up as embodiments of love and joy. Therefore, they play affectionately with female deer. What we need to understand from this is that boys and girls should grow up and be raised with education and habits appropriate to their respective natures. However, in modern times, the practice has emerged that men and women are of the same nature, and therefore, men and women should be provided with the same type of education, the same type of training, and the same type of profession. Both should study together in the same school and receive training together. But in ancient times, our educational tradition was contrary to this, which we realize when we read texts like Periya Puranam. Therefore, Sivakavimani writes that modern people should read and reflect on these ideas and reform themselves. Now, the verses describe the sounds emanating from these hunter settlements. Hunters are people with conquering weapons, fearlessness, and harsh words. Therefore, their gatherings are always filled with the shouts of "Kill! Throw! Stab!" The common words used by most people in a community reveal their way of life. Therefore, hunters, who live with hard hearts and a harsh hunting profession, also have harsh words like "Kill! Throw! Stab!" heard there. Now, the sound of the Udukkai (a small drum) is heard there. The sound from the Kombu (a horn instrument) arises. The sound from small drums is also heard. In addition, this is a mountainous region. Therefore, the sound of waterfalls descending from the mountains is also heard loudly. The waterfalls hit the rocks below very fast. They also flow in winding paths,

and rise. Thus, the waterfall creates sounds in various ways. It is like music there. These hunters also have another profession. That is, intercepting travelers on forest paths, robbing their belongings, and living off the stolen goods is also a profession of the hunters. In addition, stealing herds of cattle is also their profession. Thus, light emanates from the herds of cattle stolen by these hunters. This is a mountainous region. Therefore, the trumpeting sound of elephant herds roaming in the mountains is always heard. It is a very green mountainous region. Therefore, clouds are always colliding with each other, creating thunder. These dark-skinned hunters, these fierce warriors, never had fear. Similarly, they never had compassion. Those who follow the path of Dharma need both fear and compassion. One must adhere to the rules prescribed in the Dharma Sastras. One must avoid what is forbidden in the Dharma Sastras and live without doing them. To follow the Dharma Sastras, people need both fear and compassion. People should have the fear that if they do not live according to the Dharma Sastras, they will incur blame and sin. When living according to the Dharma Sastras, one should have compassion, such as helping other beings. Only those who have both fear and compassion can uphold and follow the Dharmas like non-violence (ahimsa). But these hunters have no fear or compassion whatsoever. Therefore, we understand that these are hunters who do not follow the path of righteousness. "Anjiyagilum anbu pattagilum nenjam vaazhi ninaiyi madanenjame" is the saying of Appar Swamigal. These hunters, who have neither fear nor compassion, eat rice mixed with meat. They carry spears and arrows tipped with poison as their tools. The leader of these mountain people is named Nagan. Nagan is one of the names given by the Kurinji people, the mountain dwellers. They worship snakes and other forest deities as their tribal gods and name their children after them. Therefore, the name Nagan here comes from that tradition. Thus, the leader of the mountain people is Nagan. This Nagan performed great penance in his previous births. That is why in this birth, he is going to receive the great blessing of having Kannappadeva Nayanar as his son. Penance means austerity and compassion. The penance one performs is the cause of all the good one receives. Here, Nagan performed penance in his previous births, and because of that penance, he is going to receive Kannappadeva Nayanar as his son, isn't that a great blessing? "Thakkar thagavilar enbadhu avaravar echchaththaal kaanappadum" is a Thirukkural. There is a chapter called "Naduvu Nilaimai" (Impartiality) in Thirukkural. What does this impartiality mean? It means the state of standing firm on righteousness, without deviation, in all three aspects: enmity, friendship, and neutrality. The Kural that comes in this is "Thakkar thagavilar enbadhu avaravar echchaththaal kaanappadum." That is, if someone has done righteousness, if someone has lived on the path of Dharma, how can that be known? If they have good children, good sons, then it means they have lived on the path of Dharma. This is the meaning of this Kural. Here, by having Kannappadeva Nayanar as his son, it is proven that Nagan performed penance in his previous births, according to this Kural. Even though Nagan performed penance in his previous births, in this birth, he is the leader of the hunter clan and is engaged in the cruel and murderous profession of hunters. This is due to his Prarabdha (destiny) related to his birth. Because Gnana Sastras state that Prarabdha comes to a person in terms of caste, lifespan, and enjoyment. Nagan, being born in the hunter clan, lives by pursuing the hunting profession due to his birth-related karma. Even for this, the penance he performed earlier is the reason. Even if one is born in a particular clan, there are great people who live without being influenced by that birth, like Thirunaalaipovaar Nayanar. But here, Nagan has performed penance only to the extent of having Kannappadeva Nayanar as his son. Therefore, having achieved that greatness, he continues the profession of hunters due to his birth. This Nagan is very skilled in archery. He is as strong as a male lion. His wife is Thaththai. Thaththai was born into a high-ranking ancient tribe of brave warriors. This Thaththai lives as the wife of Nagan, the leader of the hunter clan. Thaththai wears a tiger's tooth as a thali (marriage necklace) around her neck. Wearing a tiger's tooth strung as a thali is an ancient tradition of the mountain-dwelling Kuravas. Similarly, it is also a tradition to make children of hunters wear tiger's teeth as a protective charm. Furthermore, this Thaththai adorns her hair with peacock feathers and tender leaves. She wears a topknot. Having described Nagan and his wife Thaththai, Sekkizhar now describes the greatness of Kannappadeva Nayanar's incarnation. Just as Thiruvalluvar arranged the chapters in Thirukkural as "Ilvaazhkai" (Household Life), "Vaazhkkai Thunai Nalam" (Excellence of Spouse), and "Puthalvaraip Peruthal" (Obtaining Children), Sekkizhar Swamigal, having described Nagan and his wife Thaththai, now begins to sing about the event of Kannappa Nayanar's incarnation, the "Puthalvaraip Peruthal." Nagan and his wife Thaththai did not have children for a long time. They had also grown old. Therefore, everyone wondered how they would have a child now. The tribal deity of the hunter clan is Lord Murugan. Therefore, Nagan and Thaththai, out of their intense desire for a son, stood before their tribal deity, Murugavel, who wields the red spear, and prayed daily. Sekkizhar sings that Lord Murugavel is adorned with a garland of Kadamba flowers. This means that Lord Kumara is adorned with his characteristic Kadamba flower garland. "Sevvel" means a spear that emits a red glow. The spear is the Gnana Shakti (power of wisdom) of Lord Murugan. Sekkizhar Swamigal here praises Lord Murugan as "Sevvel Endhiya Murugavel" (Murugavel who wields the red spear). "Murugu" means youth. "Murugu" means eternal, imperishable youth. Therefore, "Murugavel" means the youthful, eternal, imperishable Vel (spear). "Vel" means one who is desired. "Vettal" means to do with desire. Therefore, "Vel" means one who is desired. That is why it is our tradition to call Lord Murugan as Murugavel and Kandavel. Lord Murugan is called Sevvel. He is also called Murugavel. Manmathan (the god of love) is called Kamavel. But Manmathan is dark-skinned. However, Lord Murugan is red-skinned. Therefore, Murugan is called Sevvel. He is also called Murugavel. Many names like Kumaran, Ilaiyon, Murugan, Kuzhagan are given to Murugan, referring to his youth and immense beauty. In Kandapuranam, there is a verse about Murugan's youth and beauty: "Aayiram kodi kaamar azhagellam thirandu onraagi meyina eninum sevvel vimalamaam saranam thannil thuuya nal ezhilukku aatraathu enridin inaiya thollon maa iru vadivirkku ellam uvamai yaar vagukka vallar." This is a verse from Kandapuranam. It means that even if a thousand crores of Manmathans were to gather and combine all their beauty, it would not be equal to the beauty of the sacred feet of Lord Kandavel. That deity, who is entirely beautiful, is the abode of beauty. He is the origin of beauty, is the meaning of this verse. Thus, Nagan and Thaththai stood before the shrine of Kandavel, who wields the red spear, and prayed with a melting heart every day, making vows and following their worship. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 130, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Nagan, the leader of the hunters, and his wife Thaththai, having been childless for a long time, pray and perform daily pujas before Lord Muruga, their tribal deity, according to their tradition. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how they prayed to Lord Muruga. Nagan and Thaththai offer roosters and peacocks as offerings to Lord Muruga. They offer them in pairs, male and female, as offerings to Lord Muruga. The rooster is the banner of Lord Muruga. "Kukkudathvajan" is one of the sacred names of Lord Muruga, isn't it? The peacock is the vehicle of Lord Muruga. Therefore, they offer both of these as pairs of birds and in large numbers as a collective offering to Lord Muruga. In Sakatheevu, King Prabhakaran had two sons, Suran and Padman, born to his wife Sukumari. They were twins. After them, Singan was born, and after him, Tharagan. Among these, Sage Agastya preaches to Suran and Padman, narrating the greatness of Lord Muruga. In this, Suran wished to be the peacock, the vehicle of Muruga. Padman, due to his devotion, wished to be the rooster, the banner of Lord Muruga. Similarly, Singan hears the greatness of Durga preached by the sages Kiranavindu and Idabayogi. Tharagan hears the greatness of Sastha. Singan, having heard the greatness of Durga, wished to be the lion, the vehicle of Durga. Tharagan, having heard the greatness of Sastha, wished to be his vehicle. Thus, because Suran, Padman, Singan, and Tharagan wished this, they were later born as the Asuras Surapadman, Simhamukhan, and Tharagan. In this, Suran and Padman were in one body as Surapadman. When Lord Muruga split this Surapadman with his spear, they, who had been in one body, became two, a rooster and a peacock. Lord Muruga accepted the rooster as his banner and the peacock as his vehicle, as described in Kandapuranam. Thus, the rooster and the peacock are always connected in many ways. Therefore, the hunters, whose tribal deity is Lord Muruga, traditionally offered roosters and peacocks together as a symbol of their prayer. What is the meaning of offering something as a sacrifice or a vow? It means removing one's ownership and attachment to these things that belonged to oneself, considering them as belonging to Lord Muruga, and placing them before Lord Muruga. This is called a vow or an offering. It is our tradition that by making such offerings, Lord Muruga will be pleased and bestow his grace. Sivakavimani, who wrote all this, further writes: "Many people in these days, without understanding these truths, without any fear, out of excessive ignorance, act cruelly and hunt and torment peacocks, goats, and other vehicles of Lord Muruga, and roosters, chickens, and other banners of his, in various ways, thus incurring the wrath of Lord Muruga and falling into terrible hell." He states that harming and disrespecting Lord Muruga's banner and vehicle while worshipping Lord Muruga will yield no results. He also states that it will lead to hell. Now, Nagan and Thaththai next offer numerous bells as garlands before Lord Muruga's shrine and pray. They offer beautiful Kadamba garlands to Muruga. They perform Kuravai Koothu (a type of folk dance) for Muruga, perform grand Anangadal (a ritual dance), and pray by holding grand festivals. Kuravai Koothu, or Kuravai, is a type of dance. Adiyarkkunallar, the commentator on Silappathikaram, writes that it falls under the category of "Vinodha Koothu" (amusing dance). Kuravai Koothu means people praising the prowess of their deity and dancing according to the specific land. As per "Seyon Meya Mai Varai Ulagamum," this is the Kurinji land. Therefore, the mountain-dwelling hunters sing and dance the praises of Velan, the deity of Kurinji land. This is called Kuravai Koothu. The women performing this dance bathe in waterfalls and springs. Then they beat the Thondakapparai (a drum) and play instruments like the Kodu (a horn). They sing songs belonging to the Kurinji land. They spread fragrant smoke using aromatic substances found in the mountainous region. They offer flowers to God using various flowers found in the Kurinji land. They scatter mixed flowers. Then they perform Veriyadal (a ritual dance) and praise Muruga, the spear in his hand, and Muruga's bravery, by dancing and singing. This Kundrakkuravai, a Kuravai Koothu praising Muruga, can be seen in detail in ancient Tamil texts like Thirumurugatruppadai and Silappathikaram. "Perumthan Kanaveera Narunthan Maalai Thunaiyura Varuththu Thoonga Naatri Nalimalai Silambin Nannagar Vaazhththi Narum Pugai Eduththu Kurinji Paadi Imilizhisai Aruviyodu Inniyam Karanga Uruvappal Pooththuuy Veruvara Kuruthich Chenthinai Parappik Kuramagal Murugiya Niruththu Muran Inar Atka Murugatruppaduththa Urukezhu Viyanagar Aadugalan Silamba Paadi Palavudan Koduvaai Vaiththuk Kodumani Iyakkki Odaap Pootkai Pinimugam Vaazhththi Vendunar Vendiyangu Eythinr Vazhipadu" is from Thirumurugatruppadai. The explanation of this verse is what we saw above. In addition, in Silappathikaram, there are also verses about Kundrakkuravai Koothu: "Uravu Neer Maa Kondra Veleththi Kuravai Thoduththondru Paadugam Vaa Thozhi" and "Seerkezhu Sentilum Sengodum Venkundrum Eragamum Neenga Iraivan Kai Vel Andre Paarirum Pavvaththinul Pukku Pandroru Naal Sormaa Thadindha Sudarilaiya Velale" and "Animugangal Oraarum Eeraaru Kaiyum Inaiyindri Thaan Udaiyaan Endhiya Velandre Pinimugam Mel Kondu Avunar Peedu Azhiyum Vannam Ani Visumbil Konetha Maaratta Velale" and "Saravana Poom Poigaiyinil Thaaymaar Aruvar Thirumulaip Paal Undaan Thirukkai Vel Andre Varuthi Kiriyon Avunan Maarbum Pilandhu Kurugu Peyar Kundram Kondra Neduvellale." Ilango Adigal has composed songs about Kundrakkuravai Koothu in Silappathikaram. Similarly, we see in Silappathikaram that the people of the Mullai land, as per "Maayon Meya Kaadurai Ulagam," perform Aychchiyar Kuravai, praising the glory of Kannan, their deity.

Thus, Nagan and Thaththai perform Kuravai Koothu and offer their prayers. After that, they perform Anangadal, a Veriyadal, remembering Lord Muruga. Performing Veriyadal means invoking and bringing Lord Muruga into a trance. In this Veriyadal, those who perform it will hold the Vel (spear), praise Muruga, and dance. Lord Muruga will enter a trance in them. When Lord Muruga enters a trance in the person performing the Veriyadal, people will pray to him for what they desire. It is a tradition that he will also grant boons in accordance with the people's prayers. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that they perform Anangadal, a Veriyadal, using Anangu. Anangu means a woman, that is, a divine woman. Therefore, they perform Veriyattam with a great divine woman and celebrate a grand festival for Lord Muruga. Thus, Nagan and Thaththai offer roosters and peacocks to Lord Muruga. They adorn him with garlands of bells and Kadamba flowers. They pray to Lord Muruga by holding festivals like Kuravaiyadal, Kuravai Paadal, and Anangadal. Lord Muruga accepts their prayers and bestows his grace upon them. In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman beautifully describes Lord Muruga. Sekkizhar states what kind of person Lord Muruga is. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Lord Muruga as "Eyil Udai Purangal Setra Enthaiyaar Maintharaana Mayil Udai Kotra Oorthi Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil Udai Thadakkai Venri Annanaar." Lord Shiva is the one who burned the three cities (Thiripuram) with great walls. Lord Muruga is his son. Lord Muruga is referred to here as "Mayil Udai Kotra Oorthi Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil Udai Thadakkai Venri Annanaar." Sekkizhar Swamigal gives so many epithets to Lord Muruga. Let's look at this in detail. Lord Muruga has a victorious peacock as his vehicle. "Kotram" means bravery and victory. Lord Muruga has a peacock with this "Kotram" as his vehicle. Everyone knows that the peacock is Lord Muruga's vehicle. But Lord Muruga has three types of peacocks as his vehicles. Firstly, Pranava itself is Lord Muruga's peacock vehicle. It is called Pranava Mayil. Next, Indra becomes a peacock vehicle for Lord Muruga. The third peacock vehicle is Surapadman, one of the two, who transforms into a peacock and becomes Lord Muruga's vehicle. Thus, Lord Muruga has three peacock vehicles: Pranava Mayil, Indra Mayil, and Asura Mayil. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Lord Muruga has a peacock with "Kotram" as his vehicle. We saw that "Kotram" means bravery and victory. This epithet applies to all three peacock vehicles. Are not the wise truly brave? Are not the wise those who have transcended all principles? Therefore, is not Nada Tattva (the principle of sound) the foremost of all principles? Does not Pranava originate from that Nada Tattva? Therefore, for the wise who have transcended principles, Pranava assists in that bravery to transcend principles, and Pranava is the cause of the victory of those wise who transcend principles. Therefore, Pranava Mayil is the peacock with "Kotram." Now, a war takes place between Surapadman and Lord Muruga. In the war, Surapadman, with his illusory powers, takes the form of a Chakravaka bird and a Nemi bird and opposes Lord Muruga. At that time, Indra transforms into a peacock and supports Lord Muruga. Lord Muruga, riding on this Indra peacock, fights with Surapadman, who has transformed into a Chakravaka bird. He splits his illusory form. Therefore, Devendra himself transformed into a peacock and supported Muruga here. Devendra is the leader of all Devas, isn't he a victorious one? Therefore, Devendra Mayil is the peacock with "Kotram." Next, at the end of the war, Surapadman transforms into a huge mango tree. Lord Muruga's Velayudham (spear) splits his form into two. One of these two parts becomes a peacock, and the other becomes a rooster. Muruga takes the peacock as his vehicle and the rooster as his banner. Therefore, this Asura Mayil is also a vehicle with "Kotram," a peacock with "Kotram." This can be seen in Kandapuranam, in the Surapadman Vadai Padalam, in verses 378, 494, and 499. Therefore, the peacock is Lord Muruga's vehicle, isn't it? Lord Shiva, Muruga's father, has the bull as his vehicle. "Aasai theera koduppaar alangam vidai mel varuvaar" is from Thirukkadavoor Mayanam Aazhudaiya Pillaiyar Thevaram. Therefore, Lord Shiva comes riding on a white bull to grant the wishes of those who desire them. But his son, Lord Muruga, to grant the wishes of those who desire them, comes faster than his father, having the peacock as his victorious vehicle. Lord Shiva comes walking on a bull. Lord Muruga comes flying on a peacock to grant the wishes of those who desire them. We will continue to reflect on the greatness of Lord Muruga through Sekkizhar Peruman's verses. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 131, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Nagan and Thaththai perform various prayers to Lord Muruga, desiring a child. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappa Nayanar is about to be born as their son. In this section, Sekkizhar Swamigal praises and describes Lord Muruga with various epithets. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Lord Muruga has a victorious peacock as his vehicle. According to Saiva Siddhanta, Anava Mala (ego), a flaw, is always present with the soul. Therefore, Anava Mala is called Sahaja Mala (innate impurity). Furthermore, the nature of the soul is to take on the color of what it associates with, as stated in Saiva Siddhanta. This means that the soul will manifest the characteristics of the object it is associated with. If a coral is placed near a crystal, the crystal will take on the red color of the coral and appear red. Saiva Siddhanta elders use this as an example to illustrate that the soul takes on the color of what it associates with. When God redeems the soul, like a coral embedded in a crystal, He manifests Himself in the soul and redeems it. At that time, the soul's quality of "asat" (non-being) will disappear. The soul's quality of "sat" (being) will prevail. Because God has redeemed the soul, the Anava Mala that had been obscuring the soul will become powerless and subside, causing no harm to the soul. As a result, the soul will experience God's bliss. The reference that Lord Muruga took Suran as his peacock vehicle clearly illustrates this truth of Saiva Siddhanta. The impurities of souls are three: Anava, Karma, and Maya. Surapadman, Simhamukhan, and Tharagan are examples of these three Asuras. Among them, Surapadman was the embodiment and locus of Anava Mala. The other two are examples of Karma and Maya. When God redeems the soul and grants it liberation, He destroys Maya Mala and Karma Mala. However, even in the state of liberation, Anava Mala does not perish. But before the presence of God, it remains inactive and subdued. Here, in Kandapuranam, Lord Muruga also destroys the two Asuras, Simhamukhan and Tharagan, who were the embodiments of Karma Mala and Maya Mala. He does not destroy Surapadman, the embodiment of Anava Mala, like the other two, but transforms him into a peacock and a rooster and keeps him with Him. He keeps him under His sacred feet. Therefore, just as Anava Mala does not perish even in the state of liberation but remains subdued at the sacred feet of God, Suran here transforms into a peacock and remains forever at the sacred feet of God, illustrating this Saiva Siddhanta concept. It is said that a peacock has seven colors. Here, the peacock, that is, the Asura peacock, is a symbol of Anava, isn't it? Even though Anava Mala is one, it expands into seven when it functions, according to Saiva Siddhanta. The seven functions of Anava are Moham (delusion), Madham (pride), Ragam (attachment), Kavalai (worry), Thabam (suffering), Vaattam (weariness), and Visiththiram (strangeness). Just as a peacock has seven colors, the functions of Anava, symbolized by the Asura peacock, are also seven. Even when God places the soul under His lotus feet, Anava does not perish there but remains powerless, which is a principle of Gnana Sastra. Just as his father, Lord Shiva, burned the three cities (Thiripuram), which were symbols of the three impurities, and purified the souls, so too his son, Lord Muruga, purifies the souls and places them under his sacred feet, demonstrating his victorious power by riding the peacock as his vehicle. This reference is noteworthy here. Next, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Lord Muruga as one who wields the "Thinmai Ayil" (powerful spear) that split the mountain. "Varai" means mountain. Here, it refers to Mount Krauncha. It was in this Mount Krauncha that Tharagan hid and performed illusions. On his way to Mahendrapuri for the Surasamharam (destruction of Suras), Lord Muruga, with his army of Bhootas, fought Tharagan, who was hiding in the illusion of Mount Krauncha. Therefore, Lord Muruga split Mount Krauncha and killed Tharagan. The details of this can be found in Kandapuranam. It was the "Thinmai Ayil," the powerful spear, that split Mount Krauncha and killed Tharagan. "Ayil" means spear. The spear is the Gnana Shakti (power of wisdom) of Lord Muruga. According to Tamil grammar, the word "Vel" itself evolved from the verb "Vel" (to win) as a primary long verbal noun. Therefore, here, Lord Muruga's Velayudham (spear) also brings only victory. The Vel is the Gnana Shakti of Lord Muruga. Among the three powers – Iccha Shakti (power of will), Gnana Shakti (power of wisdom), and Kriya Shakti (power of action) – Gnana Shakti is the most excellent. "Eththiram Gnanam Ullathu Aththiram Icchai Seithi" is the 62nd verse in the first chapter of Sivagnana Siddhiyar Pramana Iyal. Its meaning is that the special functions of Gnana Shakti are Iccha Shakti and Kriya Shakti. Gnana is the very nature of Shiva Shakti. It is Gnana that knows and reveals in a general way to bestow grace upon living beings. That is called Para Shakti. That Gnana performs the five actions to bring about the maturation of impurities in living beings. Therefore, Para Shakti manifests in the form of the Velayudham in the sacred hand of Lord Kumara. In accordance with this, there is also the tradition that Para Shakti gave the Vel to Muruga. The unobstructed knowledge that manifests as one with Sivaguhaperuman is called Gnana Shakti. That one Para Shakti, out of great compassion for souls, exists in three forms. To illustrate that he possesses these three powers, Lord Muruga manifested with Valliammaiyar, Deivanaiammaiyar, and the Velayudham. Iccha Shakti is praised as Valliammaiyar, Kriya Shakti as Deivanaiammaiyar, and Gnana Shakti as Velayudham by the pure Vidyas. Therefore, this Vel destroys the three groups of impurities, namely Suran and his army, their illusions, and their strengths. Kandapuranam states that the 11 weapons in Lord Muruga's 11 sacred hands – Thomaram, Thuvasham, Kulisam, Angusum, Ambu, Pangayam, Mani, Mazhu, Thandu, Vaal, Vil – are the 11 Rudras, and the Velayudham is Shiva Shakti. All other weapons are inanimate objects, but the Vel in Muruga's hand is a conscious entity. Just as Para Shakti is inseparable from Lord Shiva and thus Para Shakti is the Supreme Being, similarly, this Velayudham in Lord Muruga's sacred hand is said to be Kandaswami himself. The Velayudham is not conquered by any other weapon. The Velayudham, taking many forms, conquers everything else. The Velayudham destroys bondages, as it was the Velayudham that dispelled the delusion of Veerabahu Devar and others when they were bound by the illusory bondages of Bhanukopan and Simhamukhan. To bestow liberation upon souls by severing their bonds with this Para Shakti, the Gnana Shakti of such greatness, and to grant them His sacred feet, Lord Muruga, the Supreme Acharya who took a divine form, holds the Gnana Shakti, the Velayudham, in His right sacred hand, and places His left sacred hand, which resembles a lotus flower, on His thigh. This divine form, with one sacred face and two sacred eyes, is praised in texts as Gnana Shakti Dharan, Gnana Shakti Dhara Swamy. That Vel, the Gnana Shakti of Lord Muruga, possessed the strength to destroy the powerful Suras who had received boons that made them unconquerable by any power other than God's. Sekkizhar Swamigal specifically praises it as "Thinmai Ayil." "Tharaganum Maayathadankiriyum Thoolaaga Veeravadivel Viduththone" is from Kandar Kali Venpa. "Kurugu Peyar Kundram Kondra Neduvellale" is from Kundrakkuravai in Silappathikaram. "Veeravel Tharaivel Vinnor Siraimitta Theeravel Sevvel Thirukkaivel Vaari Kuliththa Vel Kotravel Soor Maarbum Kundrum Thulaiththa Vel Undae Thunai" is a unique poem about the Vel. Arunagirinathar Peruman also composed a Vel Vaguppu. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman praises the Vel in Lord Muruga's hand as "Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil." Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal gives the next epithet to Lord Muruga, saying that he has such a Vel in his "Thadankai" (large hand). Lord Muruga's "Thadankai" punished the Suras. It relieved the sufferings of the Devas. It restored their world to the Devas. Such is the greatness of Lord Muruga's "Thadankai." "Vel Kezhu Thadankai Saal Perunjelva Kundram Kondra Kundraa Kotraththu" is from Thirumurugatruppadai. Thus, by the grace of Lord Muruga, who has a victorious peacock as his vehicle, a powerful spear that split Mount Krauncha, and large sacred hands of great glory, Nagan, the leader of the hunter clan who prayed to Lord Muruga for a child, had his wife Thaththai conceive. Lord Muruga, who is united with Valliammaiyar, bestows good grace, granting appropriate enjoyment and liberation to all living beings in the world. As if to illustrate this, those who prayed to Lord Muruga for a child began to receive what they desired. Sivakavimani further explains the nuances of these verses. While the Vedas praise Lord Shiva once as "Sadashivom," they praise Lord Kumara thrice as "Subrahmanyom, Subrahmanyom, Subrahmanyom." Similarly, here, the author, in this Tamil Veda, Periya Puranam, praises Lord Shiva once as "Purangal Setra Enthaiyaar." But he praises Lord Muruga thrice, associating him with his power of grace, the Vel, as "Sevvel Murugavel Porani Neduvellon Ayil Udai Thadankai Venri Annanaar." We must observe this uniqueness. Sekkizhar Swamigal has placed the news of the birth of a child by the grace of Lord Muruga as the twelfth verse in the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar. As per "Pannirandu Kannudaiya Pillai Thondrum," Sekkizhar Swamigal, blessed with divine grace, has placed this meaning, which describes the manifestation of the grace of Lord Muruga, who has twelve sacred eyes and twelve sacred hands, in the twelfth verse of the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar in the twelfth Thirumurai, Periya Puranam. This arrangement is celebrated as one of the divine special arrangements made by Lord Nataraja, the divine speaker, in the author's divine words. Similarly, the divine aspect of Lord Nataraja is found in other places as well. In the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar, in the 51st verse, he describes the incident where the Mother (Parvati) performed penance in Kanchi and worshipped God. According to the saying "Aathi Ezhuththu Avai 50 Odu Ondru Enbar," the letters are 51. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal places the incident where Goddess Parvati performed penance in Kanchi and worshipped God in the 51st verse of the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar. There are 28 Shiva Agamas. Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed the history of Thirumoolar Nayanar, who revealed the meaning of those 28 Shiva Agamas in Tamil as Thirumantras, in 28 verses. All these are divine special arrangements made by Lord Nataraja. Therefore, this Periya Puranam, composed by Sekkizhar Swamigal, is not ordinary human speech. We must study it, realizing that it is the word of the Supreme Being, Pathi. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 132, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappa Nayanar incarnates. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this. Kannappa Nayanar incarnates to bring glory to the Kaanavar clan. Kaanavar are hunters, the people of the Kurinji land. By having Kannappa Nayanar, that hunter clan gained glory. It is the conviction of our elders that if a great soul, a great person, incarnates in a clan, that entire clan will be enlightened and saved by him. Arunachala Puranam states that Lord Shiva said, "We will grant liberation for 21 generations in the lineage of one who witnesses the Karthigai Jyothi in Thiruvannamalai." Therefore, if someone in a clan witnesses the Karthigai Jyothi in Thiruvannamalai, 21 generations in his lineage attain liberation through him. Thus, it is our ancestral tradition that if a great person incarnates in a clan, that entire clan will be enlightened and saved by him. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Thaththai conceives. Due to this, the entire clan rejoices. Since she is pregnant, the people of the hunter clan perform the appropriate rituals each month, praying for the pregnancy to progress well and for a healthy child to be born. Generally, in our tradition, when a woman in a household becomes pregnant, it was customary to perform various prayers and pujas each month, praying for the healthy growth of the fetus. This is because there are many chances for harm to occur to the fetus in each month. Therefore, it is customary for the pregnant woman and her family to pray that God protect the fetus from any harm specific to that month. Even in Thiruvachagam, in the Potti Thiru Agaval, Manikkavasagar Swamigal lists all the harms the fetus faces in each month and sings that they have survived all these harms. "Maanudap pirappil maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththu orumathi thaanriyil irumaiyil pizhaiththum irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum mummathi thannul ammatham pizhaiththum eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum aaru thingalil ooralar pizhaiththum ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum ettu thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum thakka thasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagarath thuyaridai pizhaiththum" is from Potti Thiru Agaval. Let's look at its explanation. First, Manikkavasagar says, "Maanudap pirappinul maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum." He says that the fetus survives the battle waged by worms, which are germs, inside the mother's womb, without being destroyed. When the mother and father unite, the living organisms in the white fluid released from the father's body enter the mother's womb. At that point, to unite with the mother's ovum in the red fluid in the womb, a battle ensues. One organism pushes aside all the other organisms that came with it and, without being destroyed, goes into the mother's ovum and unites with it, doesn't it? Thus, one organism pushes aside various competing organisms and unites with the ovum inside the mother's womb. This is what he first refers to as "maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum." Next, he speaks about the suffering that occurs in the first month. He says, "orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum." That is, in the first month, the fetus is the size of a Thaanri fruit inside the mother's womb. Now, what harm can befall this fetus? If the mother's and father's energies do not unite well and there is a difference, then harm can befall the fetus. There is a chance for the pregnancy to miscarry. This is what he refers to as "orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum." Now, what harm can occur in the second month? From what harm do we escape and survive, says Manikkavasagar. He says, "irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum." The explanation for this is that in the second month, the fetus escapes a certain kind of harm that occurs when the remaining bindu (seminal fluid) enters the place where nadam (sound) and bindu have united and developed. Even where the father's and mother's gametes have united, if other male gametes enter and cause harm, there is a chance for the fetus to be destroyed. To prevent this, the mother's ovum needs to be sealed. At this time, due to illness, dizziness, or other reasons, if a male gamete enters the mother's womb, there is a chance that it could be dangerous to the existing fetus. Therefore, Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that we have survived such suffering. A similar verse appears in Potti Palthodai: "Eppirappum munsei iruvinaiyal nischayiththu porppudaiya thanthai thaai pogaththul garppamaai pullil panipol pugunthu valaikku utpadungal ellaipadaraa udarath theendiya thee palvagaiyal ange kidantha anaathi uyir thampasiyal engeyumaga edukkum ena vem kumbhi kaayak karukkuzhiyil kaaththirunthum." This is a verse from the sacred text Potti Palthodai, lines 13 to 19. This is the meaning of the phrase "irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum." Next, Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings about the danger we have escaped from in the third month. He says, "mummathi thannil ammatham pizhaiththum." As mentioned above, when the gametes unite and the opening is sealed, and the fetus grows, the mother's uterine fluid will secrete inside the womb for the growth of the fetus. This uterine fluid should secrete in the required amount. If it secretes excessively, there is a chance for the womb to open and for a miscarriage to occur. This is the test or suffering that comes to the fetus in the third month. The Swamigal sings that we have escaped and survived this. Next, he sings about the fourth month. He says, "eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum." In the fourth month, the uterine fluid secreted inside the womb, after the necessary portion for fetal growth, remains as waste and appears dark. If this dark waste accumulates excessively, there is a high chance of harm to the fetus due to its toxicity. This is what he refers to as "eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum." "Irul" means darkness, doesn't it? Next, in the fifth month, he sings, "anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum." In the fifth month, if the uterine fluid and its dark waste, which accumulates in the womb, become excessive, it is difficult for the fetus to survive. Therefore, he says, "anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum." Now, in the sixth month, he sings, "aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum." In the sixth month, the fetus, which was a lump, develops organs like hands well. At that time, the womb needs to expand to accommodate the growing fetus. If the womb does not provide enough space for this expansion, then the fetus will be harmed. Manikkavasagar Swamigal says that they survived this by saying, "aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum." Next, he says, "ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum." This is the suffering that can occur in the seventh month. In the seventh month, almost all the organs of the fetus would have developed and grown. Therefore, there is a chance that the womb might not be able to bear the fetus and the opening might occur, causing harm to the fetus. Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that they survived this by saying, "ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum." Next, he sings about the suffering that comes in the eighth month as "ettam thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum." The Sanskrit word "kashtam" becomes "kattam" in Tamil. In the eighth month, due to the very rapid growth of the fetus, the excessive accumulation of waste products from the uterine fluid that nourishes it, and the density of the food consumed by the mother, the fetus experiences distress. Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that they survived all these sufferings by saying, "ettam thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum." Next, he sings about the suffering that comes in the ninth month as "onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum." He describes escaping from the sufferings that arise from the excess of the previously mentioned factors in the ninth month. The tenth month, that is, the tenth month, is the appropriate time for the birth of the child. Therefore, he says, "thakka dasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagara thuyaridai pizhaiththum." He sings that they survived the great ocean-like suffering experienced by the mother giving birth in the tenth month, and also the suffering that the fetus itself, as a child, experiences when coming out into the world. Thus, Manikkavasagar Swamigal, in Potti Thiru Agaval, sings about all the sufferings a child escapes and survives from the time it is conceived until it is born into the world. "Maanudap pirappinul maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum irumathi vilaivil orumaiyil pizhaiththum mummathi thannul ammatham pizhaiththum eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum ettu thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum thakka dasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagara thuyaridai pizhaiththum" - these are all sung by Manikkavasagar Swamigal in Potti Thiru Agaval. He does not stop there but continues to sing about the sufferings a child experiences at each stage after birth. However, the explanations of those parts are beyond the scope of the section we are currently looking at in Periya Puranam. This is because Sekkizhar Swamigal states, doesn't he, that the hunters perform worship every month to protect the fetus from the harms that come each month, so that Thaththai's pregnancy grows beautifully without any suffering? That is why we reflected on this part of Thiruvachagam, which is related to it. Devotees can find other parts of Manikkavasagar Swamigal's Potti Thiru Agaval, which lists all the sufferings that come to a child even after birth. Thus, Thaththai's pregnancy grew beautifully each month, and by the penance performed in previous births, Thaththai gave birth to a beautiful baby boy. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappadeva Nayanar incarnated there. Sekkizhar Swamigal continues to sing about how Kannappadeva Nayanar grew up. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 133, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Kannappa Nayanar incarnated due to immeasurable penance. Tattai and Naga performed immeasurable penance to have Kannappa Nayanar as their son. The hunter tribe performed immeasurable penance to have Kannappa Nayanar born into their lineage. The southern region performed great penance for Kannappa Deva Nayanar to incarnate there. Not only that, all living beings performed penance for Kannappa Deva Nayanar to be born on this earth. Thus, Kannappa Deva Nayanar incarnated due to the strength and benefit of various immeasurable penances. When the Milk Ocean was churned, didn't the moon emerge from it? Similarly, just as the dark ocean, desiring to give birth to a moon like the Milk Ocean, performed penance and received a moon, Tattai gave birth to Kannappa Deva Nayanar. The moon born from the Milk Ocean is a white moon, a pale moon. But this moon, born to Tattai, is Thinnanar, Kannappa Nayanar, who is dark-skinned. The moon born from the Milk Ocean waxes and wanes. But this moon, Kannappa Deva Nayanar, born to Tattai, grows to 16 kalais (phases), meaning he grows to 16 years of age, and then attains the bliss of liberation, a state where there is no waxing or waning. That moon was protected by God. This moon, Kannappa Deva, is always by God's side, never leaving Him, and acts as His protector. Therefore, here the ocean is compared to Tattai, and the moon emerging from the ocean is compared to the Nayanar. When a full moon appears, it first shows a reddish hue. Children, as they grow, will in later days attain their true color. But any child, immediately after coming out of the mother's womb, will have a reddish color due to the fresh blood circulation. Here too, Sekkizhar Swamigal, who described the child as a "milk moon" when the mother gave birth, compares Kannappa Nayanar to a dark cloud when his father lifted him in his arms. Furthermore, Sivakavimani writes another reason why Kannappa Deva Nayanar is called the moon. Lord Muruga incarnated from the spark of fire that emerged from Shiva's third eye. He shines like the sun, as described by "palar pugazh nyayiru kadarkkandaangu" (the sun praised by many, seen in the ocean). This child, Kannappa Nayanar, was born and developed through the grace of Lord Muruga. Therefore, he is like the moon. Isn't it a scientific truth now that the moon shines only with the light of the sun? Similarly, it is highly appropriate to compare this child, who appeared by receiving the light of Lord Muruga, the sun, and by His grace, to the moon. The hunters, upon learning that a male child was born to their leader Naga, rejoiced greatly. They showered pearls from large elephant tusks like rain. Similarly, they showered pearls of rich moisture from bamboo like rain. The hunters, in their joy, showered all other gems found in the mountains like rain and celebrated. Our leader was without a child for a long time. Now, a male child has been born to illuminate our leader's lineage and to signify the arrival of a new young leader for us, hasn't it? The meaning of this is that Naga, the leader of the hunters, and all the hunters, his subjects, showered pearls and other gems like rain upon beggars in their joy. Gems might be priceless objects for those living in cities and towns. But for the mountain-dwelling hunters, numerous gems are naturally available in the mountainous regions. That is why they shower them like rain and give them to beggars. Here, the subjects of the hunter king rejoice and celebrate the birth of his son. Then the king invites his subjects to his son's archery festival and honors them. Afterwards, the subjects present their grievances to the king. Then Naga asks them to accept his son as their king. We must note these acts of royal loyalty between the king and his subjects. We can see this same message in the Puranic histories of Manu Neethi Chozhan, Kalarittarivar, Murthi Nayanar, and others. In these days, we must compare and reflect on the internal enmity that arises, spreading throughout the country, aiming to destroy and harass the crowns of kings, which is contrary to this. Now, with the incarnation of Kannappa Deva Nayanar, the Devas from the heavens shower Kalpaka flower rain. Similarly, when the Nayanar attained liberation, the Devas showered Kalpaka flower rain. When other Nayanars attained liberation, the Devas would similarly shower Kalpaka flower rain from the heavens. We have read that divine drums would resound. But in this Purana, the Devas shower Kalpaka flower rain even when the Nayanar incarnates. Generally, the Saivite tradition is to celebrate the day when great souls join the feet of God and attain liberation. Celebrating birthdays (Jayanti) is not a Saivite tradition. That is why Guru Puja is performed for each Nayanar on the auspicious star day of their liberation. Similarly, we have read that when each Nayanar attains liberation, these Devas shower Kalpaka flower rain and divine drums resound. But here, these events occur even when Kannappa Deva Nayanar incarnates. This is because the Nayanar's incarnation itself is as glorious as liberation, which is why Sekkizhar Swamigal has sung about the showering of flowers from the heavens and the resounding of divine drums. We must also note that this happens similarly during the incarnation of Thirugnanasambandha Murthy. Father Naga arrives. He holds the newborn child in his sacred hands. This is like a dark cloud resting on a large dark mountain. Naga, with his strength, valor, and physique, is like a dark mountain. The child he holds in his hand is also dark-skinned. Sekkizhar Swamigal compares it to a dark cloud waiting to shower rain on a mountain peak. This sight of a dark cloud laden with water standing on a mountain peak is like the world being saved by the great rain showered by the cloud. Didn't this child, in later times, save the entire world with the rain of love showered by the cloud, due to the penance performed on the mountain peak? Perhaps that is why Sekkizhar Swamigal made this comparison. The newborn child is dark-skinned. It has a beautiful dark body. This dark sacred body is a radiant body that emits light. Generally, objects of other colors, when struck by the sun's rays, are said to reflect the light of their own color from the seven colors in the ray. Therefore, they appear in that color. But black color, unlike that, absorbs all the rays of the sun and does not reflect them outwards, as optical scientists now state, don't they? But here, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that even though the Nayanar's body is dark, it shines brightly. This child, born with a dark complexion, is a beautiful child. It is a child that evokes affection in all who see it. Can dark-colored objects also be beautiful? Yes. Isn't Manmatha, who is praised as the most beautiful in this universe, dark-skinned? Isn't Thirumal, the father of Manmatha, also dark-skinned? Don't we read that the bodies of Rama and Krishna, two incarnations praised as the best among the ten incarnations of Thirumal, were very dark and beautiful? Therefore, black is also beautiful. That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal praises the Nayanar as "karum kadir virikkum meni kaamar kuzhavi" (a beautiful child with a dark, radiant body). The newborn child is like a tiger cub. This child possesses all the good characteristics described in the sciences of body features like Samudrika Lakshanam and palmistry. There are many signs in the child that indicate great and rare qualities, difficult to measure or obtain in this world. When we reflect on the glory of the Thirukalathi Puranam, we recall how many great personalities praised and revered Kannappa Deva Nayanar in various texts, didn't we? Sekkizhar Swamigal subtly sings here that this child has incarnated in a way that is praised by all great and wise people. Father Naga takes the child Nayanar in his sacred hands. Since the child is difficult to hold, very strong, heavy, and firm, father Naga names this child Thinnan. Therefore, this is a name given based on the Nayanar's characteristic. It is a common practice in the world to name someone based on various reasons such as ancestral name, family name, grandfather's name, or a characteristic. Since this child was firm (thin), his father named him Thinnan. Indeed, his father named him Thinnanar based on the firmness of the child's body. In reality, this name Thinnanar is also very appropriate due to the firmness of the penance the Nayanar performed in many previous births and the firmness of the intense love the Nayanar possessed. Now, the mountain-dwelling people worship the mountain deities to protect this child, and accordingly, they perform all the necessary rituals according to their ancient customs and traditions. They place iron-mixed ornaments on the child's hands and feet. Thus, this act of protection varies from land to land and tradition to tradition. Sekkizhar Swamigal, after mentioning the naming ceremony, the first ritual for the child, in the first verse, is now going to describe the child's growth through subsequent stages. He first describes the "kaappu paruvam" (protection stage), which is the first of these stages. Similarly, Sekkizhar Swamigal will sing about the other stages up to the first year in the following verses, and about his development beyond the first year, including his archery training, in subsequent verses. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 11 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 134, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. The parents perform the "kaappu" (protection ritual) for the child Thinnanar. They fashion neem seeds and pieces of neem wood into beads, stringing them together to form a multi-beaded waist-cord. Sivakavimani writes that in ancient times, people living in different regions led natural lives, using the resources available in their respective lands. Thus, they fashioned ornaments and protective charms for their children from the materials found in their areas, thereby shaping their way of life. Tamil grammar categorized these as "mudhal," "karu," and "uriporutkal" (primary, secondary, and inherent elements) for each "thinai" (landscape). There are no records anywhere indicating that even a small item was imported from foreign countries for use. He writes that it is regrettable that people now, under the guise of civilization, use all kinds of products available from all countries and make it their way of life, which is an obscene and uncivilized way of living. Thus, they perform protection rituals and adorn the children. Not only that, but as the child grows through different stages, they perform grand pujas to the forest deities. They pile up and offer various items like fruits, tubers, and meat to the forest deities, worshipping them. Thus, the child completes its first year and enters its second year. The end of the first year is the stage of unsteady walking. The second year is the stage when the child walks and plays. We still have sayings like "ettavathu maadham eduthu adi vaikkkum" (takes a step in the eighth month) and "ettavathu maadham ettu vaikkkum" (places eight steps in the eighth month). So, in the eighth month, the child learns to walk by holding the hands of its mother, nurses, etc. After four months, when the first year ends and the second year begins, the child reaches the stage of walking unsteadily without any support. For such a growing and walking child, they tie a tiger's claw as a "chutti" (ornament). According to the hunter tradition, a "chutti" is an ornament worn on the forehead by young boys. Even today, it is customary in many families to tie a single tiger's claw or two claws facing each other, set in gold, and wear it on the chest of young boys. This is a child who has completed one year, so the hair on its head will be short and sparse. Therefore, we must remember that Sekkizhar sings "punthalai siru magaarkal" (small children with sparse hair). This sparse, small hair will later grow into curly, well-defined locks, which is common. They cut the long quills of a porcupine into small pieces of equal length and string them into a garland for the child. They adorn the child with a "sathangai" (anklet with bells) made of many beads. They adorn it with a "thandai" (anklet) made of elephant tusk. "Kudambai" is a type of earring, an ornament worn on the ear. They adorn it with "kudambai" earrings made of various beads. Thus adorned, the child, possessing great Shiva-punyam (merit from devotion to Shiva) that removes all faults of those who see it, plays there, displaying its meritorious form. The child plays in the street. When it sees its parents, it runs quickly to them and cuddles with them. This makes its father and mother very happy and joyful. The child speaks in babbling language. As per Thirukkural, "Makkal mei theendal udalukku inbam matru avar sol kettal inbam sevikku" (The touch of children is joy to the body, and hearing their words is joy to the ear), and "Kuzhal inithu yaazh inithu enbar tham makkal mazhalai sol kelaathavar" (Those who have not heard the babbling words of their children say that the flute is sweet and the lyre is sweet).
