It is explained that parents are very happy to hear the babbling language of children, isn't it? Naga and Tattai experience such happiness. Hearing the babbling words of Kannappa Nayanar, Thinnanar, how do these babbling words of Thinnanar come? They come mixed with the sacred water in his mouth. Lord Shiva later says in his history that the water in Thinnanar's mouth seems more sacred than the Ganges to him. So, these babbling words come from Thinnanar's mouth water, which is more sacred than the Ganges water. It is natural for babbling words to come mixed with the child's saliva, isn't it? Thus, Thinnanar grows slowly. It is a hunter settlement, isn't it? We have already seen that hunters keep watch animals in their homes, haven't we? Similarly, there is a tiger as a watch animal in Naga's house. Tigers and other such animals usually have the habit of keeping their mouths wide open. This watch animal, the tiger, also keeps its mouth wide open. Thinking this widely open mouth to be a cave, the child Thinnanar puts his golden hand into the tiger's open mouth. What kind of hand is Thinnanar's hand? A golden hand. In later days, this hand hunted and offered food to Lord Shiva and protected Him. This hand removed the suffering of the two luminaries, the sun and the moon. In later days, didn't Kannappa Nayanar pluck out his own eyes and apply them to stop the blood flowing from the Lord's sacred eyes? Aren't the sun and moon the two sacred eyes of the Lord? Therefore, wasn't Kannappa Devanayar the one who saw the flow of blood from the Lord's eyes, which are the sun and moon, and removed it? Wasn't it Kannappa Nayanar's golden hands that offered food to the Lord, protected Him, and removed the suffering of the sun and moon? The child Thinnanar, thinking the tiger's wide-open mouth to be a cave, puts his golden hand inside it. The affectionate father sees this. This is Thinnanar, his son, born after a long period of penance, destined to inherit his position. So, if he puts his hand in the tiger's mouth, it could cause great harm, couldn't it? Seeing this, he became agitated and pretended to strike him with a soft branch. His father pretends to hit him as a punishment. Naga, instead of raising a strong stick to hit his son who put his hand in the tiger's mouth, raises a soft branch to hit him. He doesn't hit him but pretends to. This is due to his immense affection for his son Thinnanar. Seeing this, the child Thinnanar becomes sad, and tears well up in his eyes. Then, the tears fall like pearls. His mother, Tattai, catches these pearl-like tears with her kiss. Kissing the mouth in this way is a stage called "mutthap paruvam" (kissing stage) among the ten stages of childhood. Next, Thinnanar plays. He plays with wooden toys shaped like drums. He rolls small wooden blocks and makes a small chariot to play with. This is the "siru ther paruvam" (small chariot stage) in Pillaitamil literature. Next, the hunter girls build small sand houses and play. Thinnanar plays by kicking these small sand houses with his sacred feet. This is the "sitril paruvam" (small house stage) mentioned in Pillaitamil literature. He plays with the dogs in the hunter settlements. He plays by holding, pulling, and cutting the ropes that tie the dogs. All these described events are from the child's life up to two and a half years of age. Similarly, boys make small drums and beat them. This child also plays by beating such small drums. Thinnanar, the child, grows up learning to walk and playing small games in every hunter's hut. Thus, playing many games, he grew up and now completed five years. He steps into his sixth year. Up to five years, children crawl, walk, and play inside the house, outside the house, and in nearby houses. After five years, when the sixth year begins, children go a little further and then run and play over long distances. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about this in the upcoming verses. Similarly, the child Thinnanar also plays by crossing fences made of elephant tusks. In the outskirts of the village, there will be small forests with sparse trees. Hunter boys, when they first come out of the village, practice dancing and play there. Fierce large animals like tigers and elephants do not live in these small forests. Since this area is adjacent to human settlements, small animals like rabbits and small pigs will be in this small forest area. Our young Thinnanar plays beautifully with the young rabbits, wild boar piglets, tiger cubs, and wild dog pups in this small forest area on the outskirts of the village. Didn't they already keep and raise watch animals like deer, rabbits, pigs, tigers, and wild dogs in the settlement area? So their young ones will be there. They play freely with them in that small forest area. An old hunter woman, a Kurathi, who is very old in the hunter community, has long experience in raising small children. So his parents entrust this boy to her care. The old Kurathi feeds the young Thinnanar in the evening. In the evening, she feeds him food after circling him with mustard smoke. Then, after nightfall, she gives him meat and then lets the boy play for a while before putting him to sleep. Thus, the growing boy Thinnanar reached the age for learning archery. Thus, father Naga was very happy to see his son Thinnanar reach the age of learning archery. Embracing his son Thinnanar with his strong shoulders, he decided to teach him archery and brought an old man skilled in that field. Naga performed penance to have this son. He was happy because he got the child through penance, wasn't he? He is happy to see Thinnanar grow up well. He is also happy to see Thinnanar grow up to be worthy of inheriting his chieftainship of the hunter tribe. Therefore, he expresses his joy by embracing Thinnanar so that their shoulders meet. When it was time for him to learn archery, Naga, the leader of the hunter tribe, also made arrangements for Thinnanar to learn archery. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 12 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 135, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar is growing beautifully. He has reached the age of 12. Therefore, Naga, the hunter king and Thinnanar's father, decides that this is the right time and the right age to teach Thinnanar archery. It is a rule that everyone should learn their respective education at the appropriate age. The age at which one begins to learn varies depending on the skill being taught. Generally, educational training begins around the age of five, which is the tradition. The Chandeasar Puranam, "Aindhu varuda malarkkanaiya," also mentions this. But here, archery is being taught to the Nayanar. Archery is a physical art. Therefore, one must begin this archery training only after having attained the necessary physical development. That is why Naga, his father, decides to give Thinnanar archery training as soon as he reaches 12 years of age. Archery is the best skill for hunters. It is through the strength of this archery that one becomes qualified to lead the hunter tribe. If leadership comes to an unqualified person, they will struggle to bear it and may lose that leadership position. Furthermore, the inner meanings, such as the need to learn arts at the appropriate age, are all mentioned here. To teach Thinnanar archery, Naga appoints an old man who is very skilled and experienced in that field as his teacher. Naga makes this decision to begin teaching Thinnanar archery after consulting with other experienced elder hunters. He also sets an auspicious day. Then Naga, the king of the hunters, sends word to everyone about this news. He sends messengers to everyone, announcing that Thinnanar is going to take up the bow by the grace of Kandavel. To announce major events to the public, governments would beat drums and other instruments, and then announce the news to the public who gathered upon hearing the sound. Here, the hunters announce the news that Thinnanar is going to take up the bow by beating a "thudi" (a type of drum) in the hunter community. This news is also announced to the leaders of the respective communities in the neighboring mountains. Hearing this, all the kings of the hunter tribe ruling in the neighboring mountains, bring their mountain-produced goods such as gems, gold, pearls, tiger skins, elephant tusks, loads of peacock feathers, honey, various kinds of liquor, meat, fruits, and tubers, and come from all directions to Thinnanar's Uduppur. Numerous hunters skilled in archery also come. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings in many verses about how elaborately the ceremony to begin archery training is celebrated here. "Vil pidithal" (holding the bow) is the first practice taught when beginning archery training. Just as in education, one first learns to pronounce letters orally and write them by hand, in archery, one is first taught how to hold the bow. Thus, all the people from the hunter tribe in the neighboring countries arrive at Uduppur for Thinnanar's bow-holding ceremony. A huge crowd gathers, too large to be contained within the small village of Uduppur. All relatives, friendly kings, and people from neighboring countries and mountains come. Naga welcomes everyone and asks them to perform many "paravukkadan" (vows and offerings) to the deity and hold the archery festival. Naga honors all the relatives who came, entrusts them with responsibilities, and asks them to conduct this seven-day festival. The archery festival, from performing "kaappu" (protection ritual) for the student and the bow he will hold, to the act of teaching the student how to hold the bow, is celebrated as a seven-day festival. First, they perform "paravukkadan" to the deity. Here, the deity refers to Lord Muruga, the god of the Kurinji land. So, they perform pujas to Lord Muruga according to their custom, and perform "anangaadal," "veriyaadal," "kundrak kuravai," etc., to fulfill their "paravukkadan." The artisans who teach archery hand the bow to Thinnanar. A special bow was made by skilled experts specifically for Thinnanar's training. Sekkizhar Peruman says that the bow was made "paanmaiyil samaithuk kondu" (made according to its nature). "Paanmai" explicitly means "according to the method" or "according to the rule" of how the bow should be made. "Paanmai" also means "fate" or "destiny." Therefore, we must also see the inner meaning here that the Nayanar receives this bow to begin archery training according to the divine rule of fate. Why does the Nayanar begin archery training here due to fate? Because it is related to the Nayanar's previous birth. We will discuss this in detail at the appropriate time in the latter part of this Purana. Thus, the artisans make the bow according to the prescribed method and give it to Thinnanar's hands. Lord Shiva once held a bow, the Meru mountain bow. He held it to burn the three cities (Thiripuram). The Devas used that bow of Lord Shiva to churn the Milk Ocean as a churning rod. Although it was the Mandara mountain that churned the Milk Ocean, why is it called Meru mountain here? Because Meru mountain is the king of all mountains, so Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Mandara mountain as Meru as a respectful designation. The Devas churned the Milk Ocean using the Meru mountain, the bow held by Lord Shiva. By churning with that bow, instead of nectar, poison came out. Lord Shiva consumed that poison and became Thiruneelakanthar (the blue-throated one). So, Lord Shiva received poison from the bow he held. But when the same bow came into Thinnanar's hands, this bow would bring nectar to Lord Shiva. Because, holding this bow, with the help of its arrow, the Nayanar offers nectar to Lord Shiva in the latter part of the story. So, the Nayanar's bow brought nectar to Lord Shiva. But the Devas gave poison to Lord Shiva through the Meru mountain, the bow held by Lord Shiva. Therefore, the verse implies that this bow is more glorious than that bow. That Meru bow turned nectar into poison. But this bow in the Nayanar's sacred hand helped to turn undesirable, harmful, forbidden meat into nectar and offer it to Lord Shiva. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal composes this verse very artfully. Now, they tie "kaappu" (protection thread) to Thinnanar and the bow in his hand. They tie "kaappu" to both the bow and Thinnanar with the nerve of a single tiger. That is, the "kaappu" tied to the bow was made from the nerve of a certain tiger, and the "kaappu" for Thinnanar was also tied with the nerve of the same tiger. This seems to be a tradition of the hunters. They tie the "kaappu" on the beautiful forearm of Thinnanar, who incarnated to glorify the hunter tribe. All the mountain-dwelling hunters offered their traditional blessings to Thinnanar. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal also sings about the types of food cooked and served to everyone who attended this festival. Rice cooked from mountain paddy, various kinds of millet rice, fine rice from thinai (foxtail millet) found in dense mountains, round rice from bamboo, thus, various kinds of rice made from different grains are cooked by the hunters. Along with this rice, tubers and meat mixed with rice are served there. The cooked food is piled up like a mountain in various places. All the hunters who came, and even the local people, eat this food heartily. Next, he describes what snacks they ate. They eat red millet flour mixed with honey. They eat roasted meat dipped in honey. They mix wood apple with honey, knead it into balls, and eat it. They eagerly eat dead flying ants. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal says they eat various kinds of food. The "senthinai maavu" (red millet flour) mentioned here is the finest millet flour. Just as "sennel" (red paddy) is considered superior among paddy, "senthinai" is superior among millets. They grind this "senthinai" into flour and eat it mixed with honey. Honey is a natural food that provides sweetness among the six tastes. It is abundant in the mountainous regions, which are "kurinji nilam" (mountainous landscape), and is very good for health. Honey is a food item that provides taste, fragrance, and medicinal properties all at once. Therefore, medical texts assert that there is no other food item as beneficial as honey. That is why the hunters here consume honey in various ways. They eat honey mixed with millet flour. Eating meat dipped in honey is considered a superior food among them. This is why, later, the Nayanar also offers meat mixed with honey to the Lord, saying, "Lord, this is a very delicious food." Similarly, they mix honey with wood apple and eat it in balls. We have already seen the properties of wood apple and the wood apple tree. Next, he says they ate flying ants. It is a tradition among mountain dwellers to collect large swarms of flying ants, prepare them like sundal (a dish of boiled legumes), and eat them. It is one of their food items. Flying ants emerge in large swarms from anthills, like "pulapulana kalakalana puttreesal pola" (like swarms of ants from an anthill), and die within a short time. They collect these dead flying ants in a basket, process them, and eat them mixed with honey. This verse states that they eat this with great relish. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal, like a mirror, shows us many aspects of the hunters' food types, their eating habits, food traditions, eating methods, and their enthusiasm for food, among other aspects of their lifestyle. The hunters ate more food than their usual capacity. This is because it was a celebration arranged by their leader with love. So, they ate until their stomachs were full and even more. Not only did the hunters eat food, but there is also a mention that they drank various kinds of liquor. Many kinds of liquor were consumed by them, causing them to become intoxicated and lie scattered here and there. Although these are various types of liquor, they were condemned by scholars. But in those days, in ancient times, it was customary for people of lower status to consume them during celebrations, which we can understand. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 13 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 136, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how the festival for Thinnanar to hold the bow is celebrated. The Eiyittiyar and Eiyinar, meaning the hunter women and hunter men, participate in this festival and bless Thinnanar. Although the archery festival is a ritual for men, women also participate with their partners and bless Thinnanar. From this, we can understand that even among ancient tribal people, there was a tradition of couples coming together to offer blessings. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings by placing women first, then men, i.e., Eiyittiyar, then Eiyinar, indicating the rights given to women by the ancient tribal people. The mountain dwellers wear ornaments made of leather, like a belt, crafted from various gems found in the mountains. This is generally tied around the waist to hold weapons like swords. Since it is made of leather, it is very strong and sturdy. Similarly, they wear garlands made of various green leaves found in the mountains. A large garland made of green leaves would cover the body to some extent. They also wear Vetchi flowers. Vetchi garland is characteristic of the Kurinji's Purathinai (external aspect), so they wear Vetchi flowers. They wear garlands made of Vetchi flowers. Furthermore, they offer Vetchi garlands made of Vetchi flowers to Lord Muruga, praying and blessing that Thinnanar's training may go well. To give training, to start the bow-holding ceremony, the good teacher is there, isn't he? The people gather around that place. They beat small Thondagam drums. They play horns. They sound Udukkai drums. They play flutes. Thus, a great sound filled everywhere. While making these sounds, everyone, dressed in various clothes, goes around the town in a procession. At that time, the hunters perform Varikuthu dance. The hunter women perform Thunangai Kuthu dance. Divine women dance there in a terrifying manner. In the archery festival, the teacher first holds the bow that will be given to the student, celebrates the festival, and at the end, gives it to the student's hand and begins to teach the first skill of archery, how to hold it. Therefore, this ritual begins at noon on the first day. The midday, when the sun is at its zenith, is considered an auspicious time for hunters according to their tradition. Therefore, this ritual begins at midday on the first day of the seven-day archery festival. Midday is also considered a very auspicious time by astrological texts. Astrologers call this "Abhijit Muhurtham." This Abhijit Muhurtham is about 15 Nazhigai (6 hours) from sunrise. It is important everywhere. It is important in every country. It is capable of removing all doshas (malefic influences). We can find this in astrological texts like Kalaprakasikai and Kalavidhanam. The time when "kaappu" was tied to the bow and to Thinnanar, the student, at the beginning of the archery festival, was this Abhijit Muhurtham, the midday. Similarly, the time when Thinnanar was made to hold the bow at the end of the archery festival on the seventh day was also the same time. Not only that, but at the end of this Purana, the time when the Nayanar ascended Thirukalathi mountain and had darshan of Kalathinathar was also this midday. This Abhijit Muhurtham is the time when the full heat of the sun impacts and provides its benefits to living beings, i.e., the time when the sun provides its full benefit. Similarly, astrologers say that the time of sunrise and sunset is called "Gothuli Lagnam" and this Muhurtham also has no doshas. This Gothuli Lagnam (time) is free from any doshas related to Thithi, Varam, Nakshatram, Yogam, and Karanam. It is stated in the astrological text Kalavidhana Paddhati that all actions performed during Gothuli Lagnam will be successful. The fact that the hunters also follow this tradition prevalent in the country, this tradition mentioned in astrological texts, serves as an example of how deeply Vedic culture and Vedic discipline permeated throughout Bharat Desam. All the aforementioned celebrations last for seven days. The seventh-day celebration is twice as grand as the usual celebration. Seven-day festivals are also mentioned in Agamas. The final festival performed on the seventh day is called "Thiruvizha Ganam." The concluding festival is celebrated with twice the grandeur of what happened for the six days. All the people who came bless with their mouths. Auspicious shouts are heard everywhere. The hunters teach Thinnanar to hold the bow by the hand of their ancient, traditional teacher. Antiquity and traditional lineage are very essential for the teaching profession. This is the firm decision of our ancestors. It is not our tradition to consider someone who has no tradition or lineage, but is new, as a great teacher. Our ancestors would not have done so. For isn't the proverb "Kulavithai kallamal paagam padum" (Family skill will be perfected without learning)? Therefore, it is very essential for the teaching profession to be hereditary. With a good teacher from that ancient teaching tradition, they made Thinnanar hold the bow that had been tied with "kaappu" and began his archery training. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Swamigal uses the plural "marabu vazhi vantha aasiyargal" (teachers who came through tradition) here. Therefore, it is implied here that there were many teachers skilled in teaching archery, and Thinnanar touched and held the bow from all of them and received their blessings. The phrase "aasill aasiyan" (flawless teacher) also indicates that among these many teachers, only one excellent and suitable teacher was accepted. Thus, the seven-day festival concluded. Thinnanar's archery training continued daily in a field on the mountain slopes outside Uduppur. Thinnanar, worshipping the bow, the teacher who taught it, and Lord Muruga, the presiding deity, practices archery every day. Thus, Thinnanar learns and masters archery completely. Isn't it a rule to learn any art or any education completely? What does it mean to learn completely? It means to fully acquire the benefits of learning. What is the benefit of learning? Thirukkural says, "Katradhanaal aaya payan enkol vaalarivan natraal thozhaar enin" (What is the use of learning if one does not worship the good feet of the truly wise?). The meaning of this Kural is that the benefit of education is to worship God. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar also learned this art of archery completely. By learning this archery completely, Thinnanar later uses this archery to offer food to God. Furthermore, he uses this bow to guard God at night. Isn't he going to worship the feet of God, the feet of Kudumithevar, using the archery he learned? That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar learned archery completely. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar, who rules him, learned archery completely. "Ennai aalum Thinnanar" (Thinnanar who rules me) is Sekkizhar Peruman's poetic expression. This Thinnanar, who has completely learned and mastered archery, at this point, Sekkizhar Peruman offers a salutation to the Nayanar. After offering salutations, he continues the story. If we divide the Nayanar's story into two parts, one related to worldly attachment and the other to Shiva's attachment, this is where the part related to worldly attachment ends. After this is completed, the Nayanar goes hunting. Then he is about to attain Shiva's attachment. That part begins. Therefore, since this part of the Nayanar's worldly attachment has ended and the part of the Nayanar's Shiva attachment is about to begin, Sekkizhar Swamigal offers a salutation to the Nayanar at this point and continues. It is our Agamic tradition and rule to perform "nyasams" (ritualistic placements) before performing Shiva Puja, i.e., Shiva worship. Because before performing Shiva Puja, we are in worldly attachment. We are engaged in worldly matters. Thus, before performing Shiva Puja, before entering the rituals of Shiva Puja, before beginning to perform the rituals, we purify ourselves with "nyasams" and then enter the Shiva Puja rituals. This is an Agamic rule. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman begins the next part by offering a salutation to the Nayanar, who is now about to attain Shiva's attachment. This is Sekkizhar Peruman's tradition. At this point, where the Nayanar's worldly history ends and the history of his Shiva attachment begins, we must recall the eighth Sutra of Sivagnanabodham, the primary text of all Saiva Siddhanta scriptures, composed by Meykandar Devar. "Aim pula vedarin ayarn thanai valarthu ena tham mudal guruvumaay thavathinil unartha vittu anniyam inmaiyum aran kazhal selume" (Having grown weary among the hunters of the five senses, the Lord, as the primal Guru, reveals through penance that there is no other, and leads to Shiva's feet). This explains how Shiva, as the Guru, takes possession of the soul. A king's son goes missing. That prince grows up in the forest with hunters. The king's son, growing up with hunters, forgets that he is a prince and that his father is the king ruling the country, and lives as a hunter for a long time. The king receives information about his son who had been missing for a long time. So the king wants to make his son realize that he is a prince, that he is not an ordinary hunter, and bring him back to the palace. But the king thinks that if he goes in royal attire, the prince, who has grown up as a hunter, might get scared and run away, and might not even realize the truth. So, how does he go there? He takes the form of a hunter, just like the hunters among whom his son lives. In the form of a hunter, he comes to the forest to search for his son. In the forest, he meets his son, who is a hunter in the hunter tribe. He advises his son, "You are not from the hunter community. You are the rightful ruler of this country. You are my son. Therefore, leave the company of these hunters and return to the palace." He makes him realize the truth. The king reveals that he is the king of this country and has come in the guise of a hunter. The king's son, who had grown up in the hunter community until then, realizes that he is a prince, that his leader is the king, and that his father is the king, and returns to the palace. He returns to the palace with the king. This is also how God takes possession of the soul. If God, to take possession of souls residing in human bodies, were to come in His true form, souls would not be able to recognize God. Therefore, God comes in a human form, just as the perfected soul resides in a human body. The God who comes in this way tells the soul, "You are growing up with the hunters of the five senses: body, mouth, eyes, nose, and ears. Because you have grown up with these five sensory hunters, you have reached this degraded state. You have become confused, not knowing who you are and where you came from." Thus, God comes as a Guru into the world and takes possession of the soul. Therefore, according to this Sutra, the part describing the growth of Thinnanar, the prince who actually grows up in the hunter's settlement, ends here. Now, according to the second part of the eighth Sutra of Sivagnanabodham, "thavathinil unartha vittu anniyam inmaiyin aran kazhal selume" (reveals through penance that there is no other, and leads to Shiva's feet), the story of the Nayanar's attainment of Shiva's attachment begins from here. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 14 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 137, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Archery training begins for Thinnanar, who is 12 years old. It was a seven-day festival. Thinnanar, who began his training, became highly skilled and proficient in archery. Not only archery, but he was also trained in other weapons and became very skilled in them. Thus, Thinnanar gradually grew and reached the age of 16. The moon has 16 phases, but a moon that has grown to 16 phases will then wane phase by phase, and then begin to wax again. But here, Thinnanar gradually grows and reaches the age of 16. However, he is an unwaxing moon. Upon reaching 16 years of age, by the grace of Kudumithevar, he attains the state of liberation, which knows no waxing or waning. Therefore, he is now like a full, undiminished moon of 16 years. The age of 16 is referred to in various ways: "manap paruvam" (marriageable age), "thirumana paruvam" (marriageable age), "kaalai paruvam" (youthful age). Thirugnanasambandar Puranam, song 1109, states "punniya 16 aandu perum pugali vendhar" (the great king of Pugali, of 16 meritorious years). Like Thirugnanasambandar Swamigal, the king of Pugali, Kannappa Nayanar also attained liberation at the age of 16. How does Thinnanar grow? Sekkizhar Swamigal says that he grows like ever-increasing Shiva-punyam (merit from devotion to Shiva). Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar grows very beautifully, like the splendor of boundless Shiva-punyams gathering together and increasing further and further. Shiva-punyam is an imperishable merit. Ordinary worldly merit gives its fruit and then perishes. But Shiva-punyam, the Shiva-punyam we have earned, never perishes. Not only that, but that Shiva-punyam further causes us to perform more Shiva-punyams, and it continues to grow. All other "pasu-punyams" (merits related to worldly existence) give their respective pleasures and then lead to rebirth. But Shiva-punyam leads to liberation, from which one does not return to birth. The Nayanar grows to 16 years of age like such Shiva-punyam. At this same time, Thinnanar's father, Naga, becomes old. Until now, he had protected the people of this region under his rule through his archery and hunting skills. But now, Naga has become very old, so he can no longer protect his subjects by hunting as before. He reaches the stage of physical weakness due to old age. We have already seen in this Purana, haven't we, that Naga and Tattai were childless for a very long time? So everyone who saw them used to say, "Where will they get a child now?" We saw this at the beginning of this Purana. If we assume Naga was about 60 years old then, now Thinnanar is 16 years old. So, at this time, Naga could be around 76 to 80 years old. Nevertheless, until now, he was physically strong, hunting and raiding cattle, protecting the forest. But now he has become very old. At the same time, the menace of wild animals in the forest has increased, meaning Naga can no longer go hunting as before. Going hunting once a month and destroying fierce wild animals was their profession and custom. But now he cannot go hunting. So all the hunters come and complain to Naga. They all come and complain to Naga, their tribal chief, that wild animals are causing trouble because their monthly hunting activities have not taken place. They complain that they are greatly troubled by wild animals like wild boars, tigers, bears, wild cows, deer, and stags coming from the mountainous regions. The wild animals mentioned above come onto the cultivated fields of the hunters and destroy their crops, kill the domestic animals raised by the hunters, and also harm the hunters, causing them various kinds of suffering. Therefore, usually, the leader of the hunter tribe would go hunting once a month to curb the menace of these wild animals. Since this is not happening now, their troubles have greatly increased, they say. Sekkizhar has introduced a subtle point here. Among the wild animals listed by Sekkizhar, the wild boar is mentioned first. Although the boar is smaller than the tiger, the damage caused by the boar to crops is very high. Not only that, in this story, the wild boar leads the Nayanar to Thirukalathi mountain by making him follow it. Sekkizhar Swamigal has sung about this special significance by placing the wild boar first. A king has two indispensable duties. One is to ensure that all his subjects receive food and do not suffer from hunger. This is the first duty. The second duty is to protect his subjects from harm from anyone else. Sekkizhar Peruman, who was the Chief Minister, composed this song with these two duties as its underlying meaning. Naga, having heard all these requests from the hunters, tells them. "Due to old age, I cannot engage in hunting as before. Therefore, you all accept my son as your leader." Old age, when it comes to a person, gradually envelops them, suppressing all their energies. Just as night follows day, and then darkness gradually envelops every moment, so too old age slowly envelops people, making it impossible to pinpoint when it arrived. It suppresses the body's energy moment by moment. Thus, Naga openly states that old age has enveloped him and asks them to accept his son Thinnanar as their leader from now on. Naga was a hereditary ruler. Therefore, Thinnanar was the one who should be the king of those subjects after him. Nevertheless, Naga seeks the approval of the subjects there. Therefore, in ancient times, even if it was a monarchical rule, it happened only with the approval of the subjects in our Bharat Desam. It is surprising to see the same tradition even in the hunter community. Hearing Naga's words, the hunters first feel disturbed and pity Naga. They felt pity for their leader's old age and his consequent removal from leadership. Then, upon realizing that his son, their leader's son, was going to become a superior leader, a new leader for them, they felt joy. Then the hunters say to Naga, "Leader, for all this time, we have lived happily and without harm under your bow's rule. Now, is there any other way for us than to follow the path you have commanded and blessed? Moreover, you have given us Thinnan, who possesses true valor, from your lineage. Therefore, please call your son and bestow upon him your mountain kingdom's right." The hunters said this with joy. When all the hunters agreed, Naga sends for Thinnanar to be brought. As soon as the hunters agreed, he immediately asked for Thinnanar to be brought and made him their leader. Not only that, Naga immediately began making arrangements for Thinnanar's maiden hunt. This is an example of how a good leader should act swiftly upon hearing the grievances of his subjects. Naga says, "After crowning Thinnanar, he must go on his maiden hunt. Before that, sacrifices must be made to all the forest deities. Therefore, make the necessary arrangements." He also says, "Bring the 'Devaratti' (priestess) who comes from our tradition to perform the puja and sacrifices to the forest deities." In their tradition, there was a woman, an elder in the hunter tribe, appointed specifically for this purpose, to worship the forest deities for the well-being of the hunter community. The hunters would provide her with all her needs. This elderly woman, the Devaratti, would propitiate the forest deities, make them descend into her, and, possessed by the divine, would make the forest deities grant the wishes of the hunters through her. The forest deities would similarly possess the elderly Devaratti and grant the boons desired by the hunters. That elderly Devaratti arrives there. She wears a garland made of forest sprouts. She wears earrings made from the horn of a stag. She has a dot on her forehead made of "aridaram" (a type of red pigment) extracted from the stomach of a deer. She wears a necklace of conch beads, colored like a peacock's neck, as an ornament around her neck. The elderly Devaratti, wearing a tree bark garment tied around her waist, adorned with leaves and peacock feathers, slowly arrives there. She offers "akshatai" (rice mixed with flowers) and praises Naga, the leader of the hunters, who is skilled in battle. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings here that she praises him with "akshatai" made of mountain paddy and flowers. Therefore, the tradition of showering "akshatai" also existed in the hunter community. Generally, "akshatai" refers to whole rice grains that are unbroken, uncracked, and not soaked in water. In earlier days, for ceremonies like "kumabhishekam" (consecration), it was customary to collect good paddy from many houses and process it into rice for "akshatai." Even today, many who perform Shiva Puja regularly follow this custom. The blessing given with such unbroken whole rice is believed to last without breaking, which is its principle. Naga asks the elderly Devaratti who has arrived, "Mother, are you well? Do you have any poverty? Are you living well? You are living well, aren't you?" He asks with affection. The Devaratti also blesses Naga and says, "According to the provisions you made in the beginning, I receive all the necessary soft meat, flying ants, honey, mountain produce, and other necessities. Why have you called me?" Naga addresses the elderly woman as "Mother," doesn't he? She is an elderly woman, so he calls her "Mother." Moreover, she is a divine woman who lives by making the forest deities possess her, isn't she? That is why he calls her "Mother." Furthermore, isn't it a custom in our Tamil Nadu to address any woman who is not one's wife as "Mother"? Naga's affectionate inquiry reveals his concern and care that all the people living under his rule are living happily, free from poverty and other sufferings. In response, the Devaratti says, "According to the provisions you made earlier, we receive all the items," doesn't she? It was customary in our country in ancient times for kings to establish grants to ensure that their subordinates lived well and without poverty. Similarly, it is clear from this that Naga has made provisions for the Devaratti and provides her with her necessities. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 15 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 138. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Nagana, the leader of the hunter tribe, who had sent for the priestess, tells her: “I am bestowing the leadership of this tribe, which is my birthright, upon my son, Thinnan. Therefore, from now on, Thinnan will lead this tribe. Therefore, you must worship the deities of the forest and offer them forest sacrifices so that he may excel in hunting more than I do, and achieve victory in conquering foreign lands.” After Nagana, the right to rule would naturally pass to Thinnan. However, there is a tradition that Kannappa Nayanar should receive this leadership after Nagana, even though it would come naturally. Despite this tradition, Nagana voluntarily gives his leadership position to Thinnan. Few kings renounce their sovereignty during their lifetime and crown their heir apparent. This is a characteristic of a king with excellent qualities. Only a few individuals, endowed with excellent qualities and dedicated to their duties, will, in their lifetime, establish a successor and crown them. Nagana, here, is one such person. Furthermore, Nagana tells the priestess: “Worship the deities, wishing that my son may excel in hunting more than I do.” Is it not a Kural that “It is sweet to all living beings on earth when their children are wise?” Nagana is a wise man. He had performed penance in the past to have this son. Therefore, he wished for his son to be superior to himself. Nagana further states: “My son should rule better than I do. He should conquer not only our lands but also foreign lands and rule.” Foreign lands here mean neighboring countries. There is also an inner meaning to this. Nagana engaged in physical hunting. But Thinnanar is about to engage in spiritual hunting. Thinnanar is one who conquers the hunter tribes, which are the five senses, and seizes the supreme bliss that is distinct from them. Therefore, according to Nagana's wish, Thinnanar attained not only this land, that is, this hunter nation, but also the foreign land of supreme bliss, which is liberation. The priestess blesses Thinnanar, saying, "So be it." Nagana asks the priestess to offer forest sacrifices to the deities in the forest, with food items that they desire. Just as a large government has separate officials for each department to run it, there are many minor deities residing in the forest who protect it. Worshipping these deities is not contrary to worshipping the Supreme God. Worshipping these forest deities is also not polytheism. This is because Lord Shiva Himself, according to the level of maturity of each soul, places various mature souls in various positions, and Lord Shiva Himself rules this world with His grace. Therefore, worshipping these minor deities appointed by the Lord is not contrary to worshipping only the Lord. This is a system established to understand and realize the truth that Lord Shiva Himself bestows grace through these minor deities. Therefore, in this hunter tribe, they worship not only Lord Muruga, the Supreme God, but also various forest deities. It is one of the duties of a king who rules a country to ensure that all temples and all worship services are conducted without any shortcomings. Here, it is stated that Nagana worshipped the deities residing in the forest because his son was going on his maiden hunt. Nagana, who thus appointed his son as the next king and asked for the necessary rituals to be performed for his hunt, spoke without any worry. He spoke with joy. Nagana, who had a child after a long time, is now giving the kingship to his son Thinnanar at the appropriate age, isn't he? Therefore, he does this with great joy, free from worry. Then the priestess tells Nagana: "When I came here, I saw very good omens that I had never seen before. Very good signs appeared to me. Therefore, Nagana, your son Thinnan will achieve great victory, not just at your level, but even surpassing you," the priestess blesses. The good omens that appeared to the priestess are called nimittams. Nimittams are of two types: external omens that are visible, and internal omens that appear in our body. The chirping of certain birds is a good external omen. Omens like the twitching of the right eye in our body are internal omens. The priestess says why she saw better omens than she had ever seen before. Until now, Nagana and others had received the darshan of Kalathiyappar. But no one else had excelled like Thinnanar, having received the gracious gaze of Kalathiyappar. Therefore, the appearance of very good omens for Thinnanar, better than those that appeared to anyone else, is the inner meaning of this event. After the priestess blessed him thus and left, Thinnanar, who resembled a sapphire mountain, arrived there. He came holding a bow in his hand, bowed at his father's feet, and rose. Nagana, his father, embraced Thinnanar, fitting his two broad shoulders with Thinnanar's two broad shoulders. Then he seated him beside him on the tiger skin seat where he was sitting. Nagana looked at his son Thinnanar, who was seated before him, and said, looking at his face: "As old age has come upon me, I no longer have the desire to hunt with my own effort as before. Therefore, from now on, you shall undertake the protection of this hunter tribe, hunt, and forever bear the leadership of this hunter tribe, which is your traditional right." He thus blessed Thinnanar and declared him the next leader. As a symbol of this, Nagana immediately gave Thinnanar the waist-cloth and dagger he was wearing. The waist-cloth and dagger are the symbols of the hunters' government. These were borne by Nagana until this day. Now that Thinnanar is taking over the leadership, Nagana gives them to Thinnanar. All these happen immediately. Nagana's inability, his old age, his lack of interest in hunting, and the complaints of the citizens about the cruelty of wild animals, and the need to immediately alleviate the suffering of the citizens, all led to the immediate transfer of leadership. This clearly shows Nagana's concern and affection for his citizens. Thinnanar sees Nagana saying and doing this. He understands the situation. He thinks about his father's condition. He also thinks that their tribe needs a support for its leadership. Therefore, without refusing his father's words, he accepts his father's words, bows at his father's feet, and accepts the dagger and waist-cloth that his father gave him in due course.

Chapter 16 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 139. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, having assumed the leadership of the hunter tribe, sets out for his maiden hunt. Sekkizhar Peruman describes Thinnanar's hunting attire in about seven verses. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Thinnanar's hunting attire from head to toe (Kesadi Padam). A skilled artisan, adept in making hunting attire, comes and adorns Thinnanar in this hunting attire. He straightens Thinnanar's curly hair, removes the curls, and ties it up so that it stands erect and voluminous. On this tied-up hair, he places a garland of tender leaves called Kanni. He adds peacock feathers to it. The artisan, who is making the hunting attire for Thinnanar, also adorns him with a fragrant jasmine garland, Kurinji flowers, and Vetci flowers. He has added peacock feathers to his hair, hasn't he? Below the peacock feather, on Thinnanar's forehead, he places Kundrimani beads and arranges twisted strands of hair to fit, thus decorating him. He places white conch shell earrings in his ears. These earrings, on both sides, resembled a large full moon. Thinnanar wears a necklace of various white beads around his neck. Then he wears a garland of victory called Sannaveeram. Kings wear a Sannaveeram, a garland of victory made of pearl necklaces. These are hunters, so they adorn Thinnanar with a Sannaveeram, a garland of victory made of tiger skin. This Sannaveeram, a garland of victory, is made of tiger skin, and many beads, boar's teeth, tiger's teeth, etc., are embedded in it in various shapes, like the crescent moon, flattened and intricately worked, and then placed on Thinnanar. Furthermore, a small beaded necklace made of elephant tusks hangs down on Thinnanar's chest. Armlets adorn his shoulders. A bracelet shines on his wrist. On his forearm, he wears an iron-like finger guard (kai kothai) to pluck the bowstring. Around his waist, he wears a tiger skin garment adorned with peacock feathers. Around his waist, he wears a long leather garment to carry weapons like a dagger. He wears heroic anklets on his feet. He wears large sandals on his sacred feet. Thus, Thinnanar, in his hunting attire, circumambulates his bow, takes it in his hands, bends it, and stands with the string drawn. Now, he plucks the string of the bow he holds, making a slight sound. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that the sound of the bowstring he produced dispelled all the sorrows of these worlds. Sekkizhar Swamigal vividly portrays this hunting attire of the Nayanar, from head to toe, in a very beautiful poetic style. Why does he describe it in such detail? Because Thinnanar, also known as Kannappa Nayanar, is about to see Kalathiyappar in this hunting attire. It is in this same attire that he will see Kalathiyappar, then become Kannappar, unite with that Lord, and stand eternally on His right side. Furthermore, this very attire is the sacred form that great acharyas like Thirugnanasambandamurthigal and others saw, worshipped, and praised.

That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this sacred form in such detail. We have already seen Sekkizhar Swamigal's detailed description and singing of Sundaramurthy Swamigal's wedding attire. This is because Lord Shiva, who came as an elderly Brahmin, saw Sundaramurthy Swamigal's wedding attire and stopped him, taking him into His service. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal described that wedding attire in great detail. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman will describe in great detail the sacred form of Thirugnanasambanda Peruman on his wedding day, the day he attained liberation by reaching Thirunallur Perumanan. Similarly, he will describe the sacred form of Anaya Nayanar. We will see these in their respective Puranas. Therefore, through these seven verses, from 701 to 712, Sekkizhar Swamigal adorns Thinnanar from head to toe and presents him in the hunting attire with a bow, thus showing us the same sacred form he envisioned. That sacred form is one where the curly hair on the head is tied up, standing erect and voluminous. In it, a garland of tender leaves is placed. Peacock feathers are tucked into that topknot. Above the garland of tender leaves, a jasmine garland, Kurinji flowers, and Vetci flowers are tied. On Thinnanar's forehead, twisted strands of hair are arranged with Kundrimani beads at the base of the peacock feather, thus adorning his sacred form. He wears white conch shell earrings in his ears, shining like a full moon. Around his neck, a necklace of white beads, conch shells, and boar tusks resembling crescent moon fragments are interspersed, forming a flat Sannaveeram made of tiger skin. On his chest, there is a garland of small beads made of elephant tusks, arranged in an orderly fashion. Armlets are on his shoulders, bracelets on his forearms, and a finger guard on his fingers. Around his waist, a tiger skin garment adorned with peacock feathers is worn, and its edges are neatly bordered with many straps. Over it, a waist-cloth is worn. A dagger is tied with the waist-cloth. Heroic anklets are on his feet. Large sandals adorn his sacred feet. Thinnanar took a large bow, strung it, and produced a small twang. This is the sacred hunting attire of Thinnanar. Thinnanar orders the scouts to select and bring various types of arrows and place them in his quiver. He sets out for the hunt, taking his fellow hunters, that is, the tribesmen, with him. Many shouts of blessing are heard there. Small drums are beaten. Many hunters surround Thinnanar, ready for the hunt, and amidst the crowd, Thinnanar shines like a vast forest where blue water lilies bloom together. The priestess arrives there. She offers honey, good meat, liquor, rice, puffed rice, and other such items to the forest deities as forest sacrifices. Then the priestess places Akshathai on Thinnanar's sacred forehead and blesses him, saying, "Your father's father did not receive these benefits. You have received greater benefits than them. Your strength is also very great. This cannot be contained within our limits." She then blesses Thinnanar. After blessing the priestess with great honors, Thinnanar sets out with his bow in hand, shining like a monsoon cloud. This true radiance of Thinnanar, setting out for the hunt, is a radiance born of the intention to protect the hunter-citizens and do good for them, without seeking any personal gain. In a little while, Thinnanar will attain that state where he becomes the very embodiment of true love, clear in his forgotten self, bearing pure love. Just as the sun's light slowly emerges in the darkness before it dissipates, the true radiance of the great love that Thinnanar will later embrace begins to appear slowly at this time when he sets out for the hunt. Along with Thinnanar, a group of hunters, fearless, set out in their appropriate hunting attire. The hunters take their hunting dogs with them. The hunting dogs are chained. The hunters lead the hunting dogs by these chains. Hunting dogs are very aggressive. They harm people and animals. They should be unleashed at the right time under the supervision of their owner, but at other times, they should be kept chained. Hunting dogs should always be chained. Therefore, when hunting, these hunters will control the chained hunting dogs until they chase and catch animals. We have already seen in earlier verses that hunting dogs were chained in the hunters' settlements. Furthermore, hunting dogs have a nature of running against each other, without order. Therefore, these chained hunting dogs in the hands of the hunters also run against each other without order. They go with them. Dogs are not like cows or horses, which live in a group. Dogs do not have the nature of living in a pack. Dogs are referred to by a proverb, "Swajathi Vairi" (enemy of their own kind). Therefore, dogs do not go in an orderly fashion. The tongues of these hunting dogs are very red and hang long. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes the hanging red tongues of these hunting dogs as being like the feet of the goddess of victory. They say there are eight types of Lakshmis, Ashtalakshmi. Among them, the goddess of victory is called Vijayalakshmi. The hunters who set out for the hunt have various types of nets with them. They spread these nets around and inside the forest and catch various types of animals in them. When going for the hunt, horns are blown. Small drums are played. The Pambai drum is beaten. The hunters go with great excitement, making various sounds. If horns, Aakuli, Pambai, and many other sounds are made, surrounding and besieging from all sides, the animals hiding in dens, bushes, and other hiding places will be frightened and run out. Then they will be hunted. This is the hunting tradition. Sekkizhar Swamigal compares the group of hunters setting out for the hunt with Thinnanar to the Kalindi River. The Kalindi River is the Yamuna River. It is a tradition in our country to refer to rivers as maidens. They say that the Yamuna River is black, and the Ganga River is white, like crystal, and when the Yamuna and Ganga meet, the confluence will appear black and white for a long distance. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar connects this appearance, that is, the Yamuna being black and the Ganga being crystalline white, both flowing together, with the Ardhanareeswarar form. The Lord's form, the Lord's color, is like white crystal, a crystalline color. Similarly, the Ganga is on one side. The color of Umaiyammaiyar is green, and that is the color on the left side. The Yamuna River is like the color of Umaiyammaiyar. Therefore, when the Ganga and Yamuna meet,

The appearance of black and white reminds Cheraman Perumal Nayanar of the Ardhanareeswarar form of the Lord. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that the group of hunters going for the hunt is like the flood of water in the Kalindi, also known as the Yamuna River. The black color of the hunters is compared to the black color of the Yamuna River, and the waves rising in the Yamuna River are compared to the rows of bows held by the hunters. The greatness of the Yamuna River is compared to the large stature of the hunters. Even in hunting, there are scouts. These scouts go ahead, find out the movements of animals, and report them to the hunters. They report that deer, boars, antelopes, and various other animal herds are abundant and close by in the mountain forests to the south. Therefore, the hunters, led by Thinnanar, go to hunt in the mountain forest to the south. Here, the mountain forest to the south, as reported by the scouts, can be understood as the forest area near and surrounding Kalathi Hill. This is because to the north of Kalathi Hill is Pothappi Nadu. Uduppur is a village in Pothappi Nadu. Thinnanar and his group of hunters set out from Uduppur for hunting. To their south is Kalathi Hill. Thinnanar hunts for many leagues towards this southern direction. Therefore, we can understand that what the scouts reported now is almost Kalathi Hill and the surrounding forests. Upon approaching the forest for hunting, the actions that the hunters, who are the advance party, perform before hunting include circling the forest, entering it from various directions, scouting, and setting up nets around the forest for direct guarding. Then, the group of hunters, led by Thinnanar, entered the forest, made various noises, and made all the hidden animals come out. Wild boars, deer, bears, strong herds of antelopes, wild buffaloes, elephants, tigers, and wild antelopes, many animals, rose up in anger, roared, sprang forward, and ran, leaping here and there. The group of hunters shot arrows at them. In these verses, Sekkizhar Peruman describes how the hunt takes place with beautiful rhythm and poetic grace. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 17 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 140. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how the hunting takes place in 11 verses, from verse 717 to 727. These verses are composed in a rhythmic style suitable for hunting, with a fast-paced sound. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal has beautifully composed the description of how the hunting takes place, from verse 728 to verse 740, with a beautiful rhythmic grace. Let's look at some of these rhythmically composed verses: "Thaal aruvana vidai thuniyana thalai thumivana kalai maavaligalodu kudal sorithara marivana sila marai maaneeludal vidu saramuruvida nimirvana midai kadamaa meeligal kanai padumudalazha vizhuvana pala uzhaiye venkanai padu pidarkizhipada visai uruviya kayavaai senganal pada vathanodu kanai seriyumuniru karumaa vengalasiramuruvia pozhuthadalaiyirura vathanai pongiya sinamodugavarvana puraivana sila puligal." These verses are composed with rhythmic grace. Now let's look at their meaning. Let's look at the meaning of these verses. Deer are hunted. Some of the deer die with arrows piercing their bodies. Kadama animals are hunted. Large boars are also hunted. Tigers are hunted. In some places, the hunters shoot arrows from behind the animals and hunt them. These are hunters who hide behind trees. Therefore, they attack from behind. This is not righteous. Even if it is unrighteous, the hunters do not know about unrighteousness, do they? So, some of them shoot arrows from behind in this manner. But our Thinnanar only hunts animals that come in front of him. He hunts animals from in front of them. Other hunters hunt small rabbits and deer. Our Thinnanar hunts elephants, tigers, and lions. Here, Thinnanar is engaging in a heroic battle. Now Sekkizhar sings a beautiful scene. The hunters have spread nets in those areas for hunting. Due to the sound they made, deer get caught in those nets. Then, thinking that they are caught in the nets, the deer try to escape. In front of the deer trying to escape, the hunters' hunting dogs stand. The hunting dogs stand in front of the deer, preventing them from escaping. This is the scene described by Sekkizhar Swamigal. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings a beautiful metaphorical comparison with a profound spiritual meaning for this. Just like the deer caught in the net, we, as souls, are caught in the nets of good and bad karma. Thinking that we are caught in these karmas, souls wish to escape from these nets of karma. Thus, wishing to escape, the souls' hearts seek to attain true knowledge. This is the state where the deer try to escape and survive. But there, the hunting dogs stand in front of the deer and do not let them escape, don't they? Similarly, when souls seek true knowledge to be freed from these two karmas, the five senses, called jnanendriyas and karmendriyas, stand in their way, opposing them. This is the metaphor shown by Sekkizhar Peruman. It is because Sekkizhar Swamigal sings metaphors with spiritual meaning even in a hunting profession that he earned the title "Deivasekkizhar" (Divine Sekkizhar). Good and bad karma, called hitam and ahitam, are the two karmas that subject living beings to birth and death, causing them to be born again and again in this world. Bad karma leads to sin and causes the suffering of hell. Similarly, good karma leads to merit and gives pleasures like heaven. Therefore, the merit resulting from good karma and the sin resulting from bad karma give birth again and again to living beings. As a result, living beings are born again and again, die again and again, and are caught in this cycle. This is what he compares to the deer caught in the net and suffering, isn't it? The deer are caught in the net and suffer, unable to get out of it. Souls, on the other hand, suffer in the cycle of birth and death again and again due to good and bad karma, unable to escape from this cycle. Merit is a chain made of gold that binds us. Sin is a chain made of iron that binds us. Therefore, both merit and sin bind us. This is what Manikkavasagar Peruman says: "Bound by the strong ropes of righteousness and sin." That is why Thiruvalluvar calls these two karmas "irulser vinai" (dark karmas) in Thirukkural. "Irulser iruvinaiyum sera Iraivan porulser pugazhpurindhar maattu" is the fifth Kural of the chapter "God's Praise." Even yagas and other rituals yield only perishable benefits (kamya). They do not give spiritual knowledge. Therefore, merit not only fails to give knowledge but also acts as a hindrance to the occurrence of knowledge, just like bad karma, that is, sin. Thus, if a soul wants to be freed from the cycle of good and bad karma, that is, merit and sin, it must attain the state of "iruvinai oppu" (equanimity towards two karmas). Iruvinai oppu occurs in souls only through the grace of God, through penances like chariyai and kiriyai. Thus, souls suffer like deer caught in the net of good and bad karma. The deer wishing to be freed from that net is a metaphor for the souls' attempt to attain true knowledge and escape from these nets of karma. But there, dogs stop the deer. Here, the karmendriyas and jnanendriyas of the souls, who wish to attain true knowledge, stop them. Therefore, our five senses are compared to dogs here. Let's see why the senses are compared to dogs here. Dogs keep biting a dry bone. This causes a wound in the dog's mouth. As the dog continues to bite the bone with this wound, blood oozes from the wound. The dog tastes this oozing blood and foolishly thinks it is coming from the bone, and bites the bone more and more. This causes more and more blood to ooze from the wound in the dog's mouth. It keeps tasting it more and more. It even considers it very joyful. While there are many good things to eat, the dog eats feces, which are not edible. It also eats dirt. Furthermore, the dog eats its own vomited food again. Just like this dog, living beings experience the harms that come from the five senses. They mistakenly consider these sufferings that come from the five senses as pleasures and experience them with joy. Furthermore, while there is the experience of Shiva, which is a good experience, they do not experience it, but instead repeatedly experience worldly pleasures and wallow in them. They repeatedly experience the same dirty experiences they have already experienced. "Undathe undu, udutthathae uduthu, adutthaduthu uraithathe uraithum, kandathe kandum, kettathe kettum kazhinthana kadavul naalellam" is a verse by Pattinathadigal. Therefore, this verse by Pattinathadigal states that souls waste all their days by eating what they have already eaten through the five senses, wearing what they have already worn, speaking what they have already spoken, seeing what they have already seen again, and hearing what they have already heard and are accustomed to. Based on these ideas, elders compare themselves to dogs, saying "nayen" (I, the dog) and "lower than a dog." Even in a hunting forest filled with blood and killing, finding and showing such spiritual truths is the nature of Sekkizhar Peruman's divinely fragrant poetry. Similarly, in Periyapuranam, every verse is full of devotion and spiritual meanings. Let's continue to reflect on the verses. We saw earlier that some hunters shot arrows from behind the deer. There are some such hunters. But generally, Sekkizhar Peruman says that hunters hunted according to righteous principles. As mentioned above, those who hunt unrighteously are a minority. But generally, here, Sekkizhar Peruman explains that hunters hunt according to righteous principles. Generally, the method of warfare in our Bharata Desam is based on dharma. Before starting a war, they would announce to cows, Brahmins, ascetics, children, the sick, women, childless people, and those who do not fight against them to go to a safe place. After making such announcements and securing these categories of people in safe places, war would take place according to our tradition. But in Western countries and other countries, there is no such justice at all. For the sake of food, out of greed for wealth, and out of a frenzy to conquer countries, they mercilessly slaughter innocent civilians, just as sheep and cattle are slaughtered in slaughterhouses. We still see thousands of civilians suffering from such wars. By the great compassion of our teacher Sekkizhar Peruman, for the welfare of the world, these principles exemplified here should be followed by our people not only in war but in all fields. Not only in war, but in all fields, people should not use their strength to seize others' property or to gain benefits. They should act according to righteous principles, without deviating from justice. Hunters would not hunt female animals during their hunt. They would not hunt young animals. They would not harm pregnant female animals that walk with difficulty. Thus, these hunters followed righteous principles even in hunting. Generally, hunters are described as having a very harsh appearance and a very harsh mind, using words like "kill," "throw," and "stab." They steal the belongings of travelers passing through their forests. These cruel hunters are uneducated. Even they followed a principle in hunting. They hunted based on a dharma. But in this era, which is said to be very civilized, is it not the cruellest of cruelties that those who are called educated and civilized cause suffering to this world without any justice in their professions? Now, even in the cruel-minded hunters, the mind that wants to hunt according to dharma is the grace of God. God is present everywhere, as the life of all living beings. Therefore, He is found even within these cruel hunters, and this hunting with dharma is an event that manifested without their knowledge. Moreover, the hunters who go for hunting do not completely destroy all animals. Their leaving female animals, pregnant animals, and young animals unhunted is also based on the good intention that these animal species should not be completely wiped out. As the hunting was going on in the forest, a large wild boar came running. The wild boar, making a loud thunderous sound like a huge black cloud, shaking the entire forest, even frightening elephants, broke through all the nets, and ran with great speed, emitting fire from its eyes. While the other hunters were busy with their hunting, unaware of this, only Thinnanar ran after the wild boar. Two hunter friends, accompanying Thinnanar, ran with him. Thinnanar is chasing that large wild boar, isn't he? This becomes the turning point in Thinnanar's story. It runs as if the same boar that caused a fight between God and Thinnanar in Thinnanar's previous birth has now appeared in front of Thinnanar in this birth and is running. Thinnanar also follows it very quickly. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 18 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 141. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. A very large wild boar, breaking through all the nets, ran very fast, and Thinnanar ran after it. Following Thinnanar, two hunters, Nanan and Kadan, ran with him as companions. The boar, which had run for many leagues, stopped among a cluster of trees at the foot of a small hill. Thinnanar, who had already known that the boar, having run a long distance, would stop at such a place, followed it quickly and stood in front of it. Generally, animals that sense danger to their lives, out of fear, try to escape and run away. But this boar did not run away in fear; instead, it stood its ground. Therefore, Thinnanar, instead of trying to kill the boar with his arrows from a distance, stood in front of it, as if to engage in a heroic hand-to-hand combat. The boar also stood ready to pounce on him. Therefore, Thinnanar, standing in front of the boar as if to engage in a wrestling match, drew his sword in front of it and stabbed it so that its body was cut in two. The hunter chief, Thinnanar, stabbed it so quickly that the boar fell to the ground in two pieces. Nanan saw this and told Kadan, who was with him, in wonder. Nanan was one of Thinnanar's inseparable bodyguards, and Kadan was another. But here, Nanan was very close to Thinnanar and served him intimately. He spoke to Kadan in wonder, seeing Thinnanar's bravery and skill. Then Nanan and Kadan told Thinnanar: "We are very hungry and thirsty from running for such a long distance. So, let's roast this boar. You also eat a little. We will eat this to satisfy our hunger and drink some water, and then we will continue." Both of them said this. They could not find high-quality meat like deer meat or honey to give to their chief Thinnanar. But this boar, which is of the lowest quality among meats, is what is available now. So, without any other option, they both said they would prepare and eat the boar's meat. Hearing Nanan and Kadan say this, Thinnanar asked: "Is there even water in this forest? Where is the water in this forest?" This is because they had run a long distance chasing this boar, hadn't they? While running, Thinnanar had observed all the forest areas. He had not seen any springs or water bodies along the way. That is why he asked this question. He asked where there was water in the dense forest. Nanan answered Thinnanar's question. Nanan said: "Don't you see a teak forest nearby? If you go past that teak forest, the Ponmugali River flows beside a long hill." Although Thinnanar generally asked the question, Nanan answered. Among Nanan and Kadan, Nanan was the more knowledgeable and experienced one. Nanan was the one who nurtured Thinnanar's love and interest in Kalathi Hill, saying that if he went to Kalathi Hill, he would see a good sight. Nanan was Thinnanar's guide until Thinnanar climbed Kalathi Hill and saw Kudumithevar. When he climbed to the top of Kalathi Hill, Thinnanar heard the sound of divine drums and asked Nanan what that sound was. Nanan replied that it was the sound made by bees circling the flowers around the hill. Nanan also explained to Thinnanar that the flowers and water found on Kudumithevar's sacred body were the result of a Brahmin's worship. Therefore, Nanan was responsible for the worship that Thinnanar later performed for the Lord.

Not only that, but Nanan was also a strong and trusted companion to Thinnanar's father, Nagana, in hunting, and he had come to Kalathi Hill with Nagana. It is this Nanan who now introduces the Ponmugali River and Kalathi Hill to Thinnanar. He received that great fortune. He says that if you cross this large teak forest, there is a mountain there, a continuation of long hills. Generally, the Eastern Ghats, unlike the Western Ghats, are not continuous, high, and dense, but rather a series of disconnected hills. Kalathi Hill forest is one such hilly region in the Eastern Ghats. He says that there is one such hill in a long line of hills. He also says that the Ponmugali River flows beside that hill. Nanan says that the Ponmugali River is there, implying that it is cool and capable of relieving our fatigue, hunger, and thirst. Not only does drinking the cool water of the Ponmugali River bring coolness, but there is also the meaning that merely thinking of or speaking of the Ponmugali River brings coolness to us. This river, Ponmugali, is called Suvarnamukhi in Sanskrit. It has been referred to as Ponmugali in the Thevarams. Mugaram means light, and Mugari means radiant. Since the water in this river flows making sounds, it is called Ponmugali with the suffix Mugari. Since 'la' and 'ra' are interchangeable, it can also be called Ponmugari or Ponmugali. In the Thevarams, it is referred to as Ponmugali River. We have already reflected on the sacred properties of the Ponmugali River in the Thirukalathi Puranam. Thus, Nanan speaks about the Ponmugali River and Kalathi Hill. Hearing this, Thinnanar says, "Let's go there. Bring that boar." As he said, he goes towards the Ponmugali River. After going half a league, he reaches the grove on the slopes of Thirukalathi Hill, where Lord Shiva resides. The verse says, "He himself went towards it, and after half a league, he saw the grove on the slopes of the sacred mountain where the Lord with the red eye and bull resides." Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Kalathi Hill as Thirumalai (sacred mountain). In Periyapuranam, we first saw the section called Thirumalai Sarukkam. There, Thirumalai referred to Thirukailaya Malai (Mount Kailash). This Kalathi Hill is also called Thenkailai (Southern Kailash), isn't it? Therefore, Thirumalai here refers to Kalathi Hill. The Anthadi composed by Nakkiradevar, "Kailai Paathi Kalathi Paathi," is also a work based on this idea. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, after seeing Kalathi Hill here, began his pilgrimage to Thirukailayam with a desire to see the magnificent form of the Lord residing in Thirukailayam. Thirugnanasambanda Peruman and Sundaramurthy Swamigal also sang about Thirukailaya Malai from here. Therefore, just as Thirukailaya Malai is called Thirumalai, Thirukalathi Malai is also called Thirumalai. After going half a league, Thinnanar sees the sight of Thirukalathi Hill for the first time. As soon as he saw that sight, all of Thinnanar's hunger, thirst, and fatigue vanished. Thirukalathi Hill captivated his heart. Thinnanar immediately tells Nanan, looking at him, "Nanan, let's go to that hill that appears in front." Thinnanar says, "Nanan, let's go to this hill that appears in front." Then Nanan tells Thinnanar: "If you go there, Thinnanar, you will see a good sight. On this high and sacred Kalathi Hill, Kudumithevar, who makes everything righteous and without crookedness, resides. We can worship that Kudumithevar," he said. This is a verse composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Nanan says: "Thinnanar, if you go there, you will see a good sight. On this high and sacred Kalathi Hill, Kudumithevar, who makes everything righteous and without crookedness, resides. We can worship that Kudumithevar," he said. Those who go to such sacred mountains and sacred places generally travel there. They go and come back. They go and come back without seeing all the special features there. But here Nanan says: "If you go to that Kalathi Hill, you will see a special sight." He says, "You will see a good sight." This is because the sight that Thinnanar will see will not be an ordinary sight like that of others. The sight that Thinnanar will see is a sight that the Lord shows him, having received the gracious gaze of the Lord. That is why it is called a good sight in the verse here. What is a good sight? It is seeing the sight that scriptures say one should see with internal and external instruments, and that elders say one should see. Thirunavukkarasar Swamigal sings in the Thiruangamalai, a Thiruppathikam, "Kankal kanminkalo kadal nanjunda kandan thannai en thol veesi nindraadum piran thannai kankal kanminkalo" (Oh eyes, behold the Lord who drank the poison of the ocean, the Lord who dances, swinging my shoulders, oh eyes, behold Him!). Thirunavukkarasar Swamigal sings that we should see only Lord Shiva with our eyes. Such a sight is called a good sight. Nanan had previously said that if you cross the teak forest, you can see the hill. That was something the Nayanar knew only by hearing. Now, the Nayanar directly sees Thirukalathi Hill with his sacred eyes. Furthermore, Nanan says that Kudumithevar is at the top of this hill, and we can worship him. Kudumithevar is the deity who sprouted from the hill, rooted in it. He is the one who removes all faults from souls. The name of the Lord, the Sivalinga Peruman, at the top of Thirukalathi Hill is Kudumithevar. Kudumi means the top. He is the one who resides at the top of the hill, the Kudumi. Therefore, he is named Kudumithevar. The hunters have worshipped Thirukalathiappar, the Lord of Thirukalathi, by the name Kudumithevar. Does Kudumithevar reside only at the top of this hill? Is he not also at the pinnacle of Vedanta, which is praised as the pinnacle of the Vedas? Is he not the essence of the Vedantas, called Veda Siras? At the end, that is, at the pinnacle, of the four Vedas stands the Atharvana Veda. Its head is Atharva Siras, and its crest is Atharva Sigai. What does that Atharva Sigai say? It ultimately states that one should abandon everything and meditate only on Shiva, who is auspicious. Shiva alone is to be meditated upon. Therefore, Lord Shiva is the ultimate reality that shines at that pinnacle, at the culmination of the Vedas and Vedantas. He is the one who shines here at the top of Kalathi Hill, bearing the name Kudumithevar. Therefore, the sight of Kudumithevar at Kalathi Hill is the vision of knowledge that shines at the pinnacle of the Upanishads. After seeing Thirukalathi Hill, Thinnanar says, looking at Nanan, "What is happening to me now? As I see this hill and go towards it, I feel as if all my burdens are decreasing. My desire swells, and my desire for this Thirukalathi Hill increases more and more, my eagerness grows. I have no other desire in my mind. My mind only thinks of going quickly towards Thirukalathi Hill. No other thought arises in my mind. Where is Kudumithevar's abode on this hill? Take me there quickly."

Thinnanar asks Nanan. With every step Thinnanar takes towards Thirukalathi Hill, he experiences a feeling as if his burden is decreasing. The desire to go to that Thirukalathi Hill swells. That desire rises and swells more and more, without subsiding. His mind goes to the hill much faster than his body. Thinnanar has no other desire or wish except to see that Thirukalathi Hill. This desire and eagerness come to Thinnanar because of the Shiva penance he performed in all his previous births, as a result of Shiva's merit, he now has such an experience. A similar passage also appears in the story of Chandeesa Nayanar. Sekkizhar states that Chandeesa Nayanar's Shiva worship occurs as a continuation of his previous knowledge. The Thirukkural, "One's learning in one birth is a safeguard for seven births," is also relevant here for our reflection. Thus, due to the continuation of his previous birth, Thinnanar's desire to see this Thirukalathi Hill swells more and more. The desire to go up the Thirukalathi Hill that he saw and see Kudumithevar also swells more and more. As he goes towards that Thirukalathi Hill, he feels as if all his burdens are decreasing. He has an experience. The burdens of souls are the 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas, totaling 96 entities. Until the grace of God falls upon the soul, the soul clings to these 96 entities, believing them to be pleasure, and remains bound within them, entangled in many births. But in reality, these 96 entities are a burden to the soul. However, the soul will wander through births, believing these 96 to be pleasure, not realizing they are a burden. This continues until the grace of God falls upon that soul. After the soul has matured and the grace of God has fallen upon it, the soul begins to perceive these 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas, these 96 entities, as distinct from itself. It begins to realize that it is separate from these 96 Tattvas. As the grace of God falls more and more upon the soul, the soul begins to realize that these 96 Tattvas are a burden to it. As the grace of God falls even more deeply upon the soul, these 96 Tattvas, these 96 burdens, begin to detach from the soul one by one. They fall away. It is then that the soul's burden, its true burden, begins to decrease. This experience, where the 96 Tattvas detach one by one, is what Thinnanar experiences now as he goes towards this Thirukalathi Hill. As these 96 Tattvas detach one by one, the soul experiences joy. Furthermore, as this burden decreases, the desire to attain God increases greatly.
