Until then, the soul, standing united with ego and devoid of knowledge, will become disgusted with ego, despise it, and then strive to unite with true knowledge. Sekkizhar Peruman explains this state in the verses that follow. Appar Peruman has also composed a Thevaram to the effect that if living beings, lamenting that these 96 Tattvas cause them suffering, develop love for God, God Himself, being a lover, helps those souls. This is the fourth Thirumurai, Thiruvathikai Veerattanam Thiruppathikam, Pathikam number 25, verse number 4. The verse is as follows: "Thuruththayam kurambai thannil thonnootru angaruvarnindru viruththi than tharuga endru vedanai palavum seyya varuthiyall vallavaaru vanthu vanthadaiya nindra aruthiyarkku anbar polum athigai veerattanare." In that verse, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman also states that 96 entities cause suffering to souls. Saiva Siddhanta Gnana Shastras also state the same. Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed this experience of the 96 Tattvas slowly detaching one by one, and that burden falling away one by one, in this Thiruppuranam of Kannappa Nayanar, as the 97th verse, after 96 verses. This arrangement is also a divine arrangement. It is also a historical fact that Manikkavasaga Swamigal attained a similar state when he reached Thirupperunthurai. Among those 96, there are 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas. Sekkizhar sings that when Kannappa Nayanar began to climb Thirukalathi Hill, he climbed and crossed the 36 Tattvas as steps. Therefore, the feeling of Thinnanar's burden decreasing here suggests that it is the 60 Tatvikas among those 96 Tattvas. We can list only the names of these 96 Tatvikas here. Then, when we see Kannappa Nayanar climbing Thirukalathi Hill, crossing 36 Tattvas, we can see the summaries of the 36 Tattvas there. Now, we can see only the names of the 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas here. First, the 36 Tattvas. God creates these 36 Tattvas from Maya. These 36 are divided into three: Suddha Maya, Asuddha Maya, and Prakriti Maya. In Suddha Maya, there are five Tattvas; in Asuddha Maya, there are seven Tattvas; in Prakriti Maya, there are 24 Tattvas. First, the five Tattvas in Suddha Maya: Shiva Tattva, Shakti Tattva, Sadashiva Tattva, Ishvara Tattva, Suddha Vidya Tattva. Next, the seven Tattvas in Asuddha Maya: Six Asuddha Maya Tattva, Seven Kala Tattva, Eight Niyati Tattva, Nine Kala, Ten Vidya, Eleven Aragam, Twelve Purusha. Next, the 24 Tattvas of Prakriti Maya. These are: 13 Moolappakuthi or Moola Prakriti, 14 Buddhi, 15 Ahankaram, 16 Manam, 17 Sevi (ear), 18 Thol (skin), 19 Kan (eye), 20 Naakku (tongue), 21 Mookku (nose), 22 Vaai (mouth), 23 Kaal (leg), 24 Kai (hand), 25 Payu, 26 Upastham. 27 Sabdam (sound), 28 Sparsam (touch), 29 Roopam (form), 30 Rasam (taste), 31 Gandham (smell), 32 Aakaayam (ether), 33 Vaayu (air), 34 Theyu (fire), 35 Neer (water), 36 Prithvi (earth). These are the 36 Tattvas. The external instruments that arose from these 36 Tattvas are called Tatvikas. There are 60 of these Tatvikas. They are as follows. Among the 60 Tatvikas, the five components of Prithvi are: hair, bone, skin, nerve, muscle. The five components of Appu (water) are: flowing water, blood, semen, brain, marrow. The five components of fire are: food, sleep, fear, sex, laziness. The five components of Vayu (air) are: running, sitting, lying down, walking, standing. The five components of Aakaayam (ether) are: lust, anger, greed, delusion, pride. The five functions of the five Karmendriyas are: speech, movement, giving, excretion, joy. The ten Vayus (airs) arising from Vayu are: Pranan, Apanan, Udanan, Vyanan, Samanan, Nagan, Kurman, Krikaran, Devadattan, Dhananjayan. The ten Nadis (channels) arising from Prithvi are: Idai, Pingalai, Suzhumunai, Gandhari, Athi, Singuvai, Alambudai, Purudan, Kugu, Sangini. The five arising from Aakaayam are: Muthirasayam, Malasayam, Amasayam, Pakkuvasayam, Karppasayam. Next are the impure expanded worlds of Sabda, Sparsa, Rupa, Rasa, Gandha. All these together make 60. These are the Tatvikas. Therefore, there are 60 Tatvikas. The causal Tattvas for these are 36, making a total of

It will mention the six impermanent Tattvas. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 19 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 142, Ilaimazhindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, Nanan, and Kadan reach the banks of the Ponmukali River. This Ponmukali River, with the water it carries, beautifully flows, gathering bamboo pearls, agarwood, sandalwood, other gems from the mountains, gold dust, diamonds, and other priceless items with its sand dunes and waves. It is stated in Kailai Paathi Kaalathi Paathi Anthathi that if one bathes in the Ponmukali River, bowing to Kalathinathar, all evil deeds, whether done unknowingly or knowingly, will be removed. As stated, for those who come and bathe in it to perform Shiva Puja, and for those who come and bathe in it to attain liberation through Shiva Puja, this Ponmukali River collects all the necessary items for Shiva Puja, such as agarwood for incense, sandalwood for the Lord's anointing, and gems and gold to be placed in the Lord's holy water and arghya water. It is said in the Ponmukali Sarukkam of the Thirukalathi Puranam that those who bathe in it will attain liberation. This Ponmukali River is capable of bestowing worldly benefits like gold, pearls, and gems, and the ultimate benefit of liberation, to those who come and bathe in it. They reached the banks of this Ponmukali River. There, under the shade of a tree, Nanan and Kadan placed the pig they had carried. Thinnanar then said to Kadan, "Make fire from wood by friction. We will climb this mountain and see it." Hunters know that dry wood, when rubbed, produces fire, as the fire element is abundant in it. They have also used it. Such wood used to produce fire is called Arani wood. Arani is a Sanskrit name. Is it not a philosophical truth that fire is hidden in wood and will emerge if rubbed quickly? Therefore, hunters also produce fire by rubbing these woods, which have a high fire content, when they need it. "Fire is in the firewood" is from Appar Swamigal's Thevaram. Thus, after telling Kadan to make fire and prepare the pig, Thinnanar and Nanan reached the Ponmukali River. They descended into the cool, sacred waters of the Ponmukali River and bathed. As a result, their minds became clear. Thinnanar felt more and more joy. Generally, it is believed that bathing in cool river water clears the mind and removes confusion. This is the sacred Ponmukali River, obtained through penance by the holy Agastya. Therefore, it removed Thinnanar's worldly attachments, such as his lineage, and brought him the clarity of Shiva-gnana-ananda. Kannappa Nayanar attained liberation in the month of Thai, under the Mrigasirisha star. So, this event takes place in the month of Thai. We can understand that clear water flows in the Ponmukali River during the month of Thai. Thus, Thinnanar and Nanan crossed the Ponmukali River and ascended the other bank. Thinnanar looked only at the Kalathi mountain, not at anything else. Great joy arose in his heart. At that time, the sun reached its zenith. It was high noon. They were at the foot of the Thirukalathi mountain, on the other side of the river. At that moment, divine drums (Deva Thundubhi) resounded on top of the Thirukalathi mountain. The sound of the five divine drums resounded there like the roar of the ocean. Why was it so?

Why were the five sounds of the divine musical instrument, Deva Thundubhi, heard at noon on the mountain? Because the Devas come to this mountain and worship the Lord at noon, sounding the Deva Thundubhis. This Thirukalathi mountain is the Southern Kailash. Therefore, like Kailash, Brahma and other Devas come here to worship. Hearing the sound of these Deva Thundubhis, Thinnanar asked Nanan, "What is this, Nana?" This is an expression that the worldly realm is leaving Thinnanar, and the realm of liberation is welcoming him. Thinnanar asks, "What is this, Nana?" These words are the last words Thinnanar spoke in connection with the world. After some time, by the grace of the Lord's gaze, he became the embodiment of love and lost himself. With that, Thinnanar's conversation with the world ceased. His talk about the world ceased. His talk about worldly connections ceased. Therefore, these very words, "What is this, Nana?", were Thinnanar's last words spoken with a sense of worldly connection. However, Nanan was not virtuous enough to hear the sound of the Deva Thundubhis on this mountain. He did not hear any of those divine drum sounds. Therefore, Nanan replied, "There are many flowers on this mountain. It might be the sound of bees buzzing around those flowers." Nanan also spoke with doubt. Only Thinnanar, who was at the pinnacle of great Shiva Punya, could hear the sound of the Deva Thundubhis. But Nanan's reply, unknowingly, further propelled Thinnanar towards the Lord. This becomes Nanan's third instructive word. The Ponmukali River is there. One can bathe there. One can drink water, he said about the river, which was the first instruction. The Thirukalathi mountain is there. You will get a good view there. Kudumit Thevar is there, Nanan said, which was the second instructive word. And now, Nanan's statement that bees are eating honey around the flowers on the mountaintop becomes the third instructive word. Although Nanan generally and very casually said that it was the sound of bees buzzing for honey, it created a desire in Thinnanar to go up and see Kudumit Thevar. Therefore, Shivakavimani says that this too is an instructive word, revealing its inner meaning. Nanan said that bees surrounding the honey in the flowers are making sounds, didn't he? The Lord, who is the great form of bliss enshrined in this mountain, is the great honey. The bees that buzz around this honey are the mature souls whose minds seek and approach the Lord. The sound of the buzzing bees is the sound of praise sung by the mature souls who praise the Lord. It is the tradition of great people to praise the Lord as great honey. "Shall we pluck the flower-creeper, praising the golden pot where the honey of bliss resided in Thiru Idaimaruthu?" is from Thiruvachagam, Thiruppoovalli. "Having drunk the great honey from the glorious Idaimaruthu" is the third verse of Thiruvidaimaruthur Mummanikkovai. In many such sacred verses, great people have sung of the Lord as great honey. Therefore, great people find the inner meaning in Nanan's simple reply, that it is the sound of praise sung by mature souls, like bees, surrounding the great honey that is the Lord. The next three verses are about Kannappa Nayanar climbing the mountain and seeing Kalathiappar, Kudumit Thevar. These are verses 102, 103, and 104, which describe until he sees Kalathi Thevar. Let's look at the verses and then reflect on their deep meanings. Thinnanar followed Nanan, climbing the Kalathi mountain. Now, the verses. The accumulation of past penance,

To demonstrate endless blissful love, boundless enthusiasm surged, and great love for the earth was expressed. Towards the mountain of the generous Lord, with bones melting and a great yearning rising in his heart, Nanan and Anbu ascended the cool mountain first. As they ascended the tall mountain, like those who climb the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas to reach the Lord, who is Shiva, the Lord, before they could see the Lord with the moon-adorned matted hair, the compassionate gaze of the Lord of beautiful eyes fell upon them. The old attachments of birth, which had lingered, left and moved away. He became the embodiment of incomparable love, in the shade of surging light. These are verses 102, 103, and 104. Now, let's reflect on the meaning of each verse. Verse 102: "The accumulation of past penance, to demonstrate endless blissful love, boundless enthusiasm surged, and great love for the earth was expressed. Towards the mountain of the generous Lord, with bones melting and a great yearning rising in his heart." This is the sacred verse. Its meaning is that as Thinnanar looked towards the mountain of the generous Lord, a great desire and love arose in his heart, causing even his bones to melt. Boundless desire surged, blossoming into enduring great love. This is because the accumulation of penance performed by the Nayanar in his past lives manifested as that endless blissful love. That is why such events are happening to the Nayanar. It is in this verse that references to Kannappa Nayanar's previous birth appear. When Sekkizhar Swamigal says "the accumulation of past penance," it refers to the Nayanar's previous birth. Firstly, as Thinnanar looked towards the mountain of the generous Lord, a desire arose in his heart, causing his bones to melt. "Looking" here means to gaze intently. Thinnanar is coming, continuously looking at Kalathi mountain. A desire for Kudumit Thevar arose in him. It is common in the world for someone who intensely desires something to focus on it and move towards it, isn't it? Therefore, Thinnanar comes, gazing with great desire at Kalathi mountain, the mountain of the generous Lord. When such great love arose in his heart, even the bones, which are the foundation of this body, melted there. So, if the bones melted, it means the entire body was ecstatic and melted. Bones are the hardest part of our body, aren't they? Thus, even hard bones become soft and pliable. Such great love arose in the Nayanar. Such great love arose that even his bones melted, meaning his entire body was ecstatic and melted. Manikkavasagar also sang that the Lord ruled him in such a way that all his bones melted. "Entering my body, which is a temple of flesh, making every pore ripen, you entered as a golden temple, melting all my bones, and ruled me as a humble one, O Lord, O flawless gem, cutting off all attachments to sorrow, birth, death, and delusion, O pure light of bliss, I have held you firmly, where will you now arise?" This is the tenth verse of Thiruvachagam, Piditha Pathu. Manikkavasagar says, "O Lord, who made this humble body of flesh, even its hair follicles, experience events, making this body of flesh a great golden temple, residing in it, melting all my bones, and ruling me, a humble one."

We should consider that state here in relation to Thinnanar. Why did such endless love and great desire for the Lord arise in Thinnanar? It is the accumulation of penance performed by Thinnanar in his past lives. Therefore, we must see how Thinnanar performed penance in his previous births and what penance he performed. This story of Thinnanar's previous birth is briefly mentioned by Sekkizhar in the Periya Puranam. However, it is extensively described in the Thirukalathi Puranam. Furthermore, Sekkizhar gives a hint that Thinnanar, due to the continuation of his past lives, became a very skilled archer, quickly mastering archery and becoming perfect in it. Also, a wild boar ran and came up to the foot of this Thirukalathi mountain. Thinnanar came chasing this boar. Therefore, it seems as if the same boar, which caused a fight between Thinnanar and the Lord in Thinnanar's previous life, has now come to unite Thinnanar with the Lord, as that large wild boar brought Thinnanar from Uduppur to Thirukalathi. All these are references to Thinnanar's previous birth, mentioned here and there. So, after reflecting on Thinnanar's previous birth, how he performed penance, and what boons the Lord granted him, through the Thirukalathi Puranam, we will continue from this point. Let's continue our reflection. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 20 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 143. As Thinnanar approached the foot of Thirukalathi mountain and began to ascend it, his bones melted, and he developed an unceasing, great love for the Lord. This great love and affection that came to Thinnanar stands here as a manifestation of the endless bliss of the accumulated penance he performed in his past lives. Thinnanar performed numerous penances in his previous births. What is penance? According to Saiva Siddhanta, Shiva Puja itself is penance. Righteous conduct (chariya), ritualistic worship (kriya), and yogic practices (yoga) are penance, according to Saiva Siddhanta. Performing penance directed at other deities does not become a penance that demonstrates endless blissful love. Only Shiva Puja performed towards Lord Shiva is penance. Thinnanar performed this penance, Shiva Puja and Shiva devotion, in his previous births. Who was he in his previous birth, according to the Thirukalathi Puranam? In his previous birth, Thinnanar was Arjuna and performed numerous Shiva Pujas and penances. It is due to that great penance that he has now come as Thinnanar. This is the history of Thinnanar's previous birth. Arjuna, the central figure among the Pancha Pandavas in the Mahabharata, has come as Thinnanar in this birth. When he was Arjuna, he performed numerous penances. He also performed numerous penances in births prior to that. Only after all these penances were completed and perfected did he incarnate as Thinnanar, a hunter, here. Arjuna's penance is found in three important places in the Mahabharata. Not only Arjuna, but all five Pandavas were Shiva devotees. The glories of Arjuna's penance are found in the Mahabharata in sections like Arjuna's Pilgrimage and Arjuna's Penance. Furthermore, the event that led to Arjuna's birth as Thinnanar occurred in the Kirata Parva of the Mahabharata's Vana Parva. Villiputhur Azhwar translated the Mahabharata into Tamil. His work is Villibharatham. It contains the section on Arjuna's Pilgrimage. Then, in the Vana Parva, there is Arjuna's penance. Later, during the Mahabharata war, in the 13th day of battle, there are also references to Arjuna's penance. Generally, there are three epics: Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Sivaragasyam. Among these, the Ramayana and Mahabharata are epics that subtly indicate the supremacy of Shiva. Sivaragasyam is an epic that explicitly describes the supremacy of Shiva. Therefore, all the characters in the Ramayana and Mahabharata have a history of performing Shiva Puja. The Pancha Pandavas in the Mahabharata were Shiva devotees. Among them, Arjuna's devotion to Shiva was particularly superior. In the Mahabharata, there is a section called Arjuna's Pilgrimage. On one occasion, Arjuna happened to see his elder brother Dharma and Draupadi alone. Therefore, it was a rule that he had to undertake a pilgrimage as atonement. So, Arjuna undertook a pilgrimage, bathed in various sacred rivers, and visited various Shiva and Vishnu temples. This history is described in the Adi Parva of Villibharatham, in the section on Arjuna's Pilgrimage. Arjuna, who set out on a pilgrimage from Indraprastha, bathed in sacred rivers like the Ganga and Yamuna. He traveled east and visited all the sacred places there. Then he came to Dakshina Bhumi, South India.

He bathed in rivers like Godavari and Krishnaveni and had darshan of Srisailam. In the south, he had darshan of Thiruvengadam and Thirukalathi, which are on the border of Tamil Nadu. He reached Kanchipuram. In Kanchipuram, one of the seven cities that grant liberation, he had darshan of Ekambaranathar, Kamakshi Amman, and Kumarakottam, where Lord Muruga is enshrined. In Hastigiri, he had darshan of Mahavishnu. Then he had darshan of Thiruvannamalai, where the world-renowned Annamalaiyar resides. He bathed in the Pennai River, reached Thirukkovalur, and had darshan of Veeratannar and Perumal there. He had darshan of Lord Shiva in the Shiva temples of Thiruvennainallur and Thiruvathigai. In Thiruvahindrapuram, he praised and had darshan of Lord Vishnu. Then he came to Chidambaram and had darshan of Lord Nataraja. He came to Srirangam and worshipped the feet of Namberumal. Similarly, he bathed in many sacred waters and came to Madurai. There, he worshipped Lord Shiva and Angayarkanni Amman and married Chitrangada. Then, in Thirumaliruncholai, he had darshan of Sundararaja Perumal, also known as Azhagar or Kallazhagar. Then he reached Sethu and bathed in the sacred waters of Rameswaram. He bathed in Kumari Thurai, also known as Kanni Theertham or Kanyakumari. Then he had darshan of all the temples established by Parasurama. He had darshan of Thirukokarna Kshetra and then reached Dwaraka. Thus, in Arjuna's pilgrimage, he had darshan of numerous Shiva and Vishnu temples. These are the events that occur in the Adi Parva of the Mahabharata. Similarly, during the Mahabharata war, on the 13th day, there are accounts of Arjuna's Shiva Puja and Shiva penance. To defeat Jayadratha, a great devotee of Shiva, Krishna took Arjuna to Mount Kailash to seek boons and weapons from Lord Shiva. On the way, Arjuna felt hungry. He asked, "I will not eat without performing Shiva Puja. How can I perform Shiva Puja now?" Then, there are references in Villibharatham that he performed Shiva Puja according to the Agamic tradition. He reached Mount Kailash and performed penance on its slopes. Lord Shiva was pleased with his penance and granted him the desired boons and weapons. Now, the third and important part is the event that occurs in the Vana Parva. This event forms the basis for Arjuna's birth as Thinnanar. This is found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. "Thus, Arjuna, as a hunter, performed the puja of the foremost Lord, and then, by the grace of Kalathinathar, who shares half with the Goddess, attained high liberation," states the Thirukalathi Puranam. Among the five Pandavas, Arjuna performed penance to obtain the unparalleled Pasupatha Astra from Lord Shiva, to use against his enemies, Duryodhana and others, and defeat them. He performed penance on the Indraneela Parvat. He bathed in the sacred river, wore a clean cloth, smeared sacred ash all over his body according to his vows, wore a rudraksha garland, chanted the Shiva Panchakshara Mantra, sat in a noble posture, directed his breath through the Sushumna instead of Ida and Pingala, transcended the six chakras, and performed intense penance to achieve mental absorption in the form of light in the Brahmarandhra. While Arjuna was performing such penance, Mookasura, sent by Duryodhana to kill Arjuna, took the form of a boar and went to the forest where Arjuna was performing penance, causing trouble to the sages and other great people there. At the same time, seeing Arjuna performing penance on Mount Kailash, Ambika's heart softened. Seeing Arjuna performing severe penance on one leg, the Mother's heart softened. Therefore, Parvati Devi, the Universal Mother, appealed to Lord Shiva, "O Lord, please bestow your grace upon Arjuna, who is performing severe penance." After Uma Devi, the grace-power of Lord Shiva, appealed to bestow grace upon Arjuna, the Lord immediately decided to bestow grace upon Arjuna. Lord Shiva resolved that Arjuna's penance should not be disturbed by Mookasura, who was sent by Duryodhana in the form of a boar. Therefore, Lord Shiva assumed the form of a Kirata (hunter) to bestow grace upon Arjuna. The Lord came as a hunter. Lord Shiva, as a hunter, wore sandals on his feet, tied a tiger skin, tucked a sword, adorned himself with various beads, Kuntri mani, conch shells, Athi flowers, and Konrai flowers, carried a quiver, and held a bow and arrow, coming as Kirata Murthy, in the form of a hunter. Uma Devi also took the form of a huntress, befitting the hunter's guise assumed by Lord Shiva, and came with Lord Shiva. Because, "In whatever form the Lord stands, in that form she also stands," is from Sivagnana Siddhiyar, isn't it? Therefore, whatever guise the Lord assumes, the Mother, as the power of grace, will assume a similar guise. The scriptures speak of the nine forms of the Lord as "Navam Tharum Bhedam." In all these nine forms of the Lord, the Goddess also appears. When the Lord is Parasiva, the Mother is Parasakti. When the Lord is Shiva, the Mother is Sakti. When the Lord takes the form of Sadashiva, the Mother comes as Manonmani. When the Lord is Maheswara, the Mother is Maheswari, and when the Lord is Rudra, the Mother is Uma. When the Lord is Thirumal, the Mother is Lakshmi, and when the Lord is Brahma, the Mother is Vani. This is the form that Shiva-Saktis take at the highest level of Tattvas. Not only that, as in this story, if the Lord comes as a hunter, the Mother comes as a huntress, befitting the hunter's guise assumed by the Lord. In the Purana of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, when the Lord worked in the field disguised as a farmer, the Mother also stayed with the Lord as a farmer's wife, a woman from the farmer's community. As the Lord and Goddess came as a hunter and huntress, Mookasura, the boar, came roaring before the Lord. Lord Shiva, without any anger, playfully shot a sharp arrow. It struck the boar. The boar squealed and ran. The Lord followed behind. The boar ran across the hermitage of the sages. All the sages were frightened by this. Seeing this, Arjuna, who was performing penance there, angrily shot an arrow at the boar. The boar fell and died. At that moment, Lord Shiva, who had come in the form of a hunter, also arrived there. "I killed this boar," said Lord Shiva in the hunter's form. Arjuna, however, said, "I killed this boar." This led to a fight between Arjuna and Lord Shiva. Both exchanged arrows. In this fight, the string of Arjuna's bow broke. Then, an angry Arjuna struck Lord Shiva on his sacred head with the bow. Lord Shiva accepted that blow, as if it struck all the worlds. Lord Shiva bore Arjuna's blow on his sacred head, which even Brahma could not see. He did so to show that he is easily accessible to his devotees. Then, Arjuna and Lord Shiva wrestled for a long time. Lord Shiva considered Arjuna's fighting as a game played with a child and affectionately touched and played with him. Finally, Lord Shiva revealed his true form. Lord Shiva appeared to Arjuna as Nilakantha, with four arms, accompanied by Uma, mounted on a bull.

Arjuna, realizing that it was Lord Shiva, trembled, sweated, and his mind weakened. His love overflowed, pouring out as tears of joy. He prostrated himself on the ground many times, becoming bewildered. Then, with a somewhat clear mind, he said, "O Lord, please forgive the faults I have committed." Hearing this, Lord Shiva graciously said to Arjuna, "Do not fear. Ask for the boon you desire. We will grant that boon." Arjuna, bowing respectfully, stood up and appealed to Lord Shiva, "I have suffered in the ocean of birth. Please grant me liberation so that I may be saved." To this, Lord Shiva replied, "Arjuna, you performed penance to obtain the Pasupatha Astra to kill all your enemies, didn't you? Therefore, first receive that Pasupatha Astra." He continued, "You performed penance for the Pasupatha Astra, so we have given it to you now. Now you are asking for liberation. Therefore, be born in the next life in Southern Kailash. Then we will grant you liberation." He added, "You spoke disparagingly of me as a hunter just now, didn't you? Therefore, in your next birth, you will be the king of hunters, reach Southern Kailash, and there, with great devotion and joy, worship the Shiva Lingam, which is the embodiment of boundless love, and feed me with the flesh and ambrosia of animals like boars. When you do so, we will grant you liberation." Lord Shiva then blessed him and disappeared. It is for this reason that Arjuna, in this birth, incarnated as Thinnanar, was born as the chief of the hunter tribe on Kalathi mountain, and even though he did not receive education or knowledge, he is destined to worship Lord Shiva and attain liberation according to the grace bestowed by Lord Shiva. These are the details about Kannappa Nayanar's previous birth found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. When he performed penance as Arjuna, a boar came, and a fight ensued between them, didn't it? Similarly, a boar here also served as an instrument, by the grace of God, to connect the Lord and Thinnanar, the reincarnation of Arjuna. This is the penance Arjuna performed in his previous lives. It is due to the accumulation of that penance that such great love and desire for the Lord now overflows in Thinnanar. Thinnanar did not perform penance only in his previous birth as Arjuna. He performed numerous penances in births prior to Arjuna as well. Because in Arjuna's previous births, he was Nara, one of the Nara-Narayanas. These very Nara-Narayanas came as Arjuna and Krishna during the Mahabharata period. Arjuna also received Shiva Diksha and performed numerous Shiva Pujas. He received the Pasupatha Astra. In his next birth, he was blessed to incarnate as Thinnanar. Sri Krishna also received Shiva Diksha from Upamanyu Muni. In the Anushasanika Parva of Sri Mahabharata, Chapter 17, there are details of Upamanyu Muni performing Shiva Diksha for Sri Krishna, the birth of children for Sri Krishna by the grace of Lord Shiva, and Upamanyu Muni teaching Sri Shiva Sahasranamam to Sri Krishna. Therefore, Arjuna was a severe ascetic not only in his birth as Arjuna but also in his previous births. All these penances combined have now led to his incarnation as Thinnanar, and Thinnanar now stands as an embodiment of love. This is due to the fruit of his previous births, because such a great state of love is unlikely to arise from penance performed in just one birth. Furthermore, Thiruvalluvar also states in the chapter on penance, "Penance is for those who have penance; it is folly for those who do not have it to undertake it." Thiruvalluvar beautifully states that one must have performed penance even to perform penance. Only those who have desired to perform penance and have performed it get the opportunity to do so. Therefore, the penance performed by Arjuna as Nara among the Nara-Narayanas in those times, and the Shiva penance performed in many births, and the Shiva Punya by which Lord Shiva appeared as Kirata Murthy and blessed Arjuna's penance when he was Arjuna—all these combined, "the accumulation of past penance," have come to Thinnanar in this birth, and as a result, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that he stands as an embodiment of love. This history of Arjuna receiving the Pasupatham also appears in many places in the Thirumurais. In many Shiva temples in South India, there are still historical records of Shiva Lingams established and Shiva Pujas performed by the Pancha Pandavas and Arjuna. However, here in the Periya Puranam, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only briefly mentioned the history of Thinnanar's previous birth, without elaborating. Generally, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to it as "the accumulation of past penance" in a general sense. This is because it is Sekkizhar Swamigal's tradition. Similarly, in the Purana of Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal and others, Sekkizhar Swamigal only hints at the previous births of those Nayanars. This is because those histories should be narrated in their respective Puranas. It is a rule that we should learn those histories from their respective Puranas. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal only provides hints and arranged for us to read the related history in the Puranas associated with them. However, we might wonder why he narrated the past and future history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in the Thirumalai Sarukkam and Vellanaich Sarukkam. This is because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the central figure of this Periya Puranam epic. Therefore, it was necessary to narrate the history of the origin of the Thiruthondathogai Thiruppathigam, which is the source of this Purana, and the history of the Purana itself. Since it was indispensable, Sekkizhar Swamigal sang it that way. Similarly, Kochengatchozha Nayanar is a slight exception to this tradition of Sekkizhar. To explain the reason for the history of Kochengatchozha Nayanar, who, by the grace of Adimoorthy, was born with prior knowledge, ruled, and built all the Shiva temples as Maadakkoils (temples inaccessible to elephants), Sekkizhar Swamigal extensively narrated the history of Kochengatchozha Nayanar's previous birth there. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only extensively sung about the previous and future history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and the previous birth of Kochengatchozha Nayanar. This is because they were indispensable for the Purana. For other Nayanars like Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, and Kannappa Nayanar, whom we are currently studying, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only hinted at their previous births. This is Sekkizhar Swamigal's tradition. This history of the Nayanar's previous birth is found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. It is also found in the 11th Thirumurai, in Nakkirar Deva Nayanar's work "Kailai Paathi Kalathi Paathi" and in "Pottri Thirukkali Venba." Therefore, as the Lord willed, Arjuna was born as a hunter, Thinnanar, and reached Thirukalathi. The slopes of Mount Kailash where he previously performed penance are now the slopes of Thirukalathi mountain where he performs penance. When he was Arjuna, a boar served as an aid to see the Lord in that state of penance. Similarly, here, a boar came as an aid to see Kalathiappar and led Thinnanar. Thirukalathi mountain is Kailash mountain, according to the Thirukalathi Puranam. "He who is on the peak of Kailash is Kalathian, he is within my eyes," is from Thirumurai. Therefore, understanding all these connections, Thinnanar's Shiva penance and Shiva Punya from all his previous births combined, he has now become an embodiment of love, has great love for the Lord, and is now going to attain liberation. These are the histories of Thinnanar's previous births. Let's continue our reflection. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 21 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 144. Ilaimazhindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. We saw that Sekkizhar Swamigal composed three verses describing Thinnanar's ascent of Thirukalathi mountain. We have already examined the meanings of the first verse. Now, the second verse: "Nanan and love went ahead, ascending the cool mountain. He, the Lord, ascended the tall, stable mountain, as if climbing the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas, to reach Shiva, who is the command." This is the sacred verse. Nanan and love go before Thinnanar. Love, which grows as enthusiasm, passion, and yearning, is the activity of Iccha Shakti (will-power). With this Iccha Shakti, this love, going ahead, and Nanan also going ahead, Thinnanar climbs the mountain. His ascent of Thirukalathi mountain, described by the phrase "climbing the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas," implies that just as Shivayogis arrange the 36 Tattvas, from earth to Shiva, as 36 steps, and ascend these Tattvas, transcending them to reach Shiva, similarly, Kannappa Nayanar's ascent of the mountain, his ascent of Thirukalathi mountain, signifies Thinnanar progressively transcending the 36 Tattvas to reach Lord Shiva. Thinnanar's ascent of Thirukalathi mountain is a symbol of transcending the 36 Tattvas. Why does Sekkizhar Swamigal call these Tattvas "cherished Tattvas" (Peṇu Tattvas)? "Peṇudal" means to nourish or foster. Tattvas, in the Sakala state, serve as instruments for the growth of knowledge, will, and action of souls, fostering the knowledge, will, and action of souls. Therefore, they are called "cherished Tattvas." "Peṇudal" also means to worship. Worshipping Tattvas means worshipping Tattvas in the Padmasana Puja of Shiva Puja, and in the Adhva Sodhana of Nirvaana Diksha. These methods are prescribed in the Agamas. Devotees can learn and understand these through initiation and instruction from qualified Shivacharyas. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar ascends the steps of Tattvas, doesn't he? These Tattvas are the 36 Tattvas from Prithvi Tattva to Shiva Tattva. Therefore, ascending each Tattva from Prithvi, transcending all Tattvas, and reaching the Supreme Being who is beyond Tattvas, is what is being described here. Now, let's briefly reflect on these 36 Tattvas, which are an important aspect of Saiva Siddhanta. We saw that the impurities of souls, called Malas, are three: Anava, Karma, and Maya. To experience the fruits of actions, the Lord creates and provides souls with Tanu (body), Karana (organs), Bhuvana (worlds), and Bhoga (experiences) from Maya. Therefore, the Lord creates these 36 Tattvas from Moola Maya. Each religion has Tattvas according to its philosophical principles. In Saiva Siddhanta, these are stated to be 36 Tattvas. What is Maya in Saiva Siddhanta? Maya is the primordial cause of the universe, eternal, insentient, the parigraha shakti (instrumental power) of the First Cause, pervading the First Cause, the ultimate reality, and formless. "Ma" means to dissolve, "Ya" means to arise. Therefore, that which is the cause for all effects to dissolve into it and for all effects to arise from it,

That is called Maya. This Maya is generally of two types: Suddha Maya and Asuddha Maya. Suddha Maya has other names like Maamaya, Kudilai, and Kundalini. Asuddha Maya has many names like Adhomaya, Maya, and Mohini. Suddha Maya is the cause of the pure universe, and Asuddha Maya is the cause of the impure universe. This second type, Asuddha Maya, is further divided into Suddhasuddha Maya and Asuddha Maya. Therefore, Tattvas are of three types: Suddha Tattvas, Suddhasuddha or Misra Tattvas, and Asuddha Tattvas. The third type, Asuddha Tattvas, are also called Prakriti Tattvas. Among these, the first, Suddha Tattvas, consist of five Tattvas. Suddhasuddha Tattvas are seven, and Asuddha Tattvas or Prakriti Tattvas are 24, making a total of 36 Tattvas. The five Suddha Tattvas are Shiva, Shakti, Sadashiva, Ishwara, and Suddhavidya. These are also called Shiva Tattvas. Now, the Suddhasuddha Tattvas or Vidya Tattvas are seven. They are Asuddha Maya, Kala (time), Niyati (order), Kala (art/knowledge), Vidya (pure knowledge), Raga (attachment), and Purusha (soul). Next comes the third type of Tattvas, the Prakriti Tattvas, which are 24. They are Moola Tattva or Guna, Buddhi (intellect), Ahankara (ego), Manas (mind), Mei (skin), Vaai (mouth), Kan (eye), Mookku (nose), Sevi (ear), Vaak (speech), Paadam (feet), Paani (hands), Paayu (anus), Upastham (genitals), Sabda (sound), Sparsa (touch), Roopa (form), Rasa (taste), Gandha (smell), Akasha (ether), Vayu (air), Agni (fire), Neer (water), Nilam (earth). Thus, the Shiva Tattvas, which are Suddha Maya Tattvas, are five. The Vidya Tattvas, which are Suddhasuddha Tattvas, are seven. The Atma Tattvas, which are Prakriti Tattvas, are 24, making a total of 36 Tattvas. When explaining these Tattvas, explaining from Suddha Tattvas to Prithvi Tattva is the creative order (Srishti Krama). Explaining from Prithvi Tattva to Suddha Tattvas is the dissolution order (Odukkam Krama). When the Lord creates, He creates one by one from the Suddha Tattvas above. But when souls living in the Prithvi Tattva transcend the Tattvas, they must ascend from the Prithvi Tattva below to the Shiva Tattva above. Therefore, when great people explain these Tattvas, there is a method of explaining them in the Srishti Krama. According to that, we will look at their explanations. We will briefly look at these 36 Tattvas. This brief explanation is taken from an article titled "Tattvas and Taattvikas" written by Thuthukudi Ramachangu Pandian Ayya. Devotees who wish to read their detailed explanations can learn them systematically from Saiva Siddhanta Gnana Shastras like Sivagnana Bodham, Sivagnana Siddhiyar, Sivaprakasam, Thiruvarutpayan, and Unmai Vilakkam. First, the first Tattva in Suddha Maya is Shiva Tattva. This is the first vritti (modification) of Suddha Maya, activated by the general power of knowledge (Gnana Shakti). It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as pure knowledge. It is the locus of the subtle (Sukshma) Para Vak. It is also named Laya, Nishkala, Shaktiman, and Nada. Souls with only subtle mala impressions reside in the Bhuvanas and Bhogas (worlds and experiences) within this Shiva Tattva. Next, the second Tattva is Shakti Tattva. This is presided over by the activity of Kriya Shakti (power of action). It is the locus of the Pashyanti Vak. It is also named Thoola Laya, Thoola Nishkala, and Bindu. It is the locus for Muktas (liberated souls) who have only gross Laya impressions, and for the Bhuvanas and Bhogas where they reside. Now, the third Tattva is Sadashiva Tattva. This Sadashiva Tattva is activated by the special type of Gnana Shakti and Kriya Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who is called Sadashiva. It is the locus of the Madhyama Vak. It also has names like Udyuttam, Bhogam, and Sakala Nishkala. It is the locus for Sadashiva Murthy, for the Shiva Agamas that stand as the form of Madhyama Vak, and for Pranavas and others who are the cause for the classification of those Shiva Agamas, and for Anusadashivas and others, and for their Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas. Now, the fourth Tattva is Ishwara Tattva. This Ishwara Tattva is the fourth vritti of Suddha Maya, subtly activated by greatly exerting Kriya Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as Ishwara. It is the locus of Sukshma Vaikari. It has names like Sukshma Adhikara, Sukshma Pravritti, and Sukshma Sakala. This Ishwara Tattva is the locus for the eight Vidyaswaras, such as Ananta Devar, and for the Rudras who have attained that state, and for their Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas. Next, the fifth Tattva is Suddha Vidya Tattva. This is the fifth vritti of Suddha Maya, grossly activated by greatly exerting Gnana Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as Ishwara, the cause of Vidya. It is the locus of Thoola Vaikari Vak. It has names like Thoola Ishwara, Thoola Adhikara, Thoola Pravritti, and Thoola Sakala. This Tattva is the locus for Ishwara, for the Shiva Agamas that stand as the form of Thoola Vaikari, for the seven crore Mantras, for the Ganas like Nandi, for the nine Shaktis like Vama and Jyeshta, and for Rudra, Mal, Ayan, Indra, and others who have attained liberation and have only mala impressions, and who activate the lower Rudra, Mal, Ayan, Indra, and others, and for their Tanu, Karanas, etc. Thus, we have seen the five Suddha Vidya Tattvas. Now, let's look at the seven Suddhasuddha Maya or Vidya Tattvas. The sixth is Asuddha Maya Tattva. This generally deludes souls bound by karma. Therefore, it is also called Mohini. Now, the seventh Tattva is Kala Tattva (Time). This Kala Tattva defines the experiences for souls who have gone to experience Bhogas, by various divisions of time such as Kanam, Lavasam, Thudi, etc. This Kala Tattva expands from the small unit of time called Kanam to the lifespan of Brahma, Vishnu, and Rudra. Next, the eighth Tattva is Niyati Tattva (Order). This Niyati Tattva ordains that one must experience the fruits of one's actions. The ninth Tattva is Kala (Art/Knowledge). This Tattva removes a little of the mala shakti and manifests kriya shakti for the experience of bhoga for souls that were obscured by the primordial impurity of Anava Mala, lying as if void. These three Tattvas—Kala, Niyati, and Kala—arise from Asuddha Maya Tattvas. Next, the tenth Tattva is Vidya (Pure Knowledge). This arises from Kala. It illuminates the knowledge power of souls. This Vidya then combines with the subsequent Tattva, Buddhi Tattva, and functions. The eleventh Tattva is Raga (Attachment). This arises from Vidya. It illuminates and functions the Iccha Shakti (will-power) of souls. The twelfth Tattva is Purusha (Soul). The state of the soul, which is connected to these five, from Kala to Raga, and is inwardly directed towards experience, is called Purusha. Thus, we have seen the seven Suddhasuddha Tattvas. Including the first five Suddha Tattvas, we have seen 12 Tattvas. Now, the 24 Prakriti Maya or Atma Tattvas. Let's look at them. The thirteenth Tattva is Moolaprakriti. This arises from Kala. It is also called Prakriti, Pradhana, Maan, and Avyakta. It is the cause of the five kleshas (afflictions) like Avichai (ignorance). The three Gunas—Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas—stand in equilibrium here in Moolaprakriti. From this, Guna Tattva arises. The state where the three Gunas, which are modifications of Moolaprakriti, stand in equilibrium, is the Guna Tattva. The fourteenth Tattva is Buddhi Tattva (Intellect). This Buddhi is produced from Guna Tattva. The state where Sattva predominates among the three Gunas—Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas—and Rajas and Tamas are diminished, is called Buddhi. The function of Buddhi is to ascertain. This Buddhi is what helps us to ascertain any matter. Therefore, this Buddhi Tattva is the support for the merits and demerits, which are the fruits of good and bad deeds performed by the soul. Next, the fifteenth Tattva is Ahankara (Ego). This Ahankara arises when Rajas predominates in Buddhi Tattva, and Sattva and Tamas are diminished. Its function is to stimulate the soul with the surge of "I will ascertain this and that in such and such a way." This Ahankara is of three types: Thaisatha Ahankara, Vaikarika Ahankara, and Bhootadi Ahankara. Thaisatha Ahankara arises when Sattva predominates and the other two are diminished. Vaikarika Ahankara arises when Rajas predominates and the other two are diminished. Bhootadi Ahankara arises when Tamas predominates and the other two are diminished. From Thaisatha Ahankara, Manas (mind) and the five Gnana Indriyas (sense organs of knowledge) arise. From Vaikarika Ahankara, the five Karma Indriyas (organs of action) arise. Next, the sixteenth Tattva is Manas (Mind). This arises from Sattvic Ahankara and has two functions: Sankalpa (conception) and Vikalpa (doubt). After the mind arises, the five Gnana Indriyas, which were mentioned earlier, arise from Sattvic Ahankara. Now let's look at those Gnana Indriyas. The seventeenth Tattva is Sevi (Ear). This, having Akasha (ether) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Sabda (sound). The eighteenth Tattva is Thol (Skin). This, having Vayu (air) as its locus, perceives Sparsa (touch). The nineteenth Tattva is Kan (Eye). This, having Agni (fire) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Roopa (form). The twentieth Tattva is Naakku (Tongue). This, having Appu (water) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Rasa (taste). The twenty-first Tattva is Mookku (Nose). This, having Prithvi (earth) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Gandha (smell). Now, from the Rajasic Ahankara mentioned earlier, the five Karma Indriyas—Vaak (speech), Paadam (feet), Paani (hands), Paayu (anus), and Upastham (genitals)—arise in order. These function, presided over by the soul's Kriya Shakti. They are as follows: The twenty-second Tattva is Vaai (Mouth). Its function is speaking, speech. The twenty-third Tattva is Kaal (Foot). Its function is moving. This is called Gamana. The twenty-fourth Tattva is Kai (Hand). Its functions are giving and receiving donations. The twenty-fifth Tattva is Paayuru (Anus). Its function is excretion. The twenty-sixth Tattva is Upastham (Genitals). Its function is pleasure. Now, the five Tanmatras and five elements that arise from Bhootadi Ahankara. The twenty-seventh Tattva is Sabda (Sound). This is called sound. The twenty-eighth Tattva is Sparsa (Touch). This is the sensation of touch. The twenty-ninth Tattva is Roopa (Form). This is the Tattva of light. The thirtieth Tattva is Rasa (Taste). This is the Tattva, the Tanmatra, called taste. The thirty-first Tattva is Gandha (Smell). This is the Tanmatra called smell. From these Tanmatras, the five elements arise in order. The thirty-second is Akasha Bhutam (Ether element). This arises from the Tanmatra called Sabda. The thirty-third Tattva is Vayu (Air). This arises from the Tanmatra called Sparsa. The thirty-fourth Tattva is Tejas or Theyu (Fire). This arises from the Tanmatra called Roopa. The thirty-fifth Tattva is Appu or Jalam (Water). This arises from the Tanmatra called Rasa. The thirty-sixth Tattva is Prithvi (Earth). This arises from the Tanmatra called Gandha. Thus, there are a total of 36 Tattvas. The order we have just seen is the order of creation. In the order of dissolution, the Tattvas dissolve into their causal Tattvas in the same order as they arose from them. Thus, the creation of these 36 Tattvas from Maya, the primordial substance, in a subtle to gross order, is called Srishti. Maintaining this is called Sthiti. Just as they arose, the effect Tattvas dissolve into their respective causal Tattvas, and finally, the gross Tattvas all dissolve into Moolamaya. This is called Samhara. Therefore, the Lord performs the three functions of Srishti, Sthiti, and Samhara within Maya. The soul, born in the worlds appropriate to these Maya Tattvas, experiences karmas, undergoes experiences, and then, by the grace of the Lord, following the path of the Shiva Agamas, removes its karmas (i.e., removes the Karma Mala), transcends these 36 Tattvas, starting from Prithvi up to Shiva Tattva, and by doing so, removes the Karma Mala and Maya Mala, thereby completely weakening the Anava Mala, and finally attaining the Supreme Being. This is what Saiva Siddhanta calls liberation. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Thinnanar's ascent of Kalathi mountain, symbolizing the 36 Tattvas as 36 steps, is like transcending these 36 Tattvas as 36 steps. Therefore, Thinnanar's ascent of Kalathi mountain is a symbol of his transcending the 36 Tattvas and attaining the Supreme Being. This is what Saiva Siddhanta Shastras and Thirumurais explain. Shivayogis transcend these 36 Tattvas, from Prithvi to Shiva, as steps, remove these Tattvas, and unite with Shiva, attaining Shiva. "Is there a day when you and I will stand in the space beyond the elements, senses, five sense organs, the three qualities, and the sound?" is a sacred verse by Pattinatthadigal. "Is there a way for this humble servant to attain the state beyond the six sixes?" is the 47th verse of Kandar Anubhuti. Arunagirinathar Peruman sings here, "beyond the six sixes," doesn't he? Six sixes mean 36. Therefore, Arunagirinathar Peruman sings, "When will this humble servant attain the state beyond these 36?" Thinnanar ascends these 36 Tattvas as steps when he climbs Thirukalathi mountain. Let's continue to reflect on the meaning of this second verse. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 22 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 145, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. We are contemplating the meaning of the three verses describing Kannappa Nayanar ascending the Thirukalathi mountain. We are currently contemplating the second verse. We have already pondered the meaning up to "Gnananum Anbum munbu nalirvarai era thaamum penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri." We saw that Thinnanar ascending the Thirukalathi mountain is a symbol of him transcending the 36 Tattvas. Now, in the next two lines of this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman describes what Thinnanar does after ascending these 36 Tattvas. He says, "Penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri aanaiyaam Sivathai saara anaibavar pola Aiyar neelnilai malai malaiyai eri nerpada sellu pothil." The Nayanar ascends the steps of the Tattvas, approaching Shiva, who is the embodiment of grace. "Anaithal" means to unite with Shiva. "Saara Anaithal" means that the Nayanar, leaving behind his previous attachments and the state of being one with ego, now unites with Shiva, who is true knowledge. Thus, he approaches Shiva. The verse states, "Aanaiyaam Sivanai saara anaithal." "Aanaiyaam Sivam" means Shiva who is grace. Here, "aanai" means grace. Shiva is the pure consciousness that stands without regard for external objects. This Shiva removes the "pasutvam" (animalistic nature) from souls and creates "Sivatvam" (Shiva-nature), doesn't it? That compassion, that mercy, is called grace. Therefore, he said "Aanaiyaam Sivam," meaning Shiva who is grace. When Shiva comes to bestow grace upon souls, it is out of compassion, based on compassion. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman uses the epithet "Aanaiyaam" here. Grace itself is the power of God. We worship the divine grace of God as Ambika. When God is in His true form (Swaroopa Nilai), He is Shiva Himself. When He has compassion for souls and comes to bestow grace upon them, He descends through the stages of formless (Aruvam), semi-form (Aruvuruvam), and form (Uruvam) to bless the souls. Thus, the God who is in His true form, out of compassion for souls, descends from that state in three stages – formless, semi-form, and form – and that compassion is what we celebrate as Shakti. The divine grace of God is Shakti. Therefore, these three forms that God takes are not like the bodies we have. He takes His divine grace as His form. Why does He take His divine grace as His form? Because if He did not come in this way, if God did not come with these forms from His true state, souls would not attain salvation. Therefore, for the sake of souls, for their salvation, God descends in these three stages. When He does so, He bestows the Vedas and Agamas upon souls. This is also solely due to His compassion for souls. The 66th verse of Sivagnana Siddhiyar states this: "Aaranam Agamangal Arulinaal Uruvu Kondu Kaaranan Arulaan Agil Kathippavar Illai Aagum. Naaranan Muthalaai Ulla Surar Narar Naagarkku Ellam Seerani Guru Santhana Cheythiyum Sendridaave." Therefore, we see in many places in Periya Puranam that whenever God comes to bestow grace upon souls, He comes with Shakti. The 68th verse of Sivagnana Siddhiyar also states this: "Mun Arul Sakthi Thanpaal Mugilgum Thaan Mulaiyaan." The commentary on this is that God always appears through Shakti and does not appear independently. Therefore, when asked who the God of Saivism is, the wise answer, "Shiva with Shakti." Therefore, anyone who transcends the stages of the Tattvas and attains Shiva does so only through the divine grace of God, through His great compassion, and attains that state of liberation. That is what Sekkizhar Swamigal states in this verse. "Gnananum Anbum munbu nalir varai era thaamum penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri aanaiyaam Sivathai saara anaibavar pola" – we have understood the meaning up to this point. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal continues, "Aiyar neelnilai malaiyai eri nerpada sellum pothil." He says, "Aiyar neelnilai malaiyai eri." Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to the Kalathi mountain as Aiyar Malai. "Aiyar" means a person of greatness. It also means a sage who has conquered the five senses. Therefore, "Aiyar Malai" means the mountain where sages live. Sages live and perform penance on this Thirukalathi mountain. This can be seen in many places, including the Sthala Puranas. Therefore, this Thirukalathi mountain is the mountain where such ascetic sages live. Hence, he calls it Aiyar Malai. Furthermore, "Aiyar" can also mean God, and thus it can mean the mountain where God resides. He refers to this mountain as "Neelnilai Malai." "Neelnilai" means extending for a long distance in form. The Thirukalathi Puranam, Soota Samhita, and other texts state that this place is five yojanas wide and ten yojanas long. Moreover, it is Mount Kailash itself. Therefore, it also means that it has an imperishable, permanent nature. He says that Thinnanar approaches this mountain "nerpada." "Nerpada" means to encounter, to meet directly, in the sense of attaining the liberation for which Thinnanar was born. Thus, Sekkizhar describes what happens when Thinnanar ascends the Aiyar's mountain "nerpada" in the third verse. This is the 104th verse. He concludes this verse by saying, "Nerpada sellum pothil Thingal ser sadaiyar thammai sendravar kaanaa munne Anganar karunai koorndha arul thiru nokkam eytha Thangiya pavathin munnai saarbu vittu agala neengi Pongiya oliyin neezhal poruvil anbu uruvamaanar." As Thinnanar ascends this Thirukalathi mountain, and as he goes to see Shiva there, Shiva's merciful and gracious gaze falls upon him. It falls upon Thinnanar. Because of this, all of Thinnanar's previous attachments leave him. Thinnanar, having abandoned all worldly and birth-related attachments, became the embodiment of love in the shadow of God's grace. Thinnanar ascends the Thirukalathi mountain and runs, goes, to see God there. Even before he goes to see God, God's gracious gaze falls upon Thinnanar. Even before he sees God on the mountaintop, the gracious divine gaze of God, who is on the mountaintop, falls upon Thinnanar. Didn't we see in the first verse that love goes first, and Thinnanar follows? As an echo of the love that went ahead, the grace that came from God above falls upon Thinnanar. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to God here with the epithet "Anganar." "Anganar" means one with beautiful eyes. The beauty of the eye is its compassion. "Kannottam" means compassion. The true beauty of the eye is indeed compassion. Therefore, "Anganar" means one with eyes full of compassion. Thus, the compassionate and gracious gaze falls upon Thinnanar. "Karunai koorndha arul thiru nokkam" means that the "marakkarunai" (unconscious compassion) that had hitherto been feeding the fruits of the two karmas and standing as a form of "thirodhanam" (concealment) changes into "arakkarunai" (righteous compassion) and becomes an excellent gaze. "Arul thiru nokkam" means "chakshu deekshai" (initiation through gaze). The initiation by which the guru gazes upon the disciple to remove his worldly attachments is called "chakshu deekshai." Through this "chakshu deekshai," this divine gaze, the worldly attachments of souls are removed. Therefore, the "thirodhana shakti" (power of concealment) that had hitherto stood now transforms into "arul shakti" (power of grace) and falls upon souls. God bestows such a gracious gaze, a compassionate and gracious gaze, upon Thinnanar. This divine gaze is a continuation of Thinnanar's past life. When Thinnanar was Arjuna in his past life, he was performing penance to obtain the Pasupatha Astra. When he saw God, didn't he ask for liberation? What did God say then? "In this birth, you will receive the Pasupatha Astra. In your next birth, on the Kalathi mountain, I will grant you liberation." Didn't He bless him thus? The continuation of that gracious gaze bestowed by God then is what is happening now upon Thinnanar. Therefore, the reason for God's gracious gaze to fall is Thinnanar's past penance. We should contemplate the verse from Nenju Vidu Thoodu at this point: "Thaan ennai paarthaan pazhaiya vinai pancha malak koththai ellam neethaan ninaiyu veru aakkinaan eththariya thonnootru aruvar payil thokkile thuvakku aruthan kannooru then amudham kaattinaan." The reason why this experience does not happen to everyone who goes to Thirukalathi mountain is their lack of penance. All the penances performed by Thinnanar in his past lives have now converged, and because of that, God's gracious gaze falls upon him. Because of that, he stood there as the embodiment of love. Due to this, the "pavam" (birth) that had hitherto remained with him leaves him. "Pavam" means birth. "Thangiya pavam" means the remaining birth. This means that this birth of Thinnanar was the only one remaining for him. The inner meaning of this is that he will have no more births. Due to this, the "mala," "maya," and "kanmam" (impurities, illusion, karma) that had continuously clung to the soul from eternity have all left Thinnanar. Therefore, this remaining birth for him now becomes a fulfilling birth, and he will now revel in liberation, where there are no more births. This is its meaning. Through God's gracious gaze, the soul's previous attachments will leave it. Thus, as all of Thinnanar's previous attachments left him due to God's grace, he became the embodiment of love in the shadow of the surging light. He mentions "pongiya oliyin neezhal" (shadow of the surging light), doesn't he? Here, "oli" (light) refers to Kalathinathar. "Neezhal" (shadow) refers to His divine feet. Therefore, it means that Thinnanar stood as the embodiment of love under the divine feet of Thirukalathinathar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also addressed and sang about Thirukalathinathar as "Oliye" (Oh Light). In the ninth verse of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thevaram, Thirukalathi Thirupathikam, it says, "Kaliyar vandu uraiyum Thirukalathi ullirundha oliye unnaiyallal ini ondrum unarenen." Meditating on God as a great light is prescribed in the Siva Agamas. "Aayiram Gnayiru polum Aayiram neen mudiyanum" is from Appar Swamigal's Thiruvarur Thevaram. This is the eighth verse of Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal's Fourth Thirumurai, Fourth Thirupathikam, Thiruvarur Thirupathikam. The verse is: "Aayiram thamarai polum Aayiram sevadiyanum Aayiram pon varai polum Aayiram tholudaiyanum Aayiram Gnayiru polum Aayiram neen mudiyanum Aayiram per ugandhanum Aroor amarndha ammanee." Here, Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal says, "Aayiram Gnayiru polum." This means that God shines like countless suns. When Thirumurugatrupadai begins, how does it describe Lord Muruga? It describes him as a mass of light. It describes him as the sun. Thirumurugatrupadai begins with "Ulagam uvappa valan erpu thiridharu palar pugazh Gnayiru kadal kandaangu."

How does the sun rise from the sea? The sun rises crimson from the blue sea. Similarly, Lord Muruga, on his blue peacock, with his reddish divine form, shines like the brilliance of a thousand suns. This is the meaning of the opening lines of Thirumurugatrupadai. God has been described as light and the sun in numerous texts. Lights are of three types: Akanda Jyothi, Kandakanda Jyothi, and Kanda Jyothi. Kanda Jyothi refers to the ordinary lamps and other light-emitting objects we see within this world. Kandakanda Jyothi refers to the sun, moon, and stars we see in the sky. Akanda Jyothi is called Shiva Jyothi. Among these, the Shiva Jyothi, which is Akanda Jyothi, is the greatest Jyothi that gives light to all other Jyothis. Shiva Jyothi is called Swayam Jyothi, Param Jyothi, Akilam Jyothi, and so on. Just as soul knowledge is the source for knowledge perceived by the senses, so too is Shiva knowledge the source for soul knowledge. The meaning is that God operates as the life of life, and Shiva, as Shiva knowledge, illuminates soul knowledge within the soul's knowledge. "Jyothiye Sudare Soozh Oli Vilakke" is from Thiruvasagam. "Oliyaai oli adhan oliya oli adhan oliyum thani tharum oliyaagi" is from the Perur Puranam, Niruthappadalam song. Thus, Thinnanar stands as the embodiment of love in the shadow of Kalathinathar, who is the source light, the supreme light, for all lights, in the shadow of His divine feet. They refer to God's divine feet as a shadow, don't they? God's divine feet signify "Thiruvadi Gnanam" (knowledge of the divine feet). God is the primal being who cannot be realized by "pasu gnanam" (animalistic knowledge) or "pasa gnanam" (knowledge of attachments). To realize Him, one can only do so when soul knowledge is immersed in the knowledge of His divine feet. Through that knowledge of the divine feet, all the aggregates of attachments, from earth to sound, will leave the soul. Then, that "pathi gnanam" (knowledge of the Lord), which is the knowledge of the divine feet, will manifest as a cool shadow to the heat of the suffering of birth. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman here refers to God's divine feet as a cool shadow. For Thinnanar too, since the 36 Tattvas were removed and all worldly attachments were removed from him, Thinnanar is now in the cool shadow of Kalathinathar, who is light, having abandoned "pasa gnanam" and "pasu gnanam," and is in the shadow of "pathi gnanam," which is the knowledge of God's divine feet. Therefore, he is the embodiment of love in the shadow of God's divine feet. He has taken love itself as his form. The accumulation of past penance manifested endless, blissful love. Then, through God's gracious gaze, previous attachments left Thinnanar. As all such previous attachments left Thinnanar, he saw the shadow of God's surging light. Because God's gracious gaze fell upon Thinnanar, and because Thinnanar is under God's divine shadow, Thinnanar attained boundless love for God as his form. This is its meaning. The soul has the nature of taking on the color of what it attaches to. Now, the soul is attached to Shiva. Therefore, Thinnanar has taken on the form of love, which is Shiva's form. In the first verse of the "Anbudaimai" (Possession of Love) section of the First Tantra of Thirumandiram, composed by Thirumoola Deva Nayanar, it says, "Anbum Sivanum irandu enbar arivilar. Anbe Sivamavathu aarum arikilar. Anbe Sivamavathu aarum arindha pin Anbe Sivamaai amarndhirundhare." Thinnanar now stands as an embodiment of the grammar of this verse. We will continue to contemplate the inner meanings of the next verses. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 23 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 146, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. The accumulation of Thinnanar's past penance manifested endless, blissful love. Through God's gracious gaze, all his previous attachments left him. Thinnanar was in the shadow of God's surging light. On that Thirukalathi mountain, he became the embodiment of love, taking love as his form. He saw the "Eka Nayakar" (Sole Lord), who was a rising shoot on the vast Thirukalathi mountain. The surging intensity of the great joy and love that arose within him propelled him forward with great speed, and he ran towards Him with intense longing, embraced Him, and stood there smelling Him. This is the next verse. Thinnanar saw the "Eka Nayakar," the Sole Lord, who was a rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall and vast, touching the sky. Why does God reside on the peaks of mountains that reach the sky? It is to bestow grace upon the Devas who descend from the heavens to perform worship. Thus, on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall and vast, reaching the sky, God is like a rising shoot on the mountain. He is like a shoot that rises and shines on the mountain peak. "Kozhundhu" (shoot) is the part of plants from which new leaves emerge. In the Thirukalathi Thevaram, Aazhudaiya Nambigal praises Him as "Kozhundhe en gunakkadale" (Oh Shoot, my ocean of virtues). The Thevaram also says "Kottur Kozhundhe." "Saiva Kozhundhe Thavakkadale" is from Kandar Kali Venba. Therefore, the way of addressing God as "Kozhundhu" is found in various Thirumurais and various stotras. Here, the one who is the rising shoot on the mountain is Kudumithevar. He is a Swayambhu Lingam on the mountain peak. Kudumithevar is there as a Sadashiva Murtham, an unmanifested-manifested divine form that arose as a Swayambhu. Shivalingams are of two types: Ishta Lingams and Parartha Lingams. Ishta Lingam is for those who have received Shiva Diksha to perform Aatmartha Puja. Parartha Lingams are Lingams created for Parartha Puja. The book "Saiva Samaya Neri" states that Parartha Lingams are of five types. Among these five types of Parartha Lingams, Swayambhu Lingam is the first. The second is Daivika Lingam, established by Devas. The third is Aarusha Lingam, established by Rishis. The fourth is Gaana Lingam, worshipped by Ganas, meaning established by Ganas like Asuras, Yakshas, Siddhas, etc. The fifth is Manuda Lingam, established by humans. Thus, Parartha Lingams are of five types. Among them, the first is Swayambhu Lingam. Swayambhu Lingam is further of three types. How did Swayambhu Lingam originate? From the countless movements of Anantheshwara's heads, three sparks arose from the fiery anger of Kaalagni Rudra. These three sparks became three Lingams with names like Nirmoolam and Sammoolam. Among them, Nirmoola Lingam is in water. Sammoola Lingam is on earth, based on a mountain. The other Lingam originated from earth and other elements. Thus, the book "Saiva Bhooshanam" states that Swayambhu Lingams are of three types. Here, the one on top of Thirukalathi mountain is Kudumithevar, who is a Swayambhu Murthi. He is there as a rising shoot on the mountain. Thus, Kudumithevar is the rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall to the sky. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about this Kudumithevar as "Eka Nayakar" in this verse. Thinnanar saw the "Eka Nayakar," meaning he saw Kudumithevar. "Eka Nayakar" means the unique primal God. "Oruvane Potri Oruvan Enum Oruvan Kaanaka. Eka Nayakanai Imaiayavarkku Arasai" – don't the Tamil Vedas praise Him thus? "Ekan Anekan Iraivan Adi Vaazhga" is from Sivapuranam, Thiruvasagam. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to God as "Eka Nayakar." In Thiruvasagam, Manikkavasagar says, "Ekan Anekan Iraivan Adi Vaazhga." In the book "Thiruvasaga Vishaya Soosanam," the following explanations are given for the term "Ekan" here. "Ekastatha Sarvabhootha Antharaatma" is from Kathopanishad. There are two types of universes: Chit Prapancham (conscious universe) and Jada Prapancham (insentient universe). This Upanishad states that the "Ekan" (One) who is the indwelling spirit of all these Chit Prapanchams and Jada Prapanchams is the only God. The meaning of this is that the Lord, who pervades both the conscious and insentient universes like the sky, is one. To the question of who this "Ekan" (One) God is, the Atharva Siras Upanishad answers. It says, "Eko Rudro Na Dwiteeyaka." This means that Rudra is one, and there is no second Lord besides Him. "Devanam Pathir Ekaka" is from Chandogya Upanishad. This means the One who is the Lord of the Devas. "Tam Aadi Madhya Anta Viheenam Ekam" is from Kaivalya Upanishad. This means the One who has no beginning, middle, or end. "The one God is hidden in all beings" is from Brahma Upanishad. "You are the one Brahman" is from Atharva Siras Upanishad. "Hara, the one God, guides the eternal souls" is from Svetasvatara Upanishad. Thus, from all these scriptural proofs and sayings, it is established that Shiva is the one who exists through the intermingling of all conscious and insentient universes, and that unity (Ekathvam) resides in Him alone. The name "Ekan" is one of Shiva's names, as stated in the Atharva Siras Upanishad. This is stated in the mantra beginning with "Atha Asma." Why is Shiva called "Ekan"? Because He absorbs all worlds into Himself. He then creates, destroys, and protects what He has absorbed. That is why God is called "Ekan." So says the Atharva Siras Upanishad. There are two types of universes, aren't there? Shabda Prapancham (world of sound) and Artha Prapancham (world of meaning). This world of words and world of objects are established with Shiva as their prime cause. They shine by Shiva's very nature. Therefore, the word "Shivamayam" (full of Shiva) is heard in the Vedas and Vedanta. Furthermore, the Sanatkumara Samhita says, "Sarvamayam Shivamayam Proktham Jagadethat Saraasaram," stating that this entire moving and unmoving world is full of Shiva. Therefore, according to the scriptural proofs, it is established that God, Brahman, is one, and that Brahman is Shiva. Thus, we have seen the scriptural proofs for Shiva being the "Eka Nayakan." The twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas, also praise this. "Oruvan enum Oruvan Kaanaka" is from Thiruvasagam, Thiru Andappaguthi. "Oruvane Potri" is from Thiruvasagam, Thirusathakam. "Eka Nayakanai Imaiayavarkku Arasai" is from the Ninth Thirumurai, composed by Thirumaligai Devar. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman says, "Eeraai Mudhal Ondraai endrum Oru Uru Aayine." In the Tenth Thirumurai, Thirumandiram, composed by Thirumoola Deva Nayanar, it says, "Ondru Kandeer Ulagukku Oru Deivamum Ondru Kandeer Ulagukku Uyir Aavathu Nandru Kandeer Ini Namasivaya Pazham Thinru Kandeerkku Ithu Thithiththavaare." Thus, the twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas, conclusively state that God is one, and that God is Shiva. We have seen this stated in the scriptural proofs and in the twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas. Now, Saiva Siddhanta,

We can see that Sivagnana Bodham, which is the essence of the Gnana Pada of the Agamas of Saiva Siddhanta, also states this. Sivagnana Bodham, Pramana Iyal, Second Sutra. In this, the illustrative Venpa number seven is as follows: "Ondru endrathu ondre kaan ondre pathi." In its commentary, the elders state: In the Chandogya Upanishad, it says "Ekamevadvitiyam Brahma," doesn't it? The word "advitiyam" in the phrase "Brahma advitam" here means "not two." Therefore, the meaning of "Ekamevadvitiyam Brahma" is that God is one. It conveys the meaning that there is no second God. Furthermore, it refutes the Abheda Vada, which states that God and the world are one and not two. This is because the Veda does not merely state "Brahma advitam" but also states "Brahma ekam" and "Brahma advaitam." Here, "Brahma ekam" should be understood to mean that Brahman is one. And for "there is no second thing apart from it," it should be understood to mean that there is no second God, no second Brahman, apart from that Brahman. This is what the Venpa of this Sutra of Sivagnana Bodham also states: "Ondru endrathu ondre kaan ondre pathi." Only one Brahman exists. There is no second Brahman apart from it. Only one entity called God exists. There is no second entity as God. This is its meaning. Sivagnana Swamigal also states the same idea as the meaning of the illustrative Venpa five, which begins with "Ondralaa Ondraal." All living beings, united with a body given by the unique primal being, attain birth, exist, and then attain their end through that unique primal being. That destroyer, who brings about the end, becomes the primal being. He becomes the unique primal being. No one else can be the primal being. This is its meaning. Thus, the scriptural proofs, the proofs from the twelve Thirumurais which are the Tamil Vedas, and the Agamic proofs explain the term "Eka Nayakar" used by Sekkizhar Peruman. Thinnanar saw Kudumithevar, who is such an "Eka Nayakar" and a rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain. Thinnanar, who had come with boundless eagerness and great longing due to love, wondering where God was, was seen by Kudumithevar's gracious eyes even before he saw Kudumithevar. Thus, as God's gracious gaze fell upon Thinnanar and God saw Thinnanar, Thinnanar's soul and senses shone with Shiva-consciousness. With such Shiva-conscious eyes, Thinnanar now sees Kudumithevar. Therefore, this sight seen by Thinnanar is not a sight seen with ordinary eyes like ours. It is a sight seen by Thinnanar, shown by God, with God's grace as his eyes. When Thinnanar, with Shiva-conscious eyes, saw Kudumithevar, the "Eka Nayakar," the surging intensity of the great joy and love that arose within him increased. The joy that was within him has now risen and become great joy. Therefore, that love is blissful love. Therefore, the intensity of this great joy and love increases. Ramanatha Chettiyar says that "vegum" (intensity) here refers to the speed of love that leads to Shiva-ananda (bliss of Shiva). The "vegum" mentioned here is not the "vegum" of ordinary human beings, which is subject to afflictions like hunger, thirst, sleep, etc. It is the "vegum" of love for Shiva, the desire to attain Shiva. Only when the ordinary "vegums" like hunger, thirst, sleep, etc., are overcome will the "vegum" of love to attain Shiva arise and increase.

This is what Thiruvasagam says: "Vegam keduthu aanda vendhan adi potri" (Praise to the feet of the King who subdued the speed and ruled). Thus, that desire to attain Shiva, without being contained, rose up and captivated Thinnanar, drawing him in. Thinnanar lost control of himself and completely forgot himself. He became completely devoted to God. Thus, having become devoted to God, and having seen Kudumithevar with Shiva-conscious eyes through God's grace, he ran towards Him with speed and embraced Him with longing. He embraced Kudumithevar with longing and stood there smelling Him. All these actions happened due to the abundance of love. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman has sung in Thiruththadakam: "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal peyarthum avanukke pichchiyanal annaiyaiyum aththanaiyum andre neeththal agandral agalidaththar aachcharaththai thannai marandhal than naamam kettal thalaippattal nangai thalaivan thale." Kannappa Nayanar is now an embodiment of the grammar of this verse. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman explains the state of a woman who has fallen in love with Shiva in this verse. First, the woman who has fallen in love with Shiva hears Shiva's divine name. She hears about Shiva's golden complexion. She hears about Thiruvarur, Shiva's abode. She becomes deeply in love with Shiva. She mentally abandoned her mother and father that very day. She gave up all the customs prescribed by worldly people. In her devotion to her Lord, she ceased all her actions. She forgot herself and even her own name. The meaning of this verse is that the woman united with the feet of Shiva, her Lord, and became His servant, having nothing for herself. This is the state of those who are "Shakti Nipadathu Utthamarkal" (those of the highest grace of Shakti). For those who have received Shakti Nipada, the stages in the Gnana Pada are Sravanam, Mananam, Nidhidhyasanam, and Nishtai. In Tamil, these are called Kettal, Sindhithal, Thelithal, and Nishtai Kooduthal. Here, in this verse, the first four stages are respectively called Kettal, Sindhithal, Thelithal, and Nishtai Kooduthal. The elders speak of them in terms of knowledge. "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal" (She heard His name first) is the first stage of knowledge, Sravanam, which is hearing. "Murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal" (She heard about the form in which He resides) is the second stage of knowledge, Sindhithal, which is contemplation. Name is generally perceived, that is the first stage. To specially analyze and understand the nature of that perceived name is the act of contemplation. This is the second stage of knowledge. Now, the third stage is Thelithal (clarity). "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal." This is the third stage of knowledge, clarity. Now, "peyarthum avanukke pichchiyanal" (and then she became mad for Him), doesn't it come? "Pichchi" means madness. To be madly in love with the Lord, Shiva, like this, is the fourth stage, Nishtai Kooduthal (attaining absorption), which is deeply immersing oneself in that love. "Annaiyaiyum aththanaiyum andre neeththal" (She abandoned her mother and father that very day), doesn't it come next? Here, the elders interpret "mother" as the power of concealment (Thirodhana Shakti) and "father" as Shiva in His neutral state (Thadastha Nilai), and thus it means the state beyond Tattvas, having abandoned these two. "Agalidaththar aachcharaththai andre neeththal" (She abandoned the customs of the worldly people that very day), doesn't it come? Here, Navukkarasu Peruman specifically indicates that the worldly people's customs are these actions of accumulating and experiencing karmas through self-assertion, and thus revolving in births and deaths.

They forget. Now, "Thannai marandhal" (She forgot herself), doesn't it come? This is the state of forgetting the truth that "I exist," that there is such a thing as "self," and knowing and abiding only in the primal Lord. Then, finally, he says, "Thalaippattal nangai thalaivan thale" (The maiden reached the feet of her Lord). "Thaal" (feet) refers to the divine feet of God, which is God's true nature, and it is in the form of bliss. Thus, the soul revels in the state of liberation. Thus, the elders explain the spiritual meaning of this Thiruththadakam of Navukkarasar Peruman. Thinnanar now attains the same state. According to "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal," Thinnanar first heard His name, Kudumithevar. Nanan said, "Kudumithevar is there," didn't he? That is the meaning of "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal."
