That is, the fruits of dharma and sin do not cling to the soul but remain as the fate of the body and are removed. That is the meaning of "vinai irandum aruthal." Now Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the three impurities were also removed. The nature of the body was removed. The two karmas were removed. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the three impurities were removed for Thinnanar. The three impurities are Anavam, Mayai, and Kanmam. Among these, Anavam is called Sahajamalam. The Sivagnanabodham Chitturai explains the nature of Anavamalam as follows. In Sivagnanabodham Chitturai, Sivagnanabodham Sutra Four, Adhikaranam Four,

the nature of Anavam is explained as follows: Sahajamalam, which is Anavamalam, is a deluder of knowledge, a substance, having the quality of ignorance, like rust on copper, a defect of the soul, an eternal bondage, inert, one, causing the distinctions of Vignanakalar, Pralayakalar, and Sakalar, distinct and separate in each soul, concealing and having many powers that are removed at their appointed time, the root cause for souls to experience three states, eternal, pervasive. This is the nature of Anavam. As described above, this Sahajamalam causes the pervasive soul to become atomic. Therefore, it is called Anavam. This Anavamalam is the cause of various beginnings such as Pasutvam, Pasu Neekaram, Mrityu, Moorchai, Malam, Anjanam, Avittai, Aaviruthi. Sekkizhar sings that the three impurities were removed. We have seen the nature of Anavam among those three impurities. Now, the second is Kanmam. The Kanmam here refers to Moola Kanmam (root karma). Because Sekkizhar Peruman has already sung "yaakkai than parisum vinai irandum arunthuvittathu" (the body's nature and the two karmas were removed). Now, the Kanmam among the three impurities refers to Moola Kanmam. This Moola Kanmam is the primary cause for souls to first acquire a subtle body. We have seen the nature of Anavam among the three impurities. We have seen that Kanmam refers to Moola Kanmam. Now, Mayai is the primary cause for the "thanu, karanam, buvanam, bhogam" (body, instruments, worlds, experiences) given to souls for the experience of the fruits of the two karmas. These are the three impurities: Anavam, Kanmam, and Mayai. The effects of these impurities are seven. They are of seven types due to the variations in the effects of the root impurity. Those seven effects are Moham, Madham, Ragam, Kavalai, Thabam, Vaattam, Vichitram. This is explained in Sivagnana Mapadiyam Sutra Two, Adhikaranam Two, as follows: Moham is that which is inherent in souls until the six types of bondage, such as Madham, are removed. If there is no Moham, then other things like Madham will not occur. Therefore, Moham is the primary one. What is this Moham? It is the cause of the delusion we experience in worldly matters, even after knowing what is stated in all scriptures, including ethical texts, such as what should be done, what should not be done, what should be accepted, and what should be rejected. Now, what is Madham? It is the cause of our praising a desired object as superior to all others due to the delusion caused by Moham mentioned above, and becoming engrossed in that matter, believing that no other matter is superior to it. Now, the third is Ragam. This is the increasing desire for an object or matter that we like. Next is Kavalai (worry). If we lose the object we possess due to the power of fate, or if we are about to lose it, then shedding tears and experiencing great sorrow is called Kavalai. That which causes worry is Kavalai. Next is Thabam (suffering).

Thus, when a desired object leaves us, after losing it, the internal burning and anguish is Thabam. Next is Vaattam (withering). This internal burning causes fainting, and the mind and body wither. Finally, what is Vichitram? We see various worldly matters and worldly life in this way. "He is mine. He is my relative. She is my rightful wife. These are my properties. This is my house. This is my land. How to protect them? How to increase them further? How to protect the family?" The seventh effect, Vichitram, is the cause of such various thoughts. Thus, Sivagnana Mapadiyam explains the effects of the impurities as Moham, Madham, Ragam, Kavalai, Thabam, Vaattam, and Vichitram. For Thinnanar, these three impurities mentioned above are removed. If the three impurities are removed, it means that the three impurities cease to perform their functions. What does it mean for Anavam to be removed? Anavam is called darkness. Just as darkness subsides when the sun rises, so too the power of Anavam subsides when the Lord's grace manifests in souls. This is the removal of Anavam. Sekkizhar Peruman says that these three impurities were removed for Thinnanar, doesn't he? But here, Thinnanar had the feeling "I will hunt and offer food. I will guard the Lord at night," and he had a body and the actions of internal and external instruments. He wandered in the world with these. So, were all these actions of hunting, guarding the Lord, and offering food to the Lord, which Thinnanar performed for these six days, the actions of the three impurities? No, these were not the actions of the three impurities. These did not happen for Thinnanar due to the actions of Anavam, Kanmam, and Mayai. How? Even though Thinnanar had a body, its actions, and that consciousness, he was without the feeling of "I" and "mine," such as "my body," for his own sake. After the Lord's grace fell, there is no mention that he ate food during these six days. He had no thought that he would become weak if he did not eat. He guarded the Lord for these six days. He remained awake. He had no feeling that he would get tired if he did not sleep or rest. We see that these two feelings were absent. Therefore, we should understand that other feelings like these were also absent. He did not even have the feeling "I will die." Thinnanar was in this state, under the sway of divine grace. That is why later, in Sivagochariyar's dream, Lord Shiva says, "All his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us." Thinnanar's entire consciousness knew only Kudumithevar. He did not know himself. The Anavam, which is self-consciousness, remained only as a momentary cause for his experience of Shiva-ananda. Now, Thinnanar hunted. He traveled. If one asks if these actions existed, even these were not done by Thinnanar for his own sake. Lord Shiva Himself says, "All his actions are pleasing to us." Because these actions of hunting and traveling performed by Thinnanar did not yield the fruits of karma and did not cause further karmas to sprout. The Prarabdha karma, which caused him to take a body, would remain as long as Thinnanar's body existed. Nevertheless, Thinnanar wandered as a mass of love, as the very form of love. Therefore, Thinnanar's knowledge did not go through the five senses for his own experience. So, his Prarabdha remained only at the level of his body. It did not cling to Thinnanar's soul. Unlike other souls where Prarabdha clings to the soul, for Thinnanar, it did not cling to his soul. His Prarabdha remained only at the level of his body. This is the explanation of how Prarabdha, a part of karma, did not affect Thinnanar. Now, the huge accumulation of Sanchita karma that Thinnanar had accumulated earlier, that collection of karma, was burnt and destroyed like cotton in fire the moment Kudumithevar cast his gracious gaze upon Thinnanar. Thus, we saw that Prarabdha did not affect him and remained at the bodily level. We saw that Sanchita was destroyed and burnt. Now, what remains is Agamiyam. Thinnanar stood only for the service of the Lord. Therefore, this Agamiyam also ceased to accumulate. Agamiyam accumulates only when we act for our own sake with ego. Thinnanar's actions were for the service of the Lord, for the sake of the Lord. Therefore, Agamiyam ceased to accumulate. So, even though Thinnanar performed actions like traveling and hunting, which are karmas, they did not cause Agamiyam for him. Thus, we have seen the explanation of how the three divisions of karma—Sanchita, Agamiyam, and Prarabdha—were removed from Thinnanar. Now, the Mayan things, which are the effects of Maya Malam, such as the body and instruments, ceased to have their nature. Thinnanar's body, by the gracious gaze of the Lord, became a form of love in the shadow of light. Thinnanar had a body, internal instruments, and external instruments. They also performed actions. Nevertheless, Thinnanar was completely under the sway of the Lord, and in His shadow, as a form of love, his jiva-bodham (soul-consciousness) was destroyed, and he stood in Shiva-bodham. Therefore, his actions were not his own but the actions of Shiva. As stated in the Gnana Sasthra called Thiruvunthiyar, "After we, who knew our actions, ceased to exist, the Lord's action is His own," and "He gave Himself," so sing the Thiruvunthiyar. This is the state that Thinnanar attained. Thus, Thinnanar, in the shadow of the Lord, with the nature of his body removed, the two karmas removed, and the three impurities removed, existed and wandered as a form of love, as a mass of love. Sekkizhar Peruman explains in this verse that Thinnanar, being in such a state, would not be swayed by the thoughts of Nagan, the hunters, or Devaratti. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 30 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam Periyapuranam Part 153 Ilaimalinda Sarukkam Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar offers holy food to the Lord. Sivagochariyar, believing it to be improper, performs daily pujas to the Lord according to the Agamas. On the fifth day, Sivagochariyar, after completing his puja for the day, bows to Lord Shiva and asks Him, "My Lord, I cannot bear to see such improprieties being committed in this place. By Your grace, please prevent these improprieties from happening." The Agamas are the word of the Lord, therefore they are eternal, unchangeable, and cannot be refuted by any other texts; they are established. Thinnanar performed puja according to the path he learned through direct perception (katchi alavai), inference (anumana alavai), and the words of others (urai alavai) that he heard. Sivagochariyar, the sage, performed puja based on the Agamas, which he understood as the word of the Lord, through Agamapramana (scriptural authority). Thinnanar's instruments (karanams) transformed into Shiva's instruments, and his knowledge became the knowledge of Lord Shiva, and he performed puja with that knowledge. Sivagochariyar, the sage, on the other hand, performed puja with his own instruments, guided by the Agamas, using his own knowledge. Thinnanar's puja was a puja known by the Lord Himself. The puja performed by the sage was a puja revealed by the Lord through the Agamas. Both were pleasing to the Lord. It is noteworthy that through the fruit of Sivagochariyar's puja, the Lord Himself revealed Thinnanar's love. Sivagochariyar asks, addressing Shiva as "My Lord." "My Lord" means "the master who owns me as a servant." Self-realization in Saiva Siddhanta means realizing the Master who owns oneself. Therefore, Sivagochariyar, who had realized his Master, asks Lord Shiva, "You are the Lord of my soul, the life of my life. Therefore, You know my heart. I ask this only because I desire purity in Your presence." He prays, "By Your grace, please remove all these improprieties." The inner meaning of this is that these improprieties will cease to occur only by the Lord Shiva's grace and will, and by nothing else. On that night, the fifth night, when Sivagochariyar made his appeal to the Lord, it was the auspicious day when the Lord willed to manifest His grace for both Sivagochariyar and Thinnanar to see. On that night, Lord Shiva appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream. The Lord, considering the maturity of souls, manifests His grace to some in their waking state and to others in their dreams, according to their maturity. Moreover, it is known from the scriptures that for those with one impurity, He reveals Himself from within; for those with two impurities, He appears in their presence with a deer, axe, four arms, and a blue throat; and for those with three impurities, He comes directly as a Guru in their dream in the third person. Here, on the sixth day, He is going to bless Thinnanar directly in his waking state. Now, on the fifth night, He is going to bless Sivagochariyar in his dream. "You are unknown to the gods even in dreams, yet You have graced this dog-like me even in waking," says the sacred utterance.

Sekkizhar Peruman sings in the 805th verse: "That night, in the dream of the gracious sage, the Brahmin with the radiant matted crown appeared and said, 'Do not think of him as a fierce hunter. Know that all his form is love for us, all his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us, and all his actions are pleasing to us. Know his state thus.'" Sekkizhar Peruman also sings in the 806th verse. Lord Shiva appears in Sivagochariyar's dream as a Brahmin with a radiant matted crown. This is the form in which the Lord appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream. He came as a Brahmin with matted hair, revealing His true nature, with three eyes and a blue throat, and other identifying marks. Among these sacred marks, the matted hair (Jatamudi) is primary, so it is mentioned first as "min thigazhum sadai mouli" (radiant matted crown). This Jatamudi of the Lord is such a primary sacred form, which is why the name "Kabardhine Namaha" is found in the Shiva Ashtothram for the Lord. Thus, He comes in a dream in the form of a Brahmin with matted hair, three eyes, and a blue throat. The Vedas state that Lord Shiva alone is the Brahmin. Sivagochariyar was one who praised the Lord daily with the words of the Vedas in his Parartha Puja, believing Him to be the unique and supreme Lord. Therefore, to Sivagochariyar, a Shiva Brahmin and a Vedic sage, the Lord appears in his dream in the form of a Brahmin, just as he had meditated upon and praised Him. To show that He is the same Lord Shiva whom Sivagochariyar praises daily, He comes in the dream in the form of a Brahmin, with matted hair, three eyes, and a blue throat as His identifying marks. Having come, Lord Shiva then says the following to Sivagochariyar: "Do not think of him as a hunter. Even though he belongs to the hunter tribe, he is not to be considered as one of the hunters. Nor is he just another human being. His nature is different. I will tell you his nature, listen." Lord Shiva then begins to speak. He says, "All his form is love for us." That is, Thinnanar's form is the very embodiment of love. Lord Shiva says that Thinnanar is the form that love would take if it were to gather and take a shape. Next, He says, "All his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us." Lord Shiva says that all his knowledge was directed only towards Him and did not go anywhere else. Furthermore, Lord Shiva says, "All his actions are pleasing to us." He says that all his actions were done to please Him. That is, all of Thinnanar's actions, such as waking up in the morning, going hunting, bringing food, offering food, bathing the deity, removing flowers with his footwear, and guarding at night without sleeping, are all pleasing to Him. He says that these were all done for Him, for His sake. Even if Thinnanar's actions are not considered pleasing according to the Agamic rules prescribed by Him, the Agamas themselves prescribe love above all else. Therefore, He says that all the actions performed by Thinnanar, who is the embodiment of love, are pleasing to Him. Love is desire; that is, He says that all of Thinnanar's form is love for Him. This is Ichcha (desire). He says that all his knowledge is the knowledge that knows Him. This is Gnanam (knowledge). He says that all his actions are pleasing to Him. This is Kriya (action). Thus, Thinnanar's Ichcha, Gnanam, and Kriya were all directed towards the Lord. They existed for the Lord. Therefore, Lord Shiva blessed him, saying, "You know his state." He further says in the next verse: "To show you his actions, tomorrow you hide and see. You will see all his devotion to me. Cast away your mental worries," and having blessed the great sage, the Lord with the matted crown adorned with water departed. This is the 807th verse. Lord Shiva, having appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream and told him that Thinnanar's Ichcha, Gnanam, and Kriya were all directed towards Him and happening for His sake, further blessed Sivagochariyar. "Tomorrow, you hide without the hunter seeing you. We will show you all his actions. We will show you how much love he has for me. You will see the state of his love tomorrow. Therefore, cast away your mental worries now," Lord Shiva blesses the great sage Sivagochariyar in his dream. Lord Shiva says, "Tomorrow you hide and see. See from a hidden place." That is the sixth day. Lord Shiva tells Sivagochariyar to hide in a secluded place so that Thinnanar does not see him, but Sivagochariyar can see Thinnanar's actions. Because the next day, Kudumithevar shows Thinnanar blood flowing from his eyes. Thinnanar then becomes very angry and searches for who did this. If Sivagochariyar were to be caught in front of Thinnanar's eyes at that time, something bad would happen. Therefore, the Lord commanded Sivagochariyar, the sage, to hide and watch in his dream. Furthermore, the Lord says in the dream, "We will show you all his loving qualities tomorrow. You will see." Not only the Lord's gracious acts, but everything can only be seen by us if the Lord shows it. Having said all this, Sivagochariyar, who had come with great mental worry and had prayed during the Shiva puja that night, was told by Lord Shiva to cast away his mental worries, and then He disappeared. According to the Thirukkural, "It is difficult to remove mental worries except for those who have joined the feet of the incomparable One." Therefore, Sivagochariyar sought the grace of Lord Shiva and meditated only on His feet. Therefore, that very night, the Lord blessed him, saying, "Let Sivagochariyar's mental worries be removed." Thus, Lord Shiva, who has the Ganga on His matted crown, disappeared from the dream after blessing him. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Lord Shiva as "Punal Sadila Thirumudiyar" (the Lord with the matted crown adorned with water) in this verse to indicate that just as He bore the Ganga for Bhagiratha, He blessed Sivagochariyar to remove his mental worries. Some also say that the Agamas, which are spread out like the Vedas, are the matted hair, and the grace that can explain the meanings of those Agamas is the Ganga. Thus, the Lord, having revealed His grace in the dream, Sivagochariyar emerges from the dream state into the waking state. Sivagochariyar, having attained the waking state, remains awake until dawn, meditating on that dream. The dream state is called Svapna Avastha. The waking state is called Jagrat Avastha. Jagrat, Svapna, Sushupti, Turiya, Turiyatita are the different states of the soul in the body.

It is also said that the soul resides in the middle of the eyebrows, neck, heart, navel, and Mooladhara respectively in these states. In the dream state (Svapna Avastha), the soul acts through the subtle body from the place where it saw. Then, when it comes to the waking state (Jagrat Avastha), it acts through the gross body from the middle of the eyebrows, perceiving through the five senses. This is stated in the Gnana Sasthras. Generally, it is the nature of the soul to forget many things seen in the waking state when in a dream, and not to perceive many things seen in a dream when in the waking state. But here, it was not so; these were things revealed by the Lord in a dream. Therefore, it is mentioned here that Sivagochariyar, the sage, perceived in his waking state all that the Lord revealed in his dream. Furthermore, it is a tradition that those who have a good dream will lose the benefit of that good dream if they sleep after having the dream. Also, they say that a dream seen in the last watch of the dawn will yield immediate results. This dream seen by Sivagochariyar also yielded immediate results. Therefore, it can be inferred that this was a dream seen in the early morning. Therefore, it can also be understood that he remained awake, expecting the Lord's grace. Furthermore, it is a rule not to sleep after the fourth watch of the night. Therefore, Sivagochariyar, who came to the waking state from the dream, meditating on the dream he had seen, remained awake thereafter, meditating on the Lord's grace. Wonder arose in his mind. A feeling of pride arose. When he realized what he did not know about that hunter, the greatness of Thinnanar, the hunter, as revealed by the Lord, and his own smallness and errors in comparison, and how the Lord had forgiven those errors and blessed him, Sivagochariyar felt fear. With this wonderful feeling and fear, Sivagochariyar performs the puja on the sixth day. As on the previous day, he comes, bathes, and performs puja. That is, taking a holy bath, searching for flowers, water, and other materials for puja, climbing the hill, and meditating on the Lord's grace all happen as before. Sivagochariyar performs puja as before. What is the difference? Even on this sixth day's puja, there are meat, bones, and stones, just as he saw on the previous days. But Sivagochariyar does not remove these, purify himself again by bathing in the river, and make prayers about it today. Because he has received the power of the Lord's grace, and his mind has melted in thought of that grace and attained peace. He has come for this sixth day's puja after the Lord revealed it in his dream on the fifth night. What did the Lord say in the dream? He revealed to Sivagochariyar the greatness of the hunter and the greatness of the puja performed by the hunter, saying that all the hunter's actions were for Him. Therefore, Sivagochariyar now finds the meat and bones placed by Thinnanar on the previous day in the Lord's shrine. Did he remove them? Yes, he removed them. He removed them today as well. But with what feeling did he remove them? With what thought did he remove them? He removed them thinking that these things, which Thinnanar had removed, were said by the Lord to be pleasing to Him. Therefore, for the previous five days, he had removed them thinking they were improper. Just as he removed the Nirmalyams (offerings) of the previous day's Shiva puja, he removed them on this sixth day's Shiva puja. On the previous days, he had removed them thinking that the meat and bones were improper. After that, he bathed in the Ponmugali river, brought puja materials, purified them, and performed puja. But the Lord had said in the dream on the previous day that these were pleasing to Him, hadn't He? Therefore, now, on the sixth day, his act of removing the meat and bones was like removing the Nirmalyams offered in the previous day's Shiva puja. After that, he performed the daily Nitya Puja. Then, as instructed by the Lord in the dream, he stood hidden behind the trees behind Kalathinathar. The puja performed by Sivagochariyar on this day was different from the pujas he performed on previous days. Because on previous days, he performed puja with mental worries. But now, on the sixth day, the Lord had told him to cast away his mental worries. Accordingly, on this sixth day, he performed puja with a peaceful mind, free from worries. Having completed the daily pujas, Sivagochariyar stands hidden behind the trees behind Kalathinathar, as per the Lord's command. We will continue to reflect on what happens on that day. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 31 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam Periyapuranam Part 154 Ilaimalinda Sarukkam Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. On the fifth night after Thinnanar began worshipping Kudumithevar, Lord Shiva appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream, spoke of Thinnanar's greatness, and told him to finish his puja the next day (the sixth day) and then observe what happened from a hidden place. Accordingly, the next morning, Sivagochariyar performed the daily puja for Kudumithevar and waited, hidden among the trees, to observe the events. At the same time, on the sixth morning, as usual, Thinnanar set out for hunting at dawn. This was the sixth day of Thinnanar's worship, having attained Shiva-nature through the gracious gaze of Thirukkalathiappar. This sixth day was the auspicious day when Thinnanar attained Shiva-peru (liberation). Thinnanar goes on a great hunt. The hunt Thinnanar undertook on this sixth day was not like the hunts he had undertaken on the previous four days; it was a unique and great hunt. The hunts Thinnanar undertook on the previous four days provided the meat-offering needed for his puja. Therefore, the hunts that occurred in the past four days were a means to the Shiva-peru that Thinnanar attained. The hunt Thinnanar undertakes on this sixth day became the fruit of the achievements he accomplished in the previous four days. Therefore, the hunt on this sixth day becomes a unique and great hunt. What did Thinnanar do on this sixth day? "He came near the Lord of Thirukkalathi, who is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him, with incomparable meat-offering, good holy water, and fresh flowers placed on his head, arranged in various ways." Lord Shiva, who resides on Thirukkalathi hill, is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. Lord Shiva is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. "Theruvar" means those who have clear knowledge, having clearly understood that Lord Shiva is the primordial Pasupathi. Sekkizhar Peruman, in this verse, refers to "Theruvar" as those who have mastered the four stages of hearing, contemplation, clarity, and absorption (nishtai). For such "Theruvar" who have attained absorption, the Lord is like nectar. The Lord is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. "The wealth that gives endless joy to those who cling to His feet, yet never diminishes," says the 22nd verse of Thiruvidaimarudur Mummanikkovai, composed by Pattinathu Adigal. Those who excel in knowing nothing but serving Pasupathi as His devotees are called "Theruvar." The Thiruvempavai verses 9 and 19 from Thiruvachagam, which say, "We will bow to Your devotees' feet, we will be their companions. They will be our husbands. We will serve as they wish. If our Lord grants us this, we have no lack. Let our breasts not join the shoulders of those who are not Your devotees. Let our hands do no service but Yours. Let our eyes see nothing else day and night. If our Lord grants us this," also describe the nature of devotees called "Theruvar."

Lord Shiva of Thirukkalathi, the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. To approach this Thirukkalathiappar, Thinnanar comes with the intention of approaching Him with incomparable meat-offering, good holy water, and fresh flowers placed on his head. Thinnanar hurries, thinking that it has become too late. But he encounters many bad omens. Birds, which indicate good and bad events that are about to happen through their sight and sound, now show bad omens to Thinnanar. It is a tradition that if crows, sparrows, owls, and nightjars cry out and make a commotion, it indicates destruction and is a bad omen. "Owls and nightjars, being evil birds, fight among themselves and foretell destruction," says the 632nd verse of Thirugnanasambandar Puranam. Similarly, some say that if one sees a flow of blood on the way, it is also a bad omen. All these bad omens appear to Thinnanar. As Thinnanar goes to Thirukkalathiappar, carrying the meat-offering, holy water, and flowers needed for the Lord after hunting, all these bad omens appear to him. Generally, if bad omens appear when someone goes out, they would think that evil will befall them due to those omens. But for Thinnanar, his knowledge knew nothing but Thirukkalathiappar. Therefore, all the bad omens he saw appeared to him as evil things that would happen to the Lord. So, great compassion arose in him. "Therefore, I am ruined," says Thinnanar. "I am ruined! What has happened to my Father?" Thinnanar trembles. The knowledge that birds foretell omens and show blood came to Thinnanar through his previous experience. Sekkizhar Peruman describes these events in the 812th verse as follows: "As he hurried, thinking, 'I have delayed so long,' all the gathered omens repeatedly foretold evil. As these evil birds showed blood, he thought, 'What has happened to my Father? I am ruined!' and approached." Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing in the 813th verse: "The Lord of Thirukkalathi, to show Thinnanar's devotion to the sage, made one of His sacred eyes suddenly bleed. The great archer, seeing this from afar, ran swiftly." This is the verse. Thirukkalathiappar, as He had told Sivagochariya Munivar in his dream, to show the power of Thinnanar's love, made blood suddenly flow from one of His sacred eyes. Thinnanar, seeing this from afar, ran very swiftly. In this sacred verse, Sekkizhar Peruman has depicted the three forms of the Lord: the formless (aruvathirumeni), the formless-with-form (aruvuruvathirumeni), and the form-with-form (uruvathirumeni). Calling Thirukkalathiappar "Annalar" (the Lord) is the formless form. It says, "blood flowed from one of His eyes." So, there must be an eye. This is the form-with-form.

The Kudumi Thevar, praised as Kalathi Adigalar, who was the cause of this, is the Linga form, which is the formless-with-form. Thus, in this verse, the formless, formless-with-form, and form-with-form of the Lord are seen together, giving a vision. Why does the Lord take these forms—formless, formless-with-form, and form-with-form? It is due to His compassion, which came to remove the dark bodies (karmic bodies) of souls. "Karumeni" refers to the births that souls take. The Lord takes these three forms out of compassion to remove the sufferings of birth and to remove the defects of souls. This is found in the Sivagnana Siddhiyar verse: "It is said that He took a form, yet we saw His formless form. When He took another form, we saw both His forms. All three are the result of His compassion that came to remove our dark bodies." This is the 55th verse of the first Sutra of Pramanaviyal, Svapakkam, Sivagnana Siddhiyar. Therefore, in the Sivalinga form of Thirukkalathiappar, one face with three eyes was seen, and from the right eye, blood was flowing suddenly. In reality, the Lord's true body is not a body made of the seven dhatus (elements) that are the product of Maya. Therefore, blood did not actually flow from that body. So, we should understand that what was seen here was a sight as if blood was flowing. Because the bodies of worldly people like us are made of seven dhatus. Blood is one of those seven dhatus. But the body of the Lord is a body that came with His compassion as its form. Therefore, when it is said that the Lord showed blood flowing from His sacred eyes, it means that blood was not actually flowing. We should understand that the Lord showed a sight as if blood was flowing. Thinnanar had already seen bad omens. Therefore, he ran in a panic, overwhelmed by love, wondering what had happened to the Lord. When he arrived, even from a distance, the Lord showed Thinnanar as if blood was flowing from His right eye on His sacred face. Thinnanar, seeing this from afar, ran very fast towards the Lord. Sekkizhar describes Thinnanar's state in the next verse. "He who came saw blood and fainted. The good water in his mouth spilled, the meat and bow in his hand scattered and fell. The garland of fresh flowers on his head swayed and came loose. The one with a garland on his chest fell to the ground, collapsing." This is the verse in which Sekkizhar Swamigal explains Thinnanar's state. Thinnanar saw blood flowing from the Lord's sacred face even as he approached from a distance. When he came very close to the Lord, he still saw that blood. Upon seeing it, he fainted. The sight itself caused him to faint. But only cowards faint upon seeing blood. But was Thinnanar a coward? Sekkizhar Swamigal uses the epithet "alangal marpar" (one with a garland on his chest) to indicate that he was not. This idiom refers to the valor of the chest. It indicates one's valor and the strength of the chest. Therefore, Sekkizhar says "alangal marpar." This indicates Thinnanar's valor and strength. Thinnanar was a warrior who engaged in hunting and bloodshed. But here, he fainted upon seeing Kudumi Thevar's blood, which was due to his immense love for Kudumi Thevar. Fainting means losing one's senses. The sight of blood caused him to faint, and so the sense organs that support the body and the nerves that make them function properly went awry. Therefore, his body trembled, and Thinnanar fell to the ground. This is what Sekkizhar Swamigal says, connecting "saw, fainted, trembled, and fell." As he fell to the ground, the holy water he had brought in his mouth-pot spilled. The bow he held in his hand fell. The flowers, the sacred garland for the Lord, which Thinnanar had brought on his head, scattered and fell. These were all things Thinnanar had lovingly and painstakingly collected and arranged for Kudumi Thevar. Furthermore, Thinnanar had come with compassion, thinking that it was already late today. Thinnanar protected these items as more precious than his own life. The fact that he was unable to protect them and became weary shows us the intensity of his weariness. All these items, which he had painstakingly collected, became useless for puja today. Because on the previous night, the Lord had accepted all these puja items. Therefore, they had now become Nirmalyam (discarded offerings). So, there is also a hint here that they became useless for puja. Due to Thinnanar's agitation, the meat-offering he held in his hand for the Lord fell to the ground. The bow and arrow he held in his other hand also fell. The meat-offering held in his right hand fell. The bow held in his left hand fell. The bow Thinnanar held in his hand helped him hunt for the Lord's meat-offering during the day. The same bow served the Lord's work at night, accompanying Thinnanar when he guarded the Lord. That glorious bow also fell. The holy water for the Lord, which he had brought in his mouth-pot, slowly spilled and scattered. The fresh sacred garlands for the Lord were those Thinnanar had brought on his head. They also scattered and fell. Thus, Thinnanar fell, fainting. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman explains further. "He who fell, rose and wiped the blood. He saw that the bleeding did not stop. He did not know what to do. He sighed and fell again. 'Who did this?' he asked, and rose. He looked everywhere. He picked up his bow and arrow and thought, 'Did fierce warriors, my enemies, do this in this mountain? Or did wild animals, like lions, cause this?' He did not know, and searched far and wide in the dense, dark mountain range." The above two are verses composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Thinnanar thus fell to the ground in agitation and then rose. He saw blood flowing from one of the Lord's sacred eyes. He wiped the blood. Even as he wiped, the bleeding did not stop. He did not know what to do. His mind was distraught and agitated, and he fell to the ground again. Then, regaining his composure, he rose, thinking, "Who did this?" Valor and anger arose in Thinnanar to find and punish the one who had done this to the Lord.

It is human nature for the desire for revenge to take precedence over seeking a solution for gold. The same human nature now manifests in Thinnanar for the sake of the Lord. He picked up the bow and arrow that had fallen earlier. This was to punish those who had done this to the Lord. Thinnanar's state was one where he had forgotten worldly attachments and even himself. Would such a Thinnanar have thoughts of enmity and anger? We saw earlier that Thinnanar did not experience hunger, sleep, etc., for his own sake. Hunger and sleep, which occur for Thinnanar's sake, did not come to him. He forgot them. But he engaged in hunting to satisfy the Lord's hunger, considering it his primary concern. Similarly, the valor and anger that arose in Thinnanar now were due to his immeasurable love and affection for Kudumithevar. He considered those who caused such suffering to Kudumithevar as his enemies. This was due to his love for Kudumithevar. Therefore, for the sake of Kudumithevar, due to his love for Kudumithevar, the remnants of his previous state, such as enmity and other related feelings, came back to Thinnanar's mind. Here we can see the difference between Jivanmukti and Paramukti. Jivanmuktas are united with the Lord's service, becoming one with it, and remain connected with the Lord with body-consciousness due to that service. Paramuktas, on the other hand, are absorbed in the Lord and experience Him in worship. The state of Jivanmukta is to attain and remain in liberation even while having a body and instruments. Therefore, the Ichcha (desire), Gnanam (knowledge), and Kriya (action) of Jivanmuktas are solely for the Lord's service. When there is an obstruction to the Lord's service, they experience a worldly feeling due to residual impressions (vasana) and act to remove that obstruction. This is described as the nature of the "anainthor" (those who have attained union) in the 12th Sutra of Sivagnanabodham. Thinnanar attained the state of Jivanmukta with the help of Thirukkalathiappar's gracious gaze the moment he saw Thirukkalathiappar. After that, Thinnanar lived in the world for six days in that state of Jivanmukta. Even for Jivanmuktas who have a body and instruments, their Ichcha, Gnanam, and Kriya operate externally to a small extent. But that nature does not cling to them. Therefore, when Jivanmuktas perform their actions, they worship the Shiva temple as Parameswaran, and it is said that the Lord will appear as that for those Jivanmuktas who worship and see Him as such. This is the commentary given for the Sivagnanabodham Sutra in Sivagnanabodham Mapadiyam. Therefore, Thinnanar, who was in the state of Jivanmukta, asked, "Who caused this suffering to the Lord? Which animal?" and with anger and valor, he searched far and wide, thinking, "Who did this? Which animal did this?" He searched for the hunters who might have caused suffering to the Lord, but he did not find them. He then searched, thinking that wild animals might have caused this harm. He did not see any such evil animals in that area either.

Therefore, Thinnanar returned to Thirukkalathiappar and, with great sorrow, held His feet and cried profusely. "He saw no hunters, nor evil animals anywhere. He returned to the Lord, and with deep sorrow, held His flower-like feet, embraced them closely, and cried with flowing tears," is a verse composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. As Thinnanar had thought, no enemies were found there. Therefore, his heroic fervor diminished. Furthermore, the thought came to him that he had been separated from the Lord for so long, wasting time, instead of finding a solution for the wound on the Lord. Therefore, Thinnanar returned to the Lord. As blood flowed from the Lord's sacred eye, Thinnanar's sorrow also increased accordingly. Isn't it true that as the amount of blood flowing from a person increases, their suffering increases and the danger increases? Here, the forms of Thirukkalathi Nayanar and Thinnappa Nayanar became one through the contemplation of "Sivoham" (I am Shiva), becoming a form of love. Therefore, as blood flowed from Thirukkalathiappar, Thinnanar's suffering and sorrow increased. With this great sorrow, Thinnanar held the flower-like feet of Kudumithevar, embraced them, and cried profusely. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 32 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 155, Ilaimalinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, seeing blood flowing from the right eye of Kudumithevar, was filled with immense sorrow. He clutched Kudumithevar's feet and cried out with tears streaming down his face. "What is this suffering that has come upon this Supreme Being, as I have witnessed? What has happened to our Lord, who is dearer than life itself? What has befallen this pure one, whose devotees, once they reach Him, never part from Him? I don't know what I will do now!" he cried repeatedly. "I don't know what I will do!" he lamented. Thinnanar then declared, "I will search for the medicinal herbs in the valleys of this mountain to cure this disease of blood flowing from Kudumithevar's eye," and set off. Thinnanar believed that Kudumithevar had suffered a "bhagalippuṇ" (an arrow wound). A bhagalippuṇ is a wound caused by arrows. Since Thinnanar was a hunter, he knew that sharp arrows could cause wounds and bleeding. Here, blood was flowing from Kudumithevar's eye. Therefore, he thought it was a wound caused by an arrow, and that the bleeding was a result of it. He set out to find the appropriate herbs. Thinnanar rushed faster than the speed of his mind, gathered the herbs, and returned. He squeezed the collected herbs onto the bleeding eye. Thinnanar, believing that Kudumithevar's wound was caused by a sharp arrow, gathered herbs and squeezed them onto Kudumithevar's bleeding eye. These leaves are called "Salliyakarani" in Sanskrit texts. Salliyakarani is a medicine that heals wounds caused by arrows and weapons. Generally, four types of medicines are mentioned: Salliyakarani, Santhanakarani, Samaniyakarani, and Mrithasanjeevini. Among these, Salliyakarani is the name given to the medicine and herbs that heal wounds caused by arrows and weapons. Furthermore, the Ramayana states that various types of herbs like Salliyakarani are found in the mountains. However, even after Thinnanar squeezed the herbs onto the eye, the blood from Thirukalathiappar's eye continued to flow as before, unabated. All his efforts so far had been in vain. Thinnanar, undeterred, continued to ponder. "Flesh for flesh" is an old proverb. There is also the proverb "Mamsa Mamsena Varthathe." Among hunters, the proverb "flesh for flesh" was used to describe how to cure a disease of the flesh with similar flesh. This was because when hunters engaged in their ancestral profession of hunting, various parts of their bodies could be damaged for various reasons. In such cases, the proverb "flesh for flesh" was a common solution among them. When human body parts were damaged, the practice of taking a similar organ from another human or animal body and attaching it to the affected person, making it functional again, and thereby alleviating the deficiency of that organ, has now developed into modern medicine. It is surprising that even the hunters who lived in the forests of India thousands of years ago knew this.

This proverb came to Thinnanar's mind, and he decided to act as follows: "Now, if I pluck out my own eye with an arrow and apply it to my Lord's eye, it might serve as a medicine to stop the bleeding from the wound." With this surging thought, he rejoiced. He then took a sharp arrow, aimed it at his own eye, and plucked it out. As he was about to apply it to the Lord's eye, he saw the red blood still flowing from the Lord's eye. He then jumped up from the ground, beat his mountain-like shoulders, and danced with joy. "How good is this idea I had!" he exclaimed with a smile, and in his overwhelming joy, he became like one possessed. The above two verses describe what Thinnanar did, as narrated by Sekkizhar Peruman. Didn't he say "flesh for flesh" in the first verse? Therefore, to stop the blood flowing from the right eye of his Lord, Kudumithevar, and to heal the wound, it would be good to replace it with a similar eye. The blood from the Lord's eye would stop, and the Lord's affliction would be cured. Thinnanar decided this. For this, he decided to pluck out his own eye with an arrow and apply it to the Lord. He rejoiced, thinking that if he plucked out his own eye with an arrow and applied it to the Lord, it would be a medicine for the disease in the Lord's eye, and the blood from the wound would stop flowing. According to the principle of "eye for an eye" or "flesh for flesh," to cure the defect in Kudumithevar's eye, an eye was needed. But why not kill a deer or other animal, pluck out its eye, and apply it? Thinnanar thought that if he did that, it would cause further delay. Moreover, by the time Thinnanar went to the mountain, found suitable animals, and brought back their eyes, the heat of the plucked organ would have dissipated, and it would be impossible to apply it to Kudumithevar. This would cause further delay. Therefore, he thought, "We have our own eyes to give. When we, who have worthy eyes to give, are by the Lord's side, where else should we go to seek this? We don't need to seek it anywhere else." He decided this in an instant. He chose a sharp, good arrow from his quiver. He plucked out his right eye with that arrow. He stood still as he plucked out his eye. Immediately, he applied his plucked eye to the Lord's right eye. At that moment, the blood flowing from the Lord's eye stopped. Seeing this, Thinnanar was filled with great joy. He jumped and leaped high from the ground. He beat his mountain-like shoulders. He danced. "The idea that came to me is very good. A good idea occurred to me," he thought, and with a smile, he was in a state of ecstasy, like one possessed. Dancing, forgetting oneself, is a result of immense joy. "Worshipping, prostrating, and dancing are expressions of excessive joy," says Sivagnana Siddhiyar. Thinnanar embraced Lord Shiva. Thinnanar embraced Lord Shiva with love. He made Shiva's work his own work. Standing in the grace of Shiva's inspiration, he worshipped and adored Shiva, forgetting himself. Now, seeing Shiva's disease cured, he danced with joy. "He attained the supreme peace of the Jivanmukta state," is the commentary of Thiru K. Sadasiva Chettiar. Now, as

Thinnanar was in this state of joy, the next event occurred. Sekkizhar Peruman describes it in his verses: "The generous one who applied his own eye to the Lord's right eye, to further demonstrate his goodness, saw blood flowing profusely from the Lord's other eye." He was born into the hunter clan due to their great penance in this world, and his principles were superior to those of the celestials. "After seeing this, I am ruined! My Kalathi Lord's one eye has a wound with blood, and now the other eye is also bleeding profusely. But I am not afraid of this. I have found the medicine! I still have one eye left. I will pluck out that eye and apply it to cure this," he declared. As he pondered the implications of plucking out his own eye and applying it to the Lord's eye, he placed his left foot on the Lord's eye, and with a heart full of love, he took a single arrow. Thinnanar did not hesitate to pierce his own eye with it. The Lord of the red-eyed white bull, the Lord who ruled over Thinnanar, the Lord of Thirukalathi, could not bear to see this wonder. He stretched out his hand, adorned with three layers of serpent bracelets, and with an ambrosial voice, declared, "Stop, Kannappa! Stop!" These are the verses composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Thinnanar, the generous one, who had applied his own right eye to stop the blood from the Lord's right eye, now stood as a testament to his generosity. Earlier, in a verse, Sekkizhar Peruman referred to Kalathinathar as "Vallalar" (generous one). Now, in this verse, he refers to Thinnanar as "Vallalar." "Vallal" means one who bestows what is desired by those who seek, without any self-interest, out of compassion, as stated in Sivagnanabodha Sittrurai. God possesses the compassion to grant all living beings what they desire, as they desire it. Therefore, He is Vallalar. But because Kannappa Nayanar possessed a generosity similar to that of God, granting what was desired, Sekkizhar Peruman also calls him Vallalar. Other so-called generous people in the world give what they possess outwardly, desiring fame and other such things. But Kannappa Nayanar gave his eye, which is a supreme organ, without desiring anything else. Therefore, he is Vallalar. Even though Thinnanar had given his right eye, and had only one eye remaining, to prevent us from doubting whether he would give that remaining eye as well, the Lord, as the foremost, had another compassionate thought. He now showed a scene where blood flowed from his other eye, his left eye. Thinnanar, born into the hunter clan due to their great penance, and whose principles were superior to those of the celestials, saw blood flowing from the Lord's left eye. Now, Thinnanar was not afraid when he saw blood flowing from the Lord's left eye. Because Thinnanar thought, "I know the medicine for this disease, I have experienced it. And I have that medicine with me now. I still have one eye left. Therefore, I will pluck out that eye and apply it to the Lord's eye to cure this disease of the Lord." Thinnanar was superior to the celestials in his principles. All celestials worship the Lord for some benefit. But Thinnanar worshipped the Lord as the embodiment of love, without desiring anything else.

Therefore, Thinnanar is far superior to the celestials in such principles. Furthermore, doesn't it mention "Umbar Melor" here? "Kolhaiyin Umbar Melor" - superior to the celestials in principle. "Umbar" means celestials. Among all celestials, Thirumal is the foremost. Even Thirumal once offered his eye as an archana to Lord Shiva. Thirumal would daily worship Lord Shiva by reciting Shiva's thousand names and offering a thousand lotus flowers. One day, Lord Shiva made one of those thousand lotus flowers disappear. Thirumal, who had worshipped with 999 flowers and 999 names, found himself short of one flower. So, Thirumal then plucked out his lotus-like eye and offered it to Lord Shiva as a lotus flower. However, Thirumal did this to obtain his Chakra weapon. Lord Shiva also granted Thirumal the Chakra weapon. But here, Thinnanar plucked out his eye and applied it to the Lord purely out of his unadulterated love and devotion for the Lord, without any other benefit in mind. That is why Thinnanar is superior to the celestials in principle. The verse implies that he is even superior to Thirumal, who is considered the foremost among the celestials. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal here refers to Lord Shiva as "Kan Nuthal Kadavul" (the God with an eye on the forehead). Lord Shiva's right eye removes the bonds of living beings. His left eye is the eye of grace, which makes things Shiva-like. The eye on His forehead is the eye of wisdom, which bestows the experience of Sivananda upon living beings. Thinnanar now resolves to pluck out his remaining eye, his left eye, and apply it to the Lord. If he were to remove his remaining left eye, he would not know where the Lord's left eye was to apply it. Therefore, to identify that spot, he placed his foot on the Lord's left eye as a mark. Furthermore, after an eye is plucked out, it will only adhere to the other flesh, eye to eye, if it is applied immediately, before its heat dissipates and the blood cools. Therefore, as a precautionary measure, Thinnanar placed his foot on the Lord's left eye to mark it, fearing that if he searched for the Lord's left eye after plucking out his own, it would cause delay and he would be unable to apply the eye. Now, Thinnanar tried to bring his sharp arrow near his left eye. At that moment, the Lord could not bear that action. The Lord, who had endured Thinnanar plucking out his right eye and applying it, could not bear it when he tried to pluck out his left eye. This is because the Lord's right eye is on the right side of the Lord. But the Lord's left eye belongs to Ambika. It is the compassionate part of our Mother, who gave birth to us. Therefore, Ambika, who is the embodiment of the Lord's grace, could not bear Thinnanar's attempt to pluck out that left eye, which is the compassionate part of the Mother. So, immediately, the Lord extended His divine hand, grasped Thinnanar's hand, which was ready to pluck out his left eye, and said three times, "Stop, Kannappa! Stop, Kannappa! Stop, Kannappa!" The Lord caused a hand to emerge from the Sivalinga form of Kudumithevar, and with that divine hand, He held Kannappa's hand with the arrow,

and stopped him from performing that action. "Stop, Kannappa! Stop, Kannappa! Stop, Kannappa!" He said three times with an ambrosial voice. "When the Lord of the hunters plucked out his eye and applied it, and when the Lord, who loved the offering of flesh, came forward and held him, the wise sages saw it. The celestials, led by Brahma, showered a rain of fragrant flowers. The Vedas roared." This is the next verse. Thus, Sivakochariyar, who was hidden there, saw the Lord's divine hand holding Thinnanar's hands. This great sage, Sivakochariyar, had received the wisdom to see and understand this event through the grace of the Lord. Therefore, he became a wise sage. Sivakochariyar witnessed the overflowing love of Kannappa Nayanar. Through Sivakochariyar, the Lord also granted us, other souls, the grace to witness the overflowing love and prowess of Kannappa Nayanar. From the heavens, the celestials, led by Brahma, showered a rain of Kalpaka flowers. The celestials praised Him. All the Vedas praised Him. Now, Lord Shiva bestowed His great and enduring grace upon Kannappa Nayanar, saying, "O one with unparalleled love, O one without equal, always stand by my right side!" Is there any greater blessing than this? Is there any other blessing than this? asks Sekkizhar Peruman. This refers to the highest state of Shiva Sayujya. Since Kannappa Nayanar, through his gracious wisdom, became free from the three impurities, became one with the Lord in that path, and performed the Lord's service, what he attained was Paramukti (ultimate liberation). It should not be understood as Samipya Padam (proximity to God) just because the Lord said, "Always stand by my right side." Because Sekkizhar Peruman himself asks from his divine words, "Is there any greater blessing than this, the highest blessing attained by souls?" Doesn't he ask, "Is there any greater blessing than this? Is there anything beyond this?" This refers to the state of Mukti, which is Shiva Sayujya. "What is the use of learning if one does not worship the good feet of the Pure One?" asks Thiruvalluva Deva Nayanar, posing a question. The greatest benefit of education is to worship the feet of the Lord. Is there any other greater benefit from education than worshipping the feet of the Lord? Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman asks here, "Is there any other benefit to be gained beyond this?" and answers, "No." The highest blessing of Shiva Sayujya, attained by Thinnappan, who rose to become Kannappa Nayanar, is the maximum blessing that all souls can attain. He is Kannappar, the Nambi of abundant glory. Therefore, the meaning of Thirukkural, "He attained the ultimate benefit of learning," is also present here. The idea is that Kannappar received a greater blessing than anyone else. It is for this reason that Kannappa Nayanar has been praised by Paramacharya Murthigal like Thiruvathavur Adigal and all other great people. The Lord grants Kannappa Nayanar, "Stand by my right side." The word "valam" (right) also means superiority and victory. Therefore, the greatest superiority and victory is to become Shiva through love. To become Shiva through love is Shiva's right. So, the implication here is also that the Lord said, "You will stand on my right side." Its meaning is, "You, who are the embodiment of love, stand in non-duality."

My note here is that this is the Shuddha Advaita Siddhanta Mukti described by Saiva Siddhanta. It is because Kannappa Nayanar attained this blessing, which is difficult for anyone else to obtain, that all Saiva Acharyas have praised and adored Kannappa Nayanar. It is here that Thirugnanasambandha Peruman sings with melting devotion, praising Kannappa Nayanar, "Did Kannappa Nayanar walk with his sandals soaked?" Thirunavukkarasu Peruman praises Kannappa Nayanar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, "I am a servant to Kannappar, the Nambi of abundant glory." Manikkavasagar Peruman sings in Thirukkothumbi in Thiruvachagam, "After seeing that there is no love equal to Kannappan's, my Father, who is incomparable, took me into His grace and commanded me to come, O bee, go and hum to the Lord with the white ash, who is the embodiment of compassion." Nakkirar Deva Nayanar and other Sangam poets also sing about Kannappa Nayanar. The separate work "Thirukkannappa Devar Thirumaram," composed by Kallada Deva Nayanar and Nakkirar Deva Nayanar, is found in the 11th Thirumurai. Not only in Tamil, but also in Sanskrit, the greatness of Kannappa Devar Nayanar has been sung by Adi Shankara in the 63rd verse of his Sivananda Lahari. Adi Shankara praises Kannappa Nayanar. The sandals worn by Kannappa Nayanar, soaked in his path, become as sacred as the darbha grass placed on the head before the abhishekam of Paramashivamurthy. Being moistened by the water gargled from Kannappa Devar Nayanar's mouth becomes like the divine abhishekam that burned down the three cities. The leftover portion of the meat that Kannappa Devar Nayanar tasted a little becomes like a fresh neivedyam. What a wonder! A hunter living in the forest becomes a great devotee. Is this not what "devotion can do anything" means? Thus, Adi Shankara praises and adores Kannappa Devar Nayanar. Similarly, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman's Thevaram also expresses this sentiment. Thirugnanasambandha Peruman praises in Thevaram, "The hunter who worships with his mouth as a kalasam, whose eye is a flower, who plucks it out with an arrow and joins the feet of the Lord, is the mountain of Kalathi." Following Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's praise, "I am a servant to Kannappar, the Nambi of abundant glory," Nambiyandar Nambigal praises him in Thiruthondar Thiruvandhadhi. Sekkizhar Peruman composes and sings the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar in 181 verses in Periya Puranam. Thus, Sekkizhar sings the Puranam of Kannappa Devar Nayanar. Sekkizhar Peruman concludes the sacred story of Kannappa Devar Nayanar by stating, "Wearing on my head the sacred feet of Kannappa Nayanar, the chief who had the blessing of replacing the red blood flowing from the wound in the eyes of the Lord residing in Thirukalathi with his own eye, I shall now begin to narrate the sacred service of Kalayanar Nayanar in Thirukkadavur, where Lord Shiva resides." We too shall continue to contemplate the Puranam of Thirukkadavur Kalayanar Nayanar, also known as Kungiliya Kalayanar. I am a servant to Kannappar, the Nambi of abundant glory. Praise to the sacred feet of Kannappa Devar Nayanar. Praise to the sacred feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Nambiyandar Nambigal. Praise to the sacred feet of Sekkizhar Peruman. Praise to the sacred feet of Sivakavimani Ayya, who wrote the commentary on Periya Puranam. Thiruchitrambalam.
